![img-18.jpeg](img-18.jpeg)

Figure 2.6 Use of a Thoracic Elevation Device (TED).

In addition, the cervical spine has to be appropriately immobilised all the time (unless the spine can be cleared), either with a cervical collar or by manual immobilisation. Younger children have a prominent occiput; hence the neck is prone to hyperflexion. To mitigate this, one can use a Thoracic Elevation Device (TED) or a properly folded blanket. This is usually necessary for children who are younger than 8 years old or weigh less than 25 kg. As demonstrated in Figure 2.6, the spine is well-aligned with the use of the TED.

## Breathing

Breathing and ventilation have to be timely assessed as children have greater oxygen consumption, smaller functional residual volume and increased chest wall compliance, predisposing to airway collapse and hypoxia. Furthermore, the diaphragm is easily fatigued and displaced. Urgent intervention is needed to treat tension pneumothorax, open pneumothorax, haemothorax or flail chest.

During the examination, one should look for:

1. Signs of respiratory distress
2. Symmetrical chest movements and equal air entry on both sides of the chest.

Asymmetry would suggest splinting, flail chest, pneumothorax or haemothorax

## Circulation

Intravenous access has to be established. If one encounters difficulty with peripheral intravenous access, an intraosseous (IO) needle can be inserted for vascular access. At this point, blood specimens are also taken and dispatched.

The patient is assessed for signs of poor perfusion and shock. Haemorrhagic shock is the most common cause of shock in major trauma. In the presence of shock, one should also look for the likely source of bleeding (thorax, abdomen, pelvis and long bones). The

b4854_Sec-02.indd 54

12-Dec-22 10:53:40 AM

# Major Trauma

![img-19.jpeg](img-19.jpeg)

COAGULOPATHY / ACIDOSIS / HYPOTHERMIA - A VICIOUS CYCLE

Figure 2.7 Lethal triad of trauma.

management of a child in shock involves fluid resuscitation (commonly crystalloids, usually while awaiting the arrival of blood products) and transfusion with blood products. Patients receive platelets as well as frozen plasma, in addition to packed red cells. This helps to reduce the risk of worsening coagulopathy, which is one of the components of the lethal triad in major trauma.

To ensure blood products are received in a timely manner, one can activate the Massive Transfusion Protocol (MTP) as per your institution's practice.

In addition, intravenous tranexamic acid can be given to the patient to help with haemostasis.

There are also other causes of shock in paediatric trauma patients, which include cardiogenic shock, obstructive shock, neurogenic shock as well septic shock.

## Disability

The assessment of disability comprises the following:

1. Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)
2. Pupillary size and reactivity
3. Full neurological examination, looking for any lateralising sign.

Traumatic brain injury (TBI) is a major contributor of mortality and morbidity in children. The primary goal of management of such patients is the prevention of secondary injury, control of raised intracranial pressure (ICP), maintenance of cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP) and identification of mass lesions needing emergency surgical evacuation.

TBI is classified as mild (GCS 14–15), moderate (GCS 9–13) and severe (GCS ≤8). Indications for computed tomographic (CT) scan includes:

1. GCS \(\leq 13\)
2. Fluctuating GCS with more than 2 points
3. GCS 14 or 15 plus

(i) Signs of base of skull fracture: racoon eyes, Battle sign, cerebral spinal fluid (CSF) otorrhea/rhinorrhoea
(ii) Depressed or compound skull fracture
(iii) Focal neurological signs or signs of raised intracranial pressure
(iv) Polytrauma with signs of head injury
(v) Penetrating skull injury
(vi) Bleeding diathesis (e.g. haemophilia) or history of intracranial haemorrhage

b4854_Sec-02.indd 55

12-Dec-22 10:53:41 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

For patients with signs of raised intracranial pressure, hyperosmolar therapy (3% hypertonic saline or mannitol) is indicated. As mentioned previously, secondary insults such as hypotension, hypoxia and hypoglycaemia are avoided.

## Exposure

Full exposure of the patient is prudent in order to perform a complete examination and not miss out any injury. However, children are prone to hypothermia in view of their body surface area. Hypothermia is part of the lethal triad. Therefore, it is necessary to take steps to reduce the risk of the hypothermia. Resuscitation rooms are often equipped with overhead radiant warmers, warming equipment (e.g. the Bair Hugger) as well as fluid warmers.

## Adjuncts

1. Monitoring devices, which include electrocardiogram (ECG) leads, blood pressure monitor, pulse oximetry and temperature probe.
2. Orogastric tube to compress the stomach.
3. Urinary catheter to monitor the urine output, which reflects the haemodynamic status of the body.
4. Radiographs (X-rays): Cervical spine (omitted if CT brain/cervical spine planned for), chest, pelvis, others as indicated by the injuries sustained.

## Secondary Survey

The secondary survey comprises history taking (SAMPLE) (see Table 2.8) and a full head-to-toe examination. The main aim of the secondary survey is to document the rest of the injuries that are not already noted in the primary survey.

## Head and neck

The scalp, skull, facial bones, eyes and neck are examined. Clues to a possible neck injury include cervical spine tenderness, subcutaneous emphysema, tracheal deviation or evidence of laryngeal fractures. The presence of a skull fracture signifies great force and increases the likelihood of intracranial injury.

## Chest

Paediatric chest injuries are usually blunt injuries. The compliant chest wall increases the incidence of pulmonary contusion and haemorrhage without overlying rib fractures. However, when rib fractures occur, this signifies a severe injuring force. The list of possible chest injuries is shown in Figure 2.8.

## Abdomen

Most paediatric abdominal injuries are blunt injuries. The hypotensive child with penetrating abdominal injuries requires prompt operative exploration. When a child with major trauma presents with signs of hypovolaemic shock, in the absence of other obvious source of bleeding, occult intra-abdominal bleeding has to be considered.

Focused abdominal sonography in trauma (FAST) is a useful modality that can be utilised to detect the presence of haemoperitoneum in such patients. Most solid-organ injuries may be treated conservatively, provided the child remains stable.

![img-20.jpeg](img-20.jpeg)

Figure 2.8 Possible chest injuries.

## Pelvis/perineum

The pelvic brim should be palpated carefully, looking for the presence of haematomas, tenderness or deformities. The stability of the pelvis is tested by pelvic compression. The flank, scrotum and perineum should be examined for haematomas, lacerations or urethral bleeding. The presence of pelvic fractures indicates significant force, and one should exclude concomitant intra-abdominal or retroperitoneal injury.

## Musculoskeletal

Limb swelling, open wounds and limb deformities should be looked for and documented. Peripheral pulses should be carefully assessed, particularly distal to an obvious fracture or dislocation. The possibility of compartment syndrome should be kept in mind. Any limb deformity should be splinted. For an open fracture or a large, deep or heavily contaminated wound, intravenous antibiotics should be administered. The tetanus vaccine status of the patient should also be ascertained. A booster should be given if needed.

## Spine

Spinal cord injuries are rare in children. The cartilaginous vertebral bodies are less likely to fracture and the elastic ligaments and horizontal facet joints contribute to increased

b4854_Sec-02.indd 57

12-Dec-22 10:53:42 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

spinal mobility. This tends to protect the spine by dispersing energy over several segments. There are several unique features of the paediatric spinal column:

- Relatively larger head size, resulting in greater flexion and extension injuries
- Ligamentous injuries are more common than fractures
- Increased flexibility of interspinous ligaments
- Infantile bony column can lengthen significantly without rupture
- Facet joints are flatter with a more horizontal orientation
- Incomplete ossification makes interpretation of bony alignment difficult
- Basilar odontoid synchondrosis fuse at 3-7 years old
- Apical odontoid epiphysis fuse at 5-7 years old
Posterior arch of C1 fuses at 4 years old
Anterior arch fuses at 7-10 years old
- Epiphyses of spinous process tips may mimic fractures
- Increased pre-odontoid space up to \(4 - 5\mathrm{mm}\) (3 mm in adults)
- Pseudo subluxation of C2 on C3 can be seen in \(40\%\) of children
- Prevertebral space size may change because of variations with respiration

A log roll is usually performed to complete the examination of the spine. During a log roll, the spine is inspected for any bruise and palpated for tenderness or step deformity. However, the process of a log roll can potentially de-stabilise pelvic fractures and dislodge a formed clot. Furthermore, in an intubated patient who has received medications for paralysis and sedation, the examination of the spine can no longer be accurately performed. Therefore, in our institution, we practise a limited 20° tilt to look for any open wound.

Regular re-assessments of the vital signs should be done. In the event of any deterioration, a repeat of the primary survey should be performed.

## Adjuncts

After the secondary survey is completed, appropriate adjuncts such as CT scans can be arranged for and performed.

## Transfer

After the relevant investigations have been completed and the haemodynamic status of the patient is stable, the patient is ready for transfer. Prior to transfer, all documentation should be completed and the details should be handed over to the next physician/team managing the patient. A good way to summarise would be using the acronym 'MIST'.

|  M | Mechanism of injury  |
| --- | --- |
|  I | Injuries sustained  |
|  S | Signs (vital signs)  |
|  T | Treatment  |

# CHAPTER 7

# Urinary Catheterisation

Chow Wen Hann; Chang Su Ying, Serena; Chan Meng Fai, Joel

## Indications

- Investigations (e.g. in-out catheterisation for suspected urinary tract infection)
- Acute urinary retention or bladder/ureter obstruction (e.g. transient myelitis, spinal injury, tumour invasion)
Monitoring of urine output
Urinary incontinence (e.g. in neurogenic bladder)
- Prolonged immobilisation (e.g. lumbar fractures) or to aid the healing of open perineal wounds
- Perioperative use for selected surgeries or on regional anaesthesia (e.g. epidural)

## Contraindications

- Known urethral trauma
- Acute pelvic fracture

Note: Consult the paediatric surgical team before placing a catheter in such patients or those with recent genitourinary surgery or known genitourinary abnormality, e.g. hypospadias

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian, and where appropriate, the child:

- Procedural steps and analgesia plans
- Verbal consent
- Possible complications, such as pain, haematuria from traumatic insertion, catheter blockage and inability to remove catheter

## Equipment Preparation

Prior to the procedure, prepare the following:

Urinary catheterisation set (alternative: sterile dressing set)
Antiseptic cleansing solution (e.g. chlorhexidine \(0.05\%\) solution)
1 vial of sterile water for injection

The volume of water required to inflate the balloon as stated on the catheter

Choose the most appropriate type and size of urinary catheter for the patient (Table 3.2).

Note: Table 3.2 serves as a reference guide only. It may vary depending upon the patient's body habitus.

## Procedural Steps

1. Position the child with the hips flexed and externally rotated.
2. Wash your hands with antiseptic soap and water, put on sterile gloves and clean the perineum with chlorhexidine.
3. Remove gloves and perform hand hygiene. Don a new pair of sterile gloves and set up the sterile drapes.
4. Using lignocaine gel, lubricate the tip of the catheter/feeding tube.

|  Female Child | Male Child  |
| --- | --- |
|  1) Place a small amount of lignocaine gel onto a sterile cotton ball 2) Place the cotton ball onto the urethral opening 3) Wait 3 to 5 min for an optimal anaesthetic effect 4) Remove the cotton ball and separate the labia majora to expose the urethral opening | 1) Lift the penis gently to a 60–90 degree angle 2) Slowly instil lignocaine gel into the urethral opening while holding the penis firmly 3) Remove syringe tip, keep the urethra closed and wait 3 to 5 min for an optimal anaesthetic effect 4) Hold the penis perpendicular to the body and retract the foreskin  |

5) Pass the lubricated catheter gently into the urethra, 1 cm at a time, until the urine starts to flow freely or until the estimated length needed is reached.

Note: Do not advance the catheter significantly beyond the recommended lengths as this may cause coiling and knotting of the catheter within the bladder, resulting in an inability to remove it.

# Urinary Catheterisation

|  In-Out Catheterisation Only | Urinary Catheterisation  |
| --- | --- |
|  6) Collect the urine for urine microscopy and culture. 7) Withdraw the catheter after the urine is collected. 8) For an uncircumcised male child, pull the foreskin back to cover the glans penis to reduce the risk of paraphimosis. | 6) Advance the catheter a few centimetres further. 7) Inflate the balloon with sterile water (required volume is shown on the catheter — do not over inflate). Document the volume of water injected into the balloon. 8) Pull the catheter until resistance (from balloon) is felt. 9) Connect catheter to urine bag. 10) For an uncircumcised male child, pull the foreskin back to cover the glans penis to reduce the risk of paraphimosis. 11) Secure the catheter to the body with adhesive tape.  |

## Troubleshooting Points

1. If there is no drainage of urine upon insertion of the catheter,

a. Consider the possibility of urethral false passage, especially if it was a traumatic catheterisation. Remove the catheter immediately and inform the senior doctor. A paediatric surgical consult may be required.
b. The child may be dehydrated or may have just voided prior to catheterisation. You may need to wait a short while for urine to flow.

**IMPORTANT: Do not inflate the balloon when there is no urine flow.**

2. If there is pain upon inflation of the balloon, it is likely that the catheter is not in the bladder. Immediately deflate the balloon and insert the catheter further into the bladder.
3. If there is difficulty in removal of the catheter (i.e. concerns of stuck catheter), do not manipulate further but seek help from the paediatric surgical team.

## Post Procedure

1. Ensure a closed urinary drainage system at all times to minimise infection.
2. Maintain an unobstructed downhill flow (without kinks) and keep urine drainage bag below urinary bladder to prevent reflux of urine. Ensure regular emptying of urine from urinary bag.
3. Monitor for complications and troubleshoot accordingly (see Table 3.3).

## Removal of Urinary Catheter

1. Empty the urinary bag.
2. Prepare equipment (one \(10\mathrm{ml}\) syringe, one pair of non-sterile gloves, one protective sheet).
3. Position patient in dorsal recumbent position, with knees flexed and thighs apart, and place protective sheet under the patient's buttocks.
4. Perform hand hygiene and don the non-sterile gloves.

Table 3.3 Procedural Complications of Urinary Catheterisation

|  Complication | Monitoring | Intervention  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Catheter Associated Urinary Tract Infection (CAUTI)* | Fever Cloudy urine | Remove indwelling catheter. Appropriate antibiotic therapy.  |
|  Bladder spasm and pain *Caused by inflammation of bladder and urethral mucosa* | Symptoms of urgency, perineal pain May be associated with irregular bladder emptying and constipation | Anticholinergic medications (oral, transdermal or intravesical). Use catheter with smaller lumen and balloon size. Prevent constipation.  |
|  Haematuria *Caused by traumatic insertion* | Significant pain, obstructive features, worsening haematuria | Consult the paediatric surgical team if these symptoms are present. Consider working up for coagulopathy if haematuria persists.  |
|  Catheter blockage *Caused by debris, encrustation, catheter kinking, catheter drainage holes blocked by the adjacent bladder wall* | Urinary leakage around catheter | Ensure adequate fluid intake. Perform bladder irrigation under sterile technique to remove encrustation/blood clots. Consider working up for bladder stones if there is recurrent catheter blockage.  |
|  Inability to remove catheter *Caused by faulty balloon deflation mechanism or balloon encrustation (especially if saline used to inflate balloon)* |  | Consult the paediatric surgical team immediately. Possible treatment options: puncture balloon inflation pathway to release water content, ultrasound-guided transabdominal balloon puncture or perforate balloon via cystoscopy.  |

*Definition of CAUTI: Presence of symptoms or signs consistent with UTI, with no other identified source of infection, and urine culture with no more than two species of organisms identified, at least one of which is a bacterium of ≥10^5 CFU/ml. It is considered catheter-associated if this occurs ≥2 days from placement of urinary catheter or within 48 hours of removal.

5. Attach the syringe to the urinary catheter balloon port and aspirate to deflate the balloon. Ensure that the same volume of water for injection that has been used to inflate the balloon is aspirated fully.
6. Withdraw the urinary catheter gently. If there is any difficulty withdrawing it, try rotating the catheter as you withdraw or inserting the catheter slightly with some lubricant before withdrawing it again.
7. If there is further resistance and inability to withdraw the urinary catheter, do not manipulate it further. Consult the paediatric surgical team.

# CHAPTER 8

# Lumbar Puncture

Chow Wen Hann; Chang Su Ying, Serena; Chan Meng Fai, Joel

## Indications

- Suspected meningitis or encephalitis
- Intrathecal medication
- Diagnosis or monitoring of conditions including inborn errors of metabolism, neurogenetic conditions and idiopathic intracranial hypertension

## Contraindications

- Signs suggestive of raised intracranial pressure (ICP), mass effect, focality (e.g. focal neurological deficits, dropping Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), hypertension with bradycardia), coma or encephalopathy (GCS <8, rapidly dropping or fluctuating GCS, absent brainstem reflexes)

Neuroimaging after stabilisation
Do not attempt lumbar puncture (LP) regardless of imaging

- Signs of general raised ICP (e.g. papilloedema) but otherwise stable

Neuroimaging
LP may be attempted in consultation with a senior doctor and/or neurologist

Significant respiratory distress or haemodynamic instability/shock
- Bleeding tendency — platelet count \(< 50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\), coagulopathy and/or anticoagulation/antiplatelet medication
Congenital spine abnormalities (e.g. tethered cord)
- Local infection at LP site

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian, and where appropriate, the child:

- Procedural steps, analgesia and/or sedation plans
- Written consent
- The LP can be complicated, difficult and may require several attempts, especially in young infants. In some instances, LP under ultrasound guidance may be required.
- Possible complications such as headache, backache, bleeding/haematoma, infection, small risk of injury to surrounding structures at puncture site (blood vessels, nerves), and very rarely brain herniation.

# Lumbar Puncture

Vertical line between most superior point of both iliac crests

![img-21.jpeg](img-21.jpeg)

Figure 3.1 Site of insertion when performing lumbar puncture (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/Dr Stella Zhang).

## Site Selection

A lumbar puncture can be safely performed at the L3/4 or L4/L5 interspinal space. The 4th lumbar vertebral body is identified by visualising a vertical line between the most superior point of both iliac crests and its transection with the sagittal midline of the spine. (see Figure 3.1)

## Equipment Preparation

Prior to the procedure, prepare the following:

- Sterile dressing set and antiseptic swab sticks (e.g. 3M SoluPrep Antiseptic Swab — \(2\%\) w/v Chlorhexidine Gluconate and \(70\%\) v/v Isopropyl Alcohol)
- Sterile bottles for collection of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF)
- Spinal needles (see Table 3.4)
- Manometer for patient \(>18\) months old, except when administering intrathecal chemotherapy
Glucometer
- Sterile gloves
- Local anaesthetic cream and sedation, if required

## Sedation and Analgesia

Lumbar puncture should be performed under moderate sedation (e.g. IV ketamine or midazolam) for children greater than 6 months old with normal conscious state. Provide adequate analgesia with local anaesthetic cream (e.g. EMLA cream), applied with occlusive dressing over LP site half an hour prior to procedure and/or intradermal lignocaine. In newborns and young infants, oral glucose can be offered as pain relief.

# The Baby Bear Book

Table 3.4 Choice of Spinal Needles

|  Age | Median Spinal Cord Depth | Spinal Needle  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Neonates to infants up to 7 kg | 11–21 mm | Neonatal Spinal Needle: 25 G/Length 25 mm  |
|  Infants >7 kg to young children up to 25 kg | 21–30 mm | Paediatric Spinal Needle: 22 G/Length 38 mm  |
|  Older children >25 kg | >30 mm | Paediatric Spinal Needle: 22 G/Length 90 mm  |

Children \((mm) = 0.4\) x Weight \((kg) + 20\)

## Pre-Procedural Steps

1. Neuroimaging (CT/MRI) if indicated (see contraindications).
2. Fundoscopy is necessary for children \(>18\) months old before doing LP to look for any papilloedema (see above).
3. Ensure written consent has been taken.
4. Identify the correct patient (with at least two patient identifiers) and confirm the laboratory tests and/or intrathecal chemotherapy required.
5. Conduct a time out to ensure correct patient and procedure.
6. Ensure all equipment and sterile collection bottles are prepared (refer to checklist above).

## Procedural Steps

1. Place the child in a lateral recumbent position with the neck, body, hips and knees flexed. Avoid over-flexing the neck in infants as it may cause airway obstruction and respiratory compromise.
Alternatively, LP can be performed in an older child slouched in a sitting position.
2. Identify appropriate insertion site at L3/L4 or L4/L5 interspinal space (Figure 3.1).
3. Ensure adequate sedation
4. Wash hands with antiseptic soap and water, put on sterile gloves, clean the area with standard aseptic technique using antiseptic swab sticks and set up sterile drapes.
5. Gently puncture skin with a spinal needle at the identified mark, pointing towards the umbilicus.
6. Gently advance a few millimetres at a time until a 'give' is felt on entering the dura mater. Remove the stylet and CSF should start to drip out.
7. In a child \(>18\) months old (except when LP is done to administer intrathecal chemotherapy), measure the opening pressure by attaching a sterile manometer to the end of the spinal needle (Figure 3.2a). Thereafter, the CSF can be collected by emptying the manometer into the specimen bottle (Figure 3.2b).

# Lumbar Puncture

![img-22.jpeg](img-22.jpeg)

(a)

Figure 3.2 Using a manometer during lumbar puncture (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/Dr Stella Zhang). (a) Reading opening pressure, (b) When collecting CSF from manometer tube, (c) Collecting additional CSF specimen

8. Collect the rest of CSF specimen in the specimen bottles by turning the 3-way tap of the manometer (Figure 3.2c).

a. If the CSF is bloodstained and fails to clear, reinsert the stylet and withdraw the spinal needle. Inform the senior doctor in charge for consideration of more attempts.
b. If the CSF is turbid, give IV dexamethasone \(0.2\mathrm{mg / kg}\) before or with the first dose of IV antibiotics (this has been proven to reduce severe hearing loss in bacterial meningitis, especially in Haemophilus Influenzae meningitis).
c. Where indicated, give intrathecal chemotherapy under sterile technique. Ensure aspiration of CSF into syringe before giving intrathecal medication.

9. After collecting all CSF samples, re-insert the stylet and gently withdraw spinal needle. Immediately apply pressure on the puncture site with a sterile gauze and cover it with an adhesive film.

10. Take blood glucose with glucometer.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Troubleshooting Points

1. In the event of raised opening pressure (excluding LP done for idiopathic intracranial hypertension or other conditions where LP is done as therapeutic measure):

a. Note the final opening pressure.
b. Drain the manometer and send off this CSF specimen for relevant investigations.
c. Additional CSF fluid can be collected, if required, but excessive CSF collection should be avoided as this may contribute to post-dural puncture headache.
d. Discuss with the senior medical staff if in doubt. Assess and monitor the patient's vital signs, neurological and respiratory systems closely.
e. If the patient is unwell or deteriorates, consider CT brain and transfer the patient to a higher acuity care area (e.g. high dependency unit or intensive care unit) and manage as for raised ICP.

Note: Normal reference range of opening pressure ranges between 11 and 28 cmH₂O. However, this can be influenced by multiple variables, including age, BMI, depth of sedation and medications. Hence, it should be interpreted in conjunction with the child's clinical signs and/or neuroimaging findings.

2. Bony Resistance (may be due to puncture over spinous process)

a. Attempt to overcome bony resistance by inserting the stylet and withdraw needle to subcutaneous tissue, confirming that the spine is not rotated, and the puncture site is at midline, reposition child to ensure adequate flexion to open up interlaminar space and gently advance needle more cephalad.
b. Avoid repeated manipulations as it may predispose to traumatic tap.

3. Poor CSF flow can be improved by:

a. Rotating spinal needle \(90^{\circ}\)
b. Reinserting stylet and advance needle slightly.
c. Attempting the procedure at a different site (new needle should be used each time).

## Post-Procedure Monitoring and Care

1. Keep the patient supine for 1 hour, after which some movement is allowed, e.g. sitting up in bed to eat a meal or being carried by the parent for feeding. The current evidence shows that a prolonged supine position does not decrease the incidence of post-dural puncture headache.
2. Monitor with hourly parameters for the next 6 hours, especially if sedation was given.
3. In cases of a traumatic tap, nursing monitoring should include looking out for changes in the motor and sensory function of the lower extremities and bladder and bowel dysfunction. The frequency of monitoring should be discussed with a senior doctor.

# Lumbar Puncture

Table 3.5 Procedural Complications of Lumbar Puncture

|  Complication | Monitoring | Intervention  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Headache (Most common) | Headache occurring hours to a day after LP | Adequate bed rest and hydration. Analgesia  |
|  Mild backache (especially after multiple LP attempts) | Backache within 1 to 2 d after LP | Analgesia  |
|  Bleeding (usually minimal, unless coagulopathy present) | Bleeding at the puncture site. Haematoma: - at skin, resulting in pain (usually resolves in 1 to 2 wk) - at spinal space, resulting in alterations in lower limb neurological function or bladder/bowel dysfunction | Lying flat after procedure reduces bleeding risk. Urgent neurological assessment and neuroimaging where there are concerns of spinal haematoma.  |
|  Infection (rare) — puncture site cellulitis, vertebral osteomyelitis, discitis, epidural abscess, bacterial meningitis | Puncture site for erythema and tenderness Neurological deficits Persistent fever | Appropriate antibiotic therapy  |
|  Transient dysaesthesia | Abnormal lower limb sensation reported by patient. May occur during the procedure if the needle makes contact with cauda equina nerve roots. | Symptoms should resolve immediately with needle repositioning. Permanent nerve damage is rare.  |
|  Cerebral Herniation - occurs in children with raised ICP and an abnormal ICP gradient (e.g. space occupying lesion) - children with uniform increased ICP (e.g. idiopathic intracranial hypertension) may safely undergo LP | Decerebrate posturing, decreasing responsiveness, dilated pupils and vomiting | Urgent neurological assessment and neuroimaging  |

# CHAPTER 9

# Central Venous Line Insertion

Cher Yuqin; Ng Si Min, Pamela; Lim Kian Boon, Joel

## Indications

- Vascular access to administer inotropes, high osmolality or caustic medications
- Vascular access to perform haemodialysis or plasmapheresis
Monitoring of central venous pressure

## Contra-Indications

- Infection of placement site
Distortion of anatomy
- Bleeding tendencies — coagulopathy or platelet count \(< 50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L} \rightarrow\) consider performing procedure under fresh frozen plasma or platelet cover
Thrombosed veins
Prior injury to the vein
Uncooperative patient
- For femoral venous catheters, an additional contra-indication includes suspected disruption of the inferior vena cava secondary to abdominal trauma

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian, and where appropriate, the child:

Procedural steps and analgesia plan
- Written consent
- Possible complications, such as pain, bleeding, infection (including blood stream infection), injury to surrounding structures, thrombosis, catheter obstruction/ occlusion requiring line removal or replacement and rarely catheter breakage or wire retention

## Site Selection

The *femoral vein* that lies within the femoral triangle in the inguinal-femoral region is a common site used for central venous line placement (Figure 3.3). The relationship of the important structures within the femoral triangle is easily remembered by the acronym VAN:

(Medial) Vein $\rightarrow$ Artery $\rightarrow$ Nerve (Lateral)

75

# The Baby Bear Book

![img-25.jpeg](img-25.jpeg)

Figure 3.3 Borders and contents of the femoral triangle (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/ Dr Stella Zhang).

The internal jugular vein (IJV) is another possible cannulation site — however, this carries additional risks, such as pneumothorax or injury to the carotid artery or thoracic duct. This should be done only with senior staff supervision (*refer to CICU Handbook on further details on internal jugular vein cannulation*).

## Equipment Preparation

- Central venous catheter set — Refer to CICU Handbook on the type and size of catheter and guide on depth of insertion
- Sterile procedure set
- Chlorhexidine solution
- Two additional disposable sterile drapes
- Blade (if not provided in the set)
- Silk 3-0 with curved needle
- Gallipot for heparin saline
Additional \(3\mathrm{ml} / 5\mathrm{ml}\) syringes
- Clear adhesive dressing (e.g. Tegaderm)
- Sterile ultrasound sheath with sterile conducting gel
Heparin saline
- Drawing needle
- Lignocaine \(1\%\) solution

## Sedation and Analgesia

Central Venous Line (CVL) insertion should be done under moderate sedation (e.g. IV ketamine). Provide adequate analgesia. Local anaesthesia can be given with subdermal administration of 1% lignocaine (0.3 ml/kg, up to 10 ml) to the insertion site.

b4854_Sec-03.indd 76

12-Dec-22 10:54:39 AM

## Pre-Procedural Steps

1. Ensure written consent has been taken.
2. Conduct a time out to ensure the correct patient, procedure and site.
3. Positioning of patient: For femoral venous cannulation, lie the patient in supine position, with the hip abducted and slightly externally rotated. Use a hip roll if necessary.
4. Examine the patient: Palpate the femoral pulse or use ultrasound to visualise the vascular anatomy.
5. Ensure that the equipment is prepared (refer to the check list above).
6. Gown up with the shower cap, face mask, sterile gown and sterile gloves.
7. Flush and prime all catheter lumens with heparin saline.

## Procedural Steps

1. Clean and drape the patient — ensure that the umbilicus can still be visualised.
2. If using ultrasonography, place the ultrasound probe in the sterile sheath.
3. Reassess the insertion site via ultrasonography (preferably) or via surface marking.
4. Ensure adequate sedation and analgesia.
5. Puncture the skin using a needle/cannula, connected to the syringe with heparin saline. The angle of approach should be 30 to 45°, directed towards the umbilicus.
6. Once the vein is punctured, blood should be easily aspirated into the syringe. The blood return should be dark red and non-pulsatile in a venous cannulation. This can be further confirmed by performing a blood gas to assess partial pressure of oxygen, if necessary.

- If a needle was used, fix the depth and angle of the needle, while gently rotating the syringe to disconnect it from the needle.

TIP: Do this gently to prevent displacement of the needle.

- If a cannula was used, fix the needle in place and advance the cannula.

7. Advance the guidewire into the needle/cannula.

TIP: This process should be smooth, with little to no resistance. Avoid forcing the guidewire in if resistance is encountered.

TIP: Avoid using the 'rear' end of the guidewire, as it may be sharper than the 'front' end and can potentially damage the vessel.

8. Carefully remove the needle/cannula while holding the guidewire in place

TIP: Hold on to the guidewire at its entry point to the skin to prevent it from slipping out.

9. Using a blade, with the sharp edge facing away from the guidewire, make a small incision in the skin approximately 2 to 3 mm at the puncture site, to facilitate passage of the dilator.

10. Pass the dilator over the guidewire and advance it into the vein using a rotating movement, up to a similar depth where backflow was first obtained.

TIP: Hold the dilator at its midpoint to prevent it from bending and kinking the guidewire.

11. Carefully remove the dilator while securing the guidewire.

TIP: Anticipate bleeding and use a gauze to apply pressure to the puncture site to minimise bleeding.

b4854_Sec-03.indd 77

12-Dec-22 10:54:39 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

![img-26.jpeg](img-26.jpeg)

Figure 3.4 Illustration of how to secure a central venous line (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/Dr Stella Zhang).

12. Unlock the distal lumen of the catheter. Pass the catheter over the guidewire.
13. When the catheter tip is about 2 to \(3\mathrm{cm}\) from the skin, carefully withdraw the guidewire until its 'rear' tip has emerged from the distal lumen of the catheter. While holding the guidewire, advance the catheter into the vein and remove the guidewire. TIP: Do this slowly; avoid rushing through this step and doing both withdrawal of guidewire and advancing of catheter simultaneously. TIP: In general, insert till a depth where the catheter tip should be sited below the liver and diaphragm. For measurements on depth of insertion, including IJV insertion, refer to CICU Handbook
14. Check for backflow for each lumen and flush each lumen with heparin saline.
15. Secure the catheter by applying three sutures. (Figure 3.4)
16. Clean and dry the site before applying Tegaderm.
17. Dispose of all sharps safely. Account for all used sharps and guidewires with a witness before disposing.
18. Maintain sterility of the procedure trolley, as the nurses will use the set to connect infusions to the catheter.
19. Order an abdominal X-ray to confirm placement of the central venous catheter.

## Post Procedural Monitoring and Care

- Monitor for bleeding/swelling/leaking of infusions/extravasation/signs of infection.
- Maintain sterility when accessing or breaking the line.
- Review the need for the CVL daily — avoid keeping the CVL in situ for more than 7–10 days.

## Video Resources

1. Video link — Ultrasound-guided placement of femoral venous catheter https://www.nejm.org/doi/full/10.1056/NEJMvcm0801006

# Central Venous Line Insertion

Table 3.6 Procedural Complications of Central Venous Line Insertion

|  Complication | Monitoring | Intervention  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Infection (local infection or bacteraemia) | Erythema, swelling, pain, fever, bacteraemia | Remove CVL. Consider line culture and tip culture after line removal. Appropriate antibiotic therapy.  |
|  Bleeding/hematoma | Swelling, oozing from the insertion site | If localised bleeding at the insertion site, apply pressure and reinforce dressing. Reassess the dressing site regularly for further bleeding.  |
|  Thrombosis | Inability to draw blood/flush; swelling of limb | Order ultrasound Doppler to assess. If thrombus is found, remove CVL and consider anticoagulation therapy.  |
|  Arterial puncture | Return of pulsatile, bright red blood | Remove CVL and apply pressure on puncture site for at least 15–20 min. In the event of significant puncture with uncontrolled bleeding, consult the vascular surgeons immediately.  |
|  Fistula, pseudoaneurysm | Pulsatile swelling with or without bruising | Refer vascular surgeons.  |
|  Air embolism | Shortness of breath, chest pain, seizures, loss of consciousness, hemiplegia | Intensive supportive care.  |
|  Catheter migration/embolisation | Visualisation on X-ray, arrhythmias | Remove CVL. In event of embolisation of CVL fragment, consult the interventional radiologist for retrieval.  |
|  Cardiac irritation by guidewire (for IJV cannulation) | Arrhythmias | Withdraw guidewire and monitor heart rate and rhythm. Continue with procedure if safe to do so.  |
|  Retained guidewire | Guidewire fracture on removal or guidewire left in-situ | Refer to surgeons or interventional radiology for retrieval and removal.  |
|  Pneumothorax/haemothorax (for IJV cannulation) | Respiratory distress, desaturation, difficulty with ventilation, reduced air entry, hemodynamic compromise | Chest drain insertion.  |
|  Nerve injury | Paraesthesia, weakness | Serial assessment of motor and sensory function.  |

# Bibliography

1. KK Women's and Children's Hospital. KKH CJCU Handbook. Singapore.

2. Trieschmann U, Cate UT, Sreeram N. (2007) Central venous catheters in children and neonates — What is important? Images Paediatr Cardiol 9(4):1-8.

3. Scott-Warren V, Morley R. (2015) Paediatric vascular access. BJA Educ 15(4):199-206.

4. Oxford Medical Education. (2016) Central line (central venous catheter) insertion. https://www.oxfordmedicaleducation.com/clinical-skills/procedures/central-line/.

# CHAPTER 10

# Chest Tube Insertion

Ng Si Min, Pamela; Cher Yuqin; Chan Meng Fai, Joel; Lee York Tien

## Indications

- For relief of large or symptomatic pneumothorax.
- Drainage of pleural fluid collections (haemothorax, pleural effusion, chylothorax, empyema), allow collection of samples for investigations.
- Inserted post-operatively to allow drainage of blood/fluid, e.g. post-cardiac operations.
- Part of treatment for empyema thoracis — drainage of pleural effusion and instillation of urokinase.
Rarely: Instillation of intrapleural antibiotics and pleurodesis agents.

## Cautions

- Suspicion of pulmonary tumours/pleural-based malignancy — the lungs may be adherent to chest wall, increasing risk of lung injury during chest tube insertion. \(\rightarrow\) Advised to be done under imaging guidance or by surgical team if indicated.
- Bleeding tendencies — coagulopathy or platelet count \(< 50 \times 10^{9} / \mathrm{L}\)
\(\rightarrow\) Consider doing under fresh frozen plasma or platelet cover

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian, and where appropriate, the child:

- Procedural steps, analgesia and/or sedation plans
- Written consent
- Possible complications, such as pain, bleeding, infection and injury to surrounding structures (including lung laceration/perforation)

## Site Selection

The *triangle of safety* is defined by the lateral edge of the pectoralis major, base of axilla, anterior border of latissimus dorsi and the 5th intercostal space (Figure 3.5). Enter superior to the rib edge to avoid the neurovascular bundle.

# Chest Tube Insertion

![img-27.jpeg](img-27.jpeg)

Figure 3.5 The triangle of safety (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/Dr Stella Zhang).

## Equipment Preparation

- Chest tube: As a general guide, use smaller sizes for draining air and larger sizes for draining fluid. A sizing guide is as follows:

Newborn: 8-12 Fr
Infant: 12-16 Fr
> Child: 16-24 Fr
Adolescent: 20-32 Fr

- Sterile procedure set and drape set
- Chlorhexidine cleansing solution
- \(3\mathrm{ml}\) or \(5\mathrm{ml}\) syringe for drawing up of \(1\%\) lignocaine
1% lignocaine
- Scalpel
- Artery forceps
- Suture set with silk 3-0, and prolene 3-0 (Optional — as stay suture to facilitate closure of wound during removal of chest drain later)
- Tegaderm with pad
Hypafix
Underwater seal

## Sedation and Analgesia

Chest tube insertion should be performed under moderate sedation (e.g. IV ketamine) whenever feasible. Local anaesthesia should be given with subdermal administration of 1% lignocaine (0.3 ml/kg, up to 10 ml) to the insertion site.

b4854_Sec-03.indd 81

12-Dec-22 10:54:44 AM

## Pre-Procedural Steps

1. Ensure written consent has been taken.
2. Conduct a time out to ensure the correct patient, site and procedure.
3. Ensure that all equipment are prepared (refer to checklist above).
4. Position the patient: Lie the patient supine at an inclination of 45°, with the arm (ipsilateral side as chest tube placement) above the head (Figure 3.5).
5. Identify the Triangle of Safety (Figure 3.5) — Site of insertion is the 4th intercostal space, in the mid axillary line

## Procedural Steps

1. Clean the skin with 2% chlorhexidine with alcohol.
2. Drape the area with sterile dressing towel.
3. Administer procedural sedation with appropriate monitoring.
4. At the site of insertion, infiltrate the skin and the intercostal space with 1% lignocaine.
5. Using a scalpel, make an incision (2–3 cm) in the skin at the superior border of the 5th rib.
   TIP: Avoid making incisions near the inferior border of the rib, as the neurovascular bundle runs here.
6. Using the artery forceps, dissect through the intercostal muscles in the 4th intercostal space, and continue blunt dissection down to the pleura.
   TIP: Direct the dissection towards the chest (‘stabbing into the chest’) to avoid dissecting along the subcutaneous space and ensure the size of wound is adequate in all layers to accommodate the diameter of the chest tube.
7. On entering the pleural space (you will feel a sensation of a ‘give’), ensure the wound is widened with the artery forceps or finger.
   TIP: Be careful not to insert the artery forceps more than 1 cm into the pleural cavity.
8. Prepare the chest tube and remove the trocar. Using the artery forceps, grasp the tip of the chest drain and advance it through the incision into the pleural cavity.
9. Angle the chest tube towards either
   (i) the superior pole of the lung for drainage of air or
   (ii) the xiphisternum for drainage of fluid.
      TIP: Ensure that all exit holes of the chest tube are within the pleural cavity. The correct placement of chest drain in pleural space will be evidenced by fluctuance of moist air and/or fluid in the chest drain.
10. Attach the chest tube to the underwater seal.
11. Place the stay suture with prolene 3-0 into a purse string stitch, and anchor the chest tube to the chest wall using silk 3-0 (Figure 3.6). The stay suture is used to close the wound after the removal of the chest tube.
12. Obtain a chest X-ray (CXR) to ascertain the position of the chest tube.

|  Complication | Monitoring | Intervention  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Bleeding Excessive bleeding can occur in the event of laceration to major vessels (axillary, intercostal, pulmonary, internal mammary) | Bleeding from insertion point, e.g. soaking of dressing Increasing/persistently blood-stained drainage from chest tube, especially if exceedingly more than the anticipated effusion/haemothorax volume seen on CXR/CT thorax | Consider checking coagulation profile and platelet levels. Correct as required. Repeat imaging to assess for accumulation of a haemothorax Inform the surgical team.  |
|  Infection | Local signs of erythema, pain, swelling, purulent discharge from wound Fever | Appropriate antibiotic therapy. If any purulent discharge, to send for gram stain and culture. Consider removal and reinsertion at alternative site (if the chest tube is still required).  |
|  Lung laceration/ perforation/latrogenic broncho-pleural fistula | Bleeding, respiratory distress Persistent bubbling in chest drain indicates an ongoing air leak from lung/latrogenic broncho-pleural fistula | Repeat chest imaging. Provide oxygen/respiratory support as clinically indicated. Inform the surgical team to evaluate the need for surgical intervention.  |
|  Injury to other organ systems | Signs of hypovolaemia or cardiac tamponade Abdominal distention/ pneumoperitoneum from iatrogenic perforation of diaphragm | Resuscitation as necessary. Inform the surgical team to evaluate the need for surgical intervention.  |
|  Tube blockage | Lack of oscillation Acute reduction of drainage Respiratory distress, desaturations, reaccumulation of fluid/air on chest radiograph | Milk along the length of the chest tube. Flush the chest tube with normal saline under aseptic technique. Consider change of tube. Repeat imaging to assess position of tube/residual contents.  |
|  Injury to nerve bundle | Horner's syndrome — injury to sympathetic chain (miosis, ptosis, and anhidrosis) Diaphragmatic palsy — injury to phrenic nerve (breathlessness, difficulty weaning from mechanical ventilation; decreased breath sounds and dullness to percussion of the affected side of the chest) | Supportive treatment. Supportive treatment. If symptomatic, may need surgical intervention such as plication of diaphragm and phrenic nerve stimulation.  |

- Monitor for bleeding/signs of infection.
- Monitor for signs of tube blockage.
- Monitor for haemodynamic instability resulting from possible complications such as lung laceration or injury to surrounding structures (liver/spleen).
- Remove the stay suture 1 week following the removal of the chest tube.

# CHAPTER 11

# Bone Marrow Aspiration and Trephine

Cher Yuqin; Ng Si Min, Pamela; Lee Ming Wei

## Indications

- Definitive diagnosis of haematologic disorders, such as leukaemia, aplastic anaemia, etc.
- Evaluation of pyrexia of unknown origin
- For staging of lymphomas and solid organ tumours
- To monitor response to cancer treatment

## Contraindications

- Infection at site of procedure
- When contraindications to sedation (required for the procedure) are present
- Congenital or acquired coagulopathies, e.g. haemophilia/disseminated intravascular coagulopathy are not absolute contraindications. However, the risk of procedural bleeding needs to be balanced with the need for a diagnostic sample, and the necessary peri-procedural precautions, e.g. administration of factor concentrates, should be taken.
- Thrombocytopenia is NOT a contraindication for Bone Marrow Aspirate (BMA)/trephine, but post-procedure bleeding should be anticipated and the necessary peri-procedural precautions taken.

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian, and where appropriate, the child:

- Procedural steps, analgesia and/or sedation plans
- Written consent
- Possible complications such as pain, bleeding, infection and injury to surrounding structures

## Site Selection

Common sites include:

- Posterior superior iliac spine (patient should be placed prone or lateral) (Figure 3.7)
- Anterior tibial surface in infants \(< 12\) months old (patient should be placed supine) Alternative sites may include the anterior superior iliac spine or sternum.

- \(20\mathrm{ml}\) syringes as needed
- Bone marrow aspirate needle and/or trephine needle
- Specimen tubes/bottles required for sample collection

## Sedation and Analgesia

If no contraindications to sedation are present, bone marrow aspiration and trephine should be performed under moderate sedation (e.g. IV ketamine). Provide adequate analgesia, with local anaesthetic cream (e.g. EMLA cream) applied with occlusive dressing over the biopsy site 30 to 60 minutes prior to procedure) and lignocaine infiltrated into the skin and periosteum.

## Pre-Procedural Steps

1. Ensure written consent has been taken.
2. Conduct a time out to ensure the correct patient and procedure.

b4854_Sec-03.indd 86

12-Dec-22 10:54:50 AM

# Bone Marrow Aspiration and Trephine

3. Ensure that all equipment is prepared (refer to checklist above) and haematology laboratory technician is available and ready.
4. Position the patient prone, lateral or supine position depending on the site of bone marrow aspiration. Take care to maintain the airway of the patient during the sedation.
5. Locate and identify landmarks carefully as stated in site selection above.

## Bone Marrow Aspiration Procedure Steps

1. Administer procedural sedation with appropriate monitoring.
2. Clean the skin with \(2\%\) chlorhexidine with alcohol.
3. Drape the area with sterile dressing towels.
4. At the biopsy site, infiltrate the skin and the periosteum with \(1\%\) lignocaine.
5. Holding bone marrow aspiration needle with stylet in place, puncture skin and advance through subcutaneous tissue, periosteum and into the marrow cavity using steady, controlled pressure with a twisting motion. When the needle is firmly in place and a slight pressure is felt, the cavity has been entered.
6. Remove the stylet and attach a \(20\mathrm{ml}\) syringe to the needle and aspirate \(0.5\mathrm{ml}\) of marrow.

- Hand the syringe to the laboratory technologist to obtain bone marrow smears.
- The laboratory technologist will comment on the quality of smears obtained and if a repeat aspirate should be attempted.
- Repeat the above step until satisfactory smears are obtained.

7. If the quality of smears obtained is satisfactory, obtain other samples with separate syringes as needed.
8. When sample collection has been completed, withdraw the needle very slowly using a rotatory motion.
9. Using a sterile gauze, apply manual pressure to the bone marrow aspiration site until bleeding stops. An assistant can do this.

![img-31.jpeg](img-31.jpeg)

Figure 3.8 Bone marrow aspiration procedure (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/ Dr Stella Zhang).

b4854_Sec-03.indd 87

12-Dec-22 10:54:52 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Bone Marrow Trephine Biopsy Procedure Steps

1. The trephine biopsy can be performed through the same skin incision as the bone marrow aspiration but angled differently such that a different area of the bone marrow is biopsied.
2. Holding the trephine needle with stylet in place, puncture the skin and advance through subcutaneous tissue and periosteum into the cortex of the bone using steady, controlled pressure with a twisting motion.
3. When the needle is in position in the cortex, it should feel firmly anchored and can stand on its own.
4. Remove the stylet and advance the needle further into the marrow cavity another \(1 - 2\mathrm{cm}\). There will usually be a slight decrease in resistance when the marrow cavity has been entered.
5. Rotate the trephine needle with three full twists to the right and then to the left without advancing. Rock the needle tip slightly from side to side to loosen the bone specimen from surrounding tissue.
6. Advance the needle slightly another 2 to \(3\mathrm{mm}\) to ensure the bone marrow specimen is captured in place within the needle.
7. Withdraw the needle very slowly using a rotatory motion.
8. Using a sterile gauze, apply manual pressure to the bone marrow aspiration site until bleeding stops. An assistant can do this.
9. To remove the bone marrow specimen from the trephine needle, introduce the stylet through the distal cutting end of the needle to prevent crushing the specimen (Figure 3.10). Drop the bone specimen onto a glass slide provided by the medical technologist.

## Post-Procedural Monitoring and Care

- Apply a dry gauze and pressure bandage over the bone marrow aspiration/trephine site.
- Monitor for signs of bleeding and infection.

![img-32.jpeg](img-32.jpeg)

Figure 3.9 Bone marrow trephine biopsy procedure (Illustrated by Dr Sharon Goh/Dr Stella Zhang).

# CHAPTER 12

# Exchange Transfusion

Chow Wen Hann; Eleah Nolasco Vidal; Shrenik Vora

## Definition

- Exchange transfusion is a process of replacing a part of the infant's blood with donor blood by repeatedly exchanging small aliquots of blood over a short period of time.

## Indications

- Severe unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia in the newborn due to any cause when intensive phototherapy fails or when there is a high risk of bilirubin encephalopathy.
- Severe anaemia (where there is normal or increased circulating blood volume), especially from haemolysis.

## Contraindications

- Haemodynamic instability, as such infants who may not tolerate the fluid shifts.

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian:

Procedural steps
- Written consent
Possible catheter-related complications, haemodynamic and electrolyte problems

## Equipment Preparation

- Infant warmer
- Cardiorespiratory monitor
- Pulse oximeter for oxygen saturation monitoring
- Resuscitation equipment and medication (should be readily available)
- Infant restraints
- Nasogastric (NG)/orogastric tube
- Suctioning equipment
- Equipment for vascular access: Umbilical venous catheter (UVC) or peripheral venous cannula

90

b4854_Sec-03.indd 90

12-Dec-22 10:54:56 AM

# Exchange Transfusion

Blood warmer
- Sterile exchange transfusion equipment

Two three-way stopcocks with locking connections
5, 10, 20 ml syringes
Intravenous (IV) connecting tubing
Waste receptacle (empty IV bottle or bag)

- Appropriate blood product (cross-matched blood for exchange transfusion)
- Syringes and tubes for pre- and post-exchange blood investigations

## Blood Volume

Volume of blood for exchange is calculated using an estimate of the neonate's circulating blood volume, i.e. \(80~\mathrm{ml / kg}\)
- Exchange transfusion involves double volume exchange blood transfusion using compatible blood. Hence, total exchange volume is double the circulating blood volume \((2\times 80\mathrm{ml / kg}\approx 160\mathrm{ml / kg})\) , maximum \(500~\mathrm{ml}\) as per our institution's practice.

## Pre-Procedure Steps

- Ensure written consent has been taken.
- Inform Blood Bank of procedure and retrieve blood product.
- Insert NG tube and aspirate all gastric contents before line insertion.
- Set lines:

If UVC method is used, wet infant's umbilical cord with gauze soaked with normal saline prior to UVC insertion OR
Intra-arterial lines and peripheral venous lines.

- Set up IV drip set (plain dextrose \(10\%\) or dextrose \(10\%\) with added sodium (no added potassium)).
- Perform blood investigations:

Blood Group Typing — as it will need to be matched with donor blood
Full blood count, reticulocyte count, Direct Coomb's Test — to look for haemolysis
Renal panel, calcium (Ca), magnesium \((\mathrm{Mg})\) , phosphate \((\mathrm{PO}_4)\) and capillary blood glucose to check baseline electrolytes and correct them accordingly, as exchange transfusion may aggravate these abnormalities.
Peripheral blood culture if sepsis is suspected, e.g. in a pyrexial infant

- Put infant on cardiac monitoring as there is risk of arrhythmias during the procedure.

## Procedural Steps

Exchange transfusion can be performed by either of these techniques:

1) Push–pull technique — using single umbilical venous catheter and 2 three-way stopcocks

b4854_Sec-03.indd 91

12-Dec-22 10:54:57 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

2) Iso-volumetric technique (arteriovenous method) — using peripheral arterial catheter for blood draw from infant and a peripheral venous catheter/UVC for infusion of donor blood to the infant.

## Push–Pull Technique

1) Insert and secure UVC (see chapter on 'Umbilical artery and vein catheterisation')
2) Connect UVC according to Figure 3.11.
3) Start by withdrawing blood from infant at \(10\mathrm{ml / min}\)

a. For infants \(2 - 3\mathrm{kg}\), withdraw blood in \(10\mathrm{ml}\) aliquot.
b. For infants \(>3\mathrm{kg}\), withdraw blood in \(20\mathrm{ml}\) aliquot.

Note: Amount of blood aliquots may differ based on institutional practice

4) After first withdrawal of blood in appropriate aliquot, proceed to infuse whole blood at the same rate. Continue this process for the whole exchange transfusion.
5) Ensure there are no air bubbles while introducing blood, to avoid air embolism.
6) Put the infant on continuous cardiorespiratory monitoring. Monitor the temperature as well.
7) Midway during the exchange transfusion, do ionic calcium, blood glucose and blood gas. Correct any abnormalities as necessary. Consider calcium gluconate infusion, if required.

![img-34.jpeg](img-34.jpeg)

Figure 3.11 Push–pull technique connection.

## Iso-volumetric Technique (Arteriovenous method)

UVC + arterial line OR peripheral venous line + arterial line

1) Insert UVC or peripheral IV plug and connect line to the blood according to Figure 3.12.
2) Insert peripheral arterial line and connect a syringe and three-way tap to waste container according to Figure 3.13

# Exchange Transfusion

![img-35.jpeg](img-35.jpeg)

Figure 3.12 Iso-volumetric technique venous connection.

![img-36.jpeg](img-36.jpeg)

Figure 3.13 Iso-volumetric technique arterial connection.

3) Withdraw and discard blood from arterial side at a rate of \(10\mathrm{ml / min}\), and infuse blood simultaneously at same rate into venous side. Keep flow as steady as possible and volumetrically equal for infusion and removal.
4) Follow the rest of the steps of push-pull technique until exchange is completed.

## Troubleshooting Points

If the infant develops respiratory distress, irritability, cardiac arrhythmias, bradycardia (HR < 110/min) or tachycardia (HR >160/min), pallor or cyanosis during the exchange transfusion, stop the exchange immediately. Assess and resuscitate accordingly and consider transferring to higher acuity care area (high dependency or CICU) for closer monitoring.

## Post-Procedural Steps

- Continue intense phototherapy and remove umbilical lines.
- Other investigations and monitoring listed in Table 3.9.

# CHAPTER 13

# Umbilical Artery and Vein Catheterisation

Huang Peiqi; Chow Wen Hann; Kong Juin Yee

# Indications for Umbilical Artery Catheterisation (UAC)

- Measurement of arterial blood gases
- Continuous monitoring of arterial blood pressure
- Exchange transfusion

# Indications for Umbilical Vein Catheterisation (UVC)

- Emergency vascular access during newborn resuscitation
- Exchange transfusion
- Central venous access for parenteral nutrition
- Infusion of vasopressors or hyperosmolar solutions
- Central venous pressure monitoring
- Difficult venous access
- Balloon Atrial Septostomy (BAS)

# Contraindications for Umbilical Vessel Catheterisation

- Omphalocele
- Gastrochisis
- Omphalitis
- Relative contraindications include vascular compromise to lower limbs and buttocks, necrotising enterocolitis (NEC) or peritonitis

# Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian:

- Procedural steps
- Written consent
- Possible complications, such as bleeding, infection, damage to surrounding structures, vascular compromise and catheter malposition requiring line removal

# Umbilical Artery and Vein Catheterisation

![img-37.jpeg](img-37.jpeg)

Figure 3.14 Umbilical vessels (Illustrated by Dr Huang Peiqi).

## Site Selection

There are usually two umbilical arteries and one umbilical vein (see Figure 3.14).

The umbilical arteries are small, thick-walled and round arteries.

The umbilical vein has a single large thin-walled oval/slit-like lumen.

## Equipment Preparation

- Sterile trolley, wiped with alcohol
- Sterile gown set, blue cap, mask and two sets of sterile surgical gloves each for operator and assistant
- Sterile umbilical catheter set, which contains

- Thermoplastic tray, McIndoe non-toothed dissecting forceps, Iris forceps, Curved Halstead artery forceps, Straight Halstead artery forceps, needle holder, scissors, Kimguard (sterile wrap), paper hand towel × 4, cord ligature, autoclave tape

- Blade
18 G drawing up needle
- \(10\mathrm{ml}\) syringes \(\times 2\)
- \(20\mathrm{ml}\) Normal Saline ampoule \(\times 1\)
- 3-way stopcock (red for UAC, blue for UVC)
Antiseptic swab sticks

For patients \(< 1\) kg: \(2\%\) chlorhexidine gluconate swab \(\times 3\)
For patients \(>1\) kg: \(2\%\) chlorhexidine gluconate swab with \(70\%\) isopropyl alcohol \(\times 3\)

- Alcohol swab with \(70\%\) isopropyl alcohol
Cord tie
UAC

3.5 Fr: For infants \(< 1\) kg
3.5-4 Fr: For infants 1-2 kg
4 Fr: For infants \(>2\) kg

  » 4 Fr: For infants <1 kg

## Pre-Procedure Steps

- Ensure written consent has been taken.
- Conduct a time out to ensure correct patient, site and procedure.
- Position and gently restrain limbs as necessary to prevent contamination or interruption of procedure once started.
- Calculate the length of insertion
  » UAC: (Birth weight in kg) × 3 + 9
  » UVC: Calculated UAC insertion length/2 + length of stump
- Perform hand hygiene and gown up with a cap, mask, sterile gown and double sterile gloves.
- Prepare tray and equipment.
  » Cut a hole in the drape approximately the size of the umbilical cord stump.
  » The nursing assistant will open the items using the No-Touch technique
  » Connect the catheter to the 3-way stopcock and prime the catheter with normal saline. Ensure there is no leak. Turn stopcock to off position.
  » Keep the plastic sheath covering the catheter until it is ready to be used.
- Clean the insertion site under aseptic technique.
  » Clean the umbilical stump as well as clamp thoroughly, furthest from the abdominal wall to the circumferential area of abdominal wall surrounding the stump. Care should be taken for the extremely low birth weight (ELBW) infants and clean ONLY the stump, avoiding excessive scrubbing of the abdominal wall, in order to prevent abrasions or burns on their premature skin.
  » Place sterile paper at the 6 o'clock region of the stump over the abdominal area to allow the umbilical ties to rest on. This is to keep the string of the ties sterile so that it can be loosened/or tightened whenever needed during the procedure, while maintaining sterility.
  » Tie the umbilical cord with sterile cord tie using a simple single knot.
  » The knot should be tight enough to prevent bleeding but loose enough to allow catheterisation of the umbilical catheters.
  » Hold the cord upright by grasping the cord clamp with a toothed forceps or gloved fingers by the assisting nurse. Cut the cord horizontally 1–2 cm above

# Umbilical Artery and Vein Catheterisation

» Place the pre-cut sterile drape over the cut cord, exposing the cord through the hole. The entire length of umbilical tie should be brought up over with the cord to enable access if necessary throughout the procedure.

Gently blot any blood with gauze for better visualisation of the lumens.

## Procedural Steps — Umbilical Artery Catheter

- Identify the three vessels (2 arteries and 1 vein).
- Control any bleeding by adjusting the umbilical tie and blotting the cut surface.
- Use the haemostat to hold the side of cord to stabilise the cord during dilation and insertion. This can be done with an assistant, if available.
- Open the curved iris forceps and gently insert one tip of the iris forceps into the artery lumen, to gently dilate the vessel. Subsequently insert both tips of the closed iris forceps into the artery lumen. Be gentle and do not force it as this may create a false passage in the artery.
- Once in the lumen of the artery, gently allow the spring of the iris forceps to spread apart as the artery then relaxes and dilates. Repeat this until the tip of the forceps is 0.5 to \(1\mathrm{cm}\) deep into the lumen, and the wall is dilated adequately to accommodate the selected catheter.
- Be careful not to disrupt the support of the surrounding jelly.
- Following adequate dilation of the vessel wall, insert the saline-filled catheter into the umbilical artery between the tips of the iris forceps already in the umbilical vessel (Figure 3.15). This can also be done without keeping the iris forceps in the

Figure 3.15 Dilation of umbilical artery with iris forceps (Illustrated by Dr Huang Peiqi).

lumen if the vessel is too small to accommodate both. After passing the catheter 1.5 to 2 cm into the umbilical vessel, the indwelling iris forceps can be removed.

- Apply gentle and steady pressure and advance the catheter slowly, while applying gentle traction (counter forces) on the cord to support the body of the cord.
- Insertion should be smooth up till the measured pre-calculated level.
- Once the UAC is in the correct position, arterial blood (in an infant with normal oxygenation) should be easily drawn back with no resistance.
- Flush the line with the primed normal saline to prevent any clotting.
- Proceed with UVC insertion (as below) if required.

# Umbilical Artery and Vein Catheterisation

Table 3.12 Troubleshooting Points for UAC and UVC Insertion

|  Problems Encountered | Troubleshooting  |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|   |  UAC | UVC  |
|  Inability to advance | - Obstruction may be encountered at different levels:     - 2–3 cm: This may be due to the umbilical tie being too tight or the catheter being located at the entry level of the anterior abdomen. May try loosening the umbilical tie and applying 30–60 sec of gentle steady pressure.     - 6–8 cm: This corresponds to the level of the bladder or the curvature of the umbilical artery before it enters the iliac artery. May try positioning infant on their side or applying some abdominal pressure. | - Resistance may be encountered at 3–5 cm upon insertion. - This may be due to the UVC entering the portal venous system (see below) or impeded blood flow due to angle of the UVC, which may be overcome with simple manoeuvres such as gentle pressure on the abdomen.  |
|  Unusual Course | - The UAC may loop caudally back down the contralateral iliac artery or one of the arteries leading to the buttocks. - Consider using a larger stiffer catheter or retracting the catheter, rotating it and readvancing it into the aorta | - Insertion to the portal system - There is commonly no back flow of blood once it is in the portal system. Avoid repetitive probing, or trying to advance the catheter tip by force, as this may lead to liver injury. - Pull back the catheter, up to 2 cm level from the tip of the catheter and reinsert it. Try rotating the catheter during insertion. - Try applying pressure on the liver and redirecting the tip of the UVC towards the left shoulder, so as to allow the catheter to slip towards the ductus venosus.  |
|  False Passage | - If a pop is felt with a sudden ‘release’ of the catheter, the catheter may have punctured the vessel wall leading through a false passage in the cord. If so, attempt umbilical artery catheterisation on the remaining artery instead (unless infant has single umbilical artery). | If false passage is suspected, do not attempt further umbilical vein catheterisation. Consider alternative access such as peripheral line or a peripherally inserted central catheter.  |

# The Baby Bear Book

## Procedural Steps: Umbilical Vein Catheter

- Use the haemostat to hold the side of the cord in order to stabilise it, with care not to accidentally remove the already inserted UAC.
- Remove any clot in the umbilical vein using iris forceps.
- The technique for UVC insertion is similar to that of UAC insertion. Catheter is inserted into the umbilical vein, which is larger and thin-walled compared to the umbilical artery. Efforts to further dilate the umbilical vein are usually not necessary.

- If the catheter is being used for emergency access, it should be advanced only as far as required to establish good blood flow, which is usually a depth of 5 cm. Secure the catheter between your fingers or with tape. Avoid inserting the catheter too deeply, as the catheter may enter the portal system instead of the IVC. The inadvertent administration of medication and fluids through the hepatic vessels may result in hepatocellular damage.

- Ascertain the accurate position of the catheters with an X-ray of the chest and abdomen.

- Secure the umbilical catheters by suturing them to the umbilical stump while awaiting X-ray confirmation. Leave the fields sterile during this time.

- The ideal position for the UAC tip should be located at T6-9 for a high UAC or L3-5 for a low UAC. The high UAC position is preferred due to association of complications with low UAC placement.
- The ideal position for the UVC tip should be at the diaphragm (T8-9).
- Review the X-ray images immediately to determine if the tip position is satisfactory and adjust if necessary at the same setting. Repeat X-ray is recommended to document the final position of the catheters in the procedure note.
- Following confirmation of the position, secure the umbilical catheters further by putting additional knots along the umbilical lines outside of the body.
- Connect the infusate at the same time.

- For UAC, run a patency drip with heparinised saline (typically 0.45% sodium chloride for preterm, 0.9% sodium chloride for term).

- For UVC, start desired infusate (heparinised total parental nutrition/dextrose drip).

## Post-Procedural Steps

- Dispose of any sharps and perform hand hygiene.
- Document the final position of the catheters, the length inserted and all adjustments made.
Monitoring: Leave the umbilical catheter site exposed, do not cover with a diaper.
- Readjustment of UVC/UAC after sterile drape is broken is strongly discouraged as this might led to contamination and risk of Central Line Associated Blood Stream Infection (CLABSI). Withdrawal of a line is acceptable if absolutely necessary but pushing in of the catheter is not recommended.

# *Umbilical Artery and Vein Catheterisation*

# **Catheter Removal**

- Perform hand hygiene and put on sterile gloves. Prepare sterile dressing set. Swab the area to clean it, then apply drape around catheter site.
- When removing catheter, remove it slowly over 30–60 seconds while the catheter is still occluding the distal end.
- Secure clamping with forceps at the stump of the umbilical vessels concurrently with the removal of the tip of catheter to stop bleeding.

# **Video Resources**

# CHAPTER 14

# Percutaneous-Inserted Central Catheter Insertion

Huang Peiqi; Chow Wen Hann; Kong Juin Yee

## Indications

- Long-term need for intravenous access (e.g. parenteral nutrition or prolonged antibiotics)
- Safe and continuous administration of hyperosmolar solutions (e.g. concentrated dextrose solution) or vasoactive medications (e.g. inotropes)
- Secure vascular access, if peripheral options are limited or exhausted

## Contraindications

Active bloodstream infection
Thrombus in the targeted vein
- Excessive bleeding tendency

Aim platelets $> 50 \times 10^9/\text{L}$

- Correct any coagulopathy before proceeding with insertion of percutaneous-inserted central catheter (PICC)

## Patient Communication and Consent

The following should be discussed with the parent or legal guardian:

- Procedural steps, analgesia and/or sedation plans
- Written consent
- Possible complications such as bleeding, infection, damage to surrounding structures including pleura and pericardium, catheter occlusion or obstruction requiring line removal

## Site Selection

- Select a large bore vessel (Figure 3.16)

Great saphenous veins, antecubital fossa veins are preferable
Other options include small saphenous vein, popliteal vein or scalp vein

- Jelco cannula set 20 G (optional)
- Sterile neonatal long line catheter set which contains:

- Thermoplastic tray, dissecting forceps × 2, Iris forceps (blunt tip), scissors, Kimguard (sterile wrap) × 3, hand paper towel × 4, autoclave tape

18 G drawing needle
21 G sharp green needle
- \(10\mathrm{ml}\) syringe \(\times 2\)
- \(20\mathrm{ml}\) Normal saline ampoule \(\times 1\)
- Sterile measuring tape
3-way stopcock (Blue)
- Sterile Gauze pack
- Steristrips \(\times 2\)
- Tegaderm \(\times 3\)
- Alcohol swab
Antiseptic swab sticks

For patients \(< 1\) kg: \(2\%\) chlorhexidine gluconate swab \(\times 3\)
For patients \(>1\) kg: \(2\%\) chlorhexidine gluconate swab with \(70\%\) isopropyl alcohol \(\times 3\)

## Pre-Procedural Steps

- Ensure written consent has been taken.
- Conduct a time out to ensure correct patient, site and procedure.
- Position the face of the patient towards the insertion site, to reduce the risk of line migrating to the neck vessels.
Estimate depth of insertion.

For upper body (arm or scalp): Measure from insertion site along the course of the vein to the right of the sternal border, then to the 3rd intercostal space. For arm vein insertion, extend arm at a \(90^{\circ}\) angle.
For lower extremity: Measure from the insertion site along the course of the vein to the right of the umbilicus and up to the xiphoid.

- Perform hand hygiene and gown up with cap, mask, sterile gown and double sterile gloves.
Prepare tray and equipment

Cut a hole in the drape approximately the size of the limb selected.
The nursing assistant will open the items using the No-Touch technique.
Connect and prime the line with normal saline. Ensure there is no leak.
» Fold the sterile paper to cover catheter to prevent it from being contaminated.

- Clean insertion site with sterile technique.

Clean the limb from distal to proximal direction. Clean the upper limb to the axilla and the lower limb to the groin.
» Drape the patient. Place the drape with the arm through the hole carefully — ensure it is not too loose to prevent contamination of the insertion site.
Remove external glove after completing the cleaning process.

## Procedural Steps

- The 'No-Touch technique' should be practiced throughout the procedure. There should be no direct touching of the insertion site or catheter with the gloved finger. Use the iris forceps to handle the catheter at all times.
- For 24 G catheter (Refer to Figure 3.17 for the following steps)

- Access the selected vein with the introducer needle (A). Once blood return is seen, stop advancing the needle.

# Percutaneous-Inserted Central Catheter Insertion

- Insert the catheter (B) through the needle (A) slowly until the pre-measured desired length is achieved. The catheter should advance easily.

- ☐ If there is difficulty advancing the catheter, gently massage the vein or readjust the limb or needle position

- Partially unscrew the blue connector hub (E) to release the distal end (D) of the catheter. The needle (A) is then removed carefully without injuring the catheter itself.

- ☐ If there is bleeding at the insertion site, use a gauze to apply pressure until the bleeding stops.

- Carefully reinsert the distal end of catheter (D) back into the blue connector hub (E), ensuring that the black marking (C) is completely hidden inside the blue connector hub.

- Flush the catheter intermittently with normal saline (F) once it is connected, to prevent back flow or occlusion, with a 10 ml syringe (G). The use of smaller syringes is discouraged as it will generate higher pressure and risk damage to the line.

For 28 G catheter

- Access the vein using a 24 G IV cannula.

- Remove the stylet then feed the catheter through the plastic hub.

- Flush the inner hub intermittently with saline to clean the clots.

- Once the estimated length has been achieved, remove the plastic hub without tugging onto the catheter.

- Temporarily secure the catheter with steristrips. Cover the insertion site with gauze while awaiting X-ray confirmation of line position.

The ideal location for the catheter tip is

- Upper limb and scalp: Superior vena cava, or the superior vena cava right atrial junction (Figure 3.18)

Lower limb: Inferior vena cava

- Lines that course into the neck or cardiac chambers must be immediately withdrawn and rechecked on a repeat X-ray.

- Once the line position is confirmed to be optimal, secure the line, ensuring all parts of the line and steristrip are covered completely by tegaderm.

![img-41.jpeg](img-41.jpeg)

Figure 3.18 Optimal PICC tip placement at the superior vena cava — right atrial junction (Illustrated by Dr Huang Peiqi).

# The Baby Bear Book

» The dressing should not encircle the limb, and the plastic hub should be placed on a small piece of gauze to avoid pressure damage to the skin.

- Prime the infusate and connect to the pump before connecting to the line. This should be done in a sterile manner and the infusate should run immediately to prevent occlusion.

## Post-Procedure Steps

- Dispose of any sharps and perform hand hygiene.
- Document the final position of the catheter, the length inserted and all adjustments made.

## Additional Points

- If the catheter tip cannot be advanced to the central venous circulation, it may be used functionally as a peripheral line, e.g. line tip terminating in the proximal third of the clavicle. With the catheter tip in this position, it can only be used to administer fluids suitable for peripheral administration.
- The risk of infection is proportionate to the duration of the indwelling catheter. Review for removal of PICC daily.

## Removal of PICC

- Perform hand hygiene and put on sterile gloves. Clean and drape the patient, Remove the dressing carefully to avoid injury to the skin.
- Gently pull out the catheter with a slow steady rate to minimise the risk of phlebitis or break of line. Grasp the line and not the hub.

Table 3.13 Procedural Complications of PICC Insertion

|  Complications | Monitoring  |
| --- | --- |
|  Central Line Associated Blood Stream Infection (CLABSI) | Signs of infection including fever, shock, acidosis and line insertion site erythema, swelling or discharge  |
|  Intravascular thrombosis | Swelling of limb with inability to flush line  |
|  Pleural and pericardial effusions | Respiratory distress, reduced air entry, difficulty with ventilation, cardiorespiratory instability May have unexplained hypoglycaemia  |
|  Cardiac tamponade | Acute or refractory hypotension, arrhythmias, cardiac arrest, respiratory deterioration, cardiomegaly on CXR  |
|  Arrhythmias | Arrhythmias noted on ECG monitoring, respiratory distress, pallor, cyanosis or hypotension  |
|  Air embolism | Respiratory distress, hypoxemia, arrhythmias, seizures  |
|  Arterial cannulation | Pulsation of blood flow, bright red blood  |
|  Catheter migration | Delayed migration may lead to any of the complications above  |
