# CHAPTER 15

# Sedation and Analgesia

Wong Ju Ming, Judith; Lim Kian Boon, Joel; Yeo Siok Hoong, Angela

## General Principles of Sedation and Analgesia

# 1. Sedation:

- To ensure comfort during painful and traumatic procedures through reducing anxiety, pain and/or consciousness with or without inducing amnesia (Figure 4.1).
- Monitored with the State Behavioural Scale (SBS score).

# 2. Analgesia:

- Defined as the absence of sensibility to pain (absence of pain on noxious simulation) or the relief of pain without loss of consciousness.
- Observe the analgesic ladder (Figure 4.2) — optimise lower steps before moving to higher steps.
- Monitored with the Neonatal Infant Pain Scale (NIPS), Face, Leg, Activity, Cry and Consolability Scale (FLACC), Wong-Baker Faces Pain Rating Scale and Numeric Rating scale as is age-appropriate.

3. Patients weighing \(>50\mathrm{kg}\) should be on standard adult doses rather than per kg dosing.
4. Anticipate and treat pain and agitation in patients rather than rescue an already distressed patient — observe the therapeutic window (Figure 4.3).

![img-42.jpeg](img-42.jpeg)

Figure 4.1 Procedural sedation alleviates factors contributing to distress.

112

5. Use the minimal amount of sedation/analgesia needed to accomplish the therapeutic goal.

- Do not use sub-therapeutic doses, which result in inadequate sedation yet exposes the patient to side effects.
- Use drugs for their primary effect, e.g. primary effect of morphine is analgesia.

6. Special consideration:

- Preterm/ex-preterm — Immature hepatic/renal metabolism/elimination resulting in prolonged effect and possible post-procedure apnoea.
- Local anaesthesia — Cardiac depression and central nervous system (CNS) depression/excitation, methaemoglobinemia. Pay attention to cumulative doses.

# Recommended Analgesia for Post-Operative Patients

1. Regular intravenous (IV)/oral (PO) Paracetamol at 15 mg/kg/dose (max. 1g/dose) for at least 3 days post-op.
2. Regular IV/PO Ibuprofen 10mg/kg/dose (max. 400 mg/dose).
3. Consider IV opioid infusion if pain anticipated/assessed to be severe.
4. If frequent or intense sporadic noxious stimuli are likely, consider referring to Children's Pain Service for patient or nurse-controlled analgesia (PCA or NCA).

# Procedural Sedation

1. Goals:

- Minimise the psychological distress and pain associated with the procedures.
- Optimise conditions for the procedure to be completed safely.
- Patient undergoes the sedation without any adverse events.

2. Consent for procedural sedation should ensure that patient or parent/legal guardians understand the sedation process, risks and benefits and alternatives to sedation.

3. Preparing for procedural sedation:

Essential equipment

Blood pressure monitoring device
Pulse oximeter
Oxygen source capable of delivering \(100\%\) oxygen continuously for at least 60 minutes, flow meter and appropriately sized self-inflating bag, masks and oral airways
Suction catheters and suction equipment
Cardiac monitor
Emergency 'crash cart' near location, including defibrillator

4. Roles and responsibilities

- All staff involved in sedation should be competent in:

Techniques and modes of sedation
Monitoring physiological parameters
Basic Cardiac Life Support and Bag and Mask skills
Recognising and managing complications
Initiating advanced resuscitation when necessary

- Doctor's/sedationist's responsibilities:

Sedation pre-assessment

i. Current medical history and physical examination record (including airway assessment)
ii. Previous sedation encounters

Non-pharmacological measures to facilitate minimal medication use:

1. A sedation strategy should follow the patient's trajectory, and weaning of sedation and analgesia should begin as soon as it is safe and appropriate to do so.

# Sedation and Analgesia

2. Over-sedation or unnecessarily prolonged sedation should be avoided, as it can lead to tolerance (and iatrogenic withdrawal syndrome), disrupted sleep, delirium, muscle weakness, and prolonged ventilation and immobility.

3. Iatrogenic withdrawal syndrome:

- The unpleasant signs and symptoms resulting from rapid/abrupt tapering of opioid, benzodiazepines or other drugs with CNS depressant effects.
- Manifests as symptoms of sympathetic overactivity, sleep-wake disturbance and seizures.
- Mitigated by use of \(\alpha\)-agonist clonidine to block sympathetic activity.

4. Monitored with the Withdrawal Assessment Tool (WAT-1) Scoring.

5. Clonidine withdrawal:

- Risk of rebound hypertension and increased sympathetic activity with rapid wean/cessation.
- Also requires gradual weaning, best done after opioid or benzodiazepine wean is complete.

6. Risk Factors for Withdrawal

- Infants less than 6 months of age
- Pre-existing cognitive impairment
- Use of regular opioids/benzodiazepine for 5 days or more
- Use of \(>2\) agents or high doses (e.g. morphine \(>40\mathrm{mcg / kg / h}\), midazolam \(>4\) mcg/kg/min)

## Therapeutic Dosages for Sedation and Analgesia

PO Chloral hydrate 50 mg/kg/dose once for non-painful procedures or 20 mg/kg/dose Q8H for maintenance sedation

IV/PO Paracetamol 10–15 mg/kg/dose Q6H (max. 1 g/dose)

IV/PO Ibuprofen 10 mg/kg/dose Q6H (max. 400 mg/dose)

IV Ketorolac 0.3–0.5 mg/kg/dose Q6H (max. 30 mg)

IV Morphine infusion 10–40 mcg/kg/h (titrate to effect)

IV Morphine bolus 0.05–0.1 mg/kg/dose (max. 10 mg)

IV Fentanyl bolus 0.3–0.5 mcg/kg/dose

Intranasal (IN) Fentanyl 1–2 mcg/kg/dose

IV Midazolam infusion 1–4 mcg/kg/min (titrate to effect)

IV Midazolam bolus 0.05–0.1 mg/kg/dose (max. 4 mg)

PO Mist Morphine (syrup) 0.2 to 0.4 mg/kg/dose (max. 15 mg) Q4–6H with/without 0.1mg/kg/dose (max. 15 mg) Q4H/PRN for breakthrough pain

PO Oxycodone (capsule) 0.1 mg/kg/dose (max. 5 mg) Q4–6H with/without 0.05 mg/kg/dose (max. 5 mg) Q4h/PRN for breakthrough pain

IV Ketamine bolus 1 mg/kg/dose Q15min

IV Ketamine infusion 0.1–0.2 mg/kg/h

IV Dexmedetomidine 0.2–0.7 (up to 1 for procedural sedation) mcg/kg/h

IN Dexmedetomidine 3–4 mcg/kg/dose (duration of action 40 min)

IV Clonidine infusion 0.1–3 mcg/kg/h

IV Clonidine bolus 1–4 mcg/kg/dose

PO Clonidine 2–4 mcg/kg/dose

# CHAPTER 16

# The Adolescent Interview: HEADSS Assessment

Kumudhini Rajasegaran; Courtney Davis; Chew Chu Shan, Elaine; Tan Sher Kit, Juliet; Oh Jean Yin

## Introduction

Adolescence is generally a healthy period of life in comparison to early childhood and old age. As healthcare professionals in contact with this group of individuals, it is important to understand adolescent development and to have effective communication and consultative skills to better guide treatment and management.

The Home, Education/Employment, Activities, Drugs, Sexuality, Suicide/Depression (HEADSS) assessment is an excellent guide to obtain a bio-psycho-social biopsy and an opportunity to build rapport with the adolescent. It aids in risk assessment as well as helps guide intervention.

## Confidentiality

Prior to starting a HEADSS assessment — it is important to address confidentiality and the limits of confidentiality with the adolescent patient and their parent. All information should be considered confidential with several exceptions: (1) Concerns for harm to self/suicidality, (2) Concerns for harm to adolescent (sexual or physical abuse), (3) Concern for harm to others, and (4) Disclosures that require police reporting, including underage sexual abuse/activity and drug use.

Please discuss with your senior colleagues if you are unsure about whether the information disclosed by an adolescent requires disclosure to the parents/guardians or authorities.

## HEADSS Comprehensive Adolescent Psycho-Social Screening Interview

Reminder: Discuss confidentiality and limits to confidentiality prior to starting

| --- |
|  - Who lives with the patient? Where? Own room? - What are the relationships like at home?  |

# CHAPTER 17

# Eating Disorders

Oh Jean Yin; Chew Chu Shan, Elaine; Courtney Davis; Kumudhini Rajasegaran; Tan Sher Kit, Juliet

## Introduction

Adolescents are at risk of developing an eating disorder with a lifetime prevalence reported between 6 and 12%. While binge eating disorder is the most common eating disorder, restrictive eating disorders most commonly present for clinical care. Anorexia nervosa is the most commonly known restrictive eating disorder; however, other clinical variants have now been described, including other specified feeding and eating disorders (OSFED) and avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder (ARFID). These conditions will usually present with physical manifestation of malnutrition, growth and pubertal concerns. Bulimia nervosa tends to present in older adolescents with little or minimal physical symptoms.

## Anorexia Nervosa

- Restriction of energy intake leading to a significantly low body weight in the context of age, sex, developmental trajectory and physical health.
- Intense fear of gaining weight or persistent behaviour that interferes with weight gain.
- Body image disturbance or persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.

## Sub Types

- Restricting: Only restriction of intake over the last 3 months
- Binge/purge: Engages in binge eating or purge behaviours (vomiting, laxatives, diuretics) in the last 3 months

## Clinical Manifestation

Most adolescents presenting with eating disorders will have the following issues:

- Characteristic cognitive and behavioural signs

  - Intentional caloric restriction
  - Preoccupation with weight, food, calories, fat, getting healthy and dieting
  - Thoughts of 'feeling fat' when weight is normal or low
  - Fear of gaining weight

123

b4854_Sec-05.indd 123

12-Dec-22 10:56:37 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Feelings of guilt and shame about eating
» Frequent weighing
Weight determines self-esteem
Binge-eating
Inappropriate compensatory behaviours - including self-induced vomiting, use of laxatives, diet pills, excessive exercise

- Growth and developmental abnormalities

» Precipitous weight loss or gain or frequent weight fluctuations
» Failure to gain expected weight or height
» Delayed or interrupted pubertal development

Medical consequences of the eating disorder

Nutritional deficiencies: acute malnutrition can be a medical emergency
Medical instability (see criteria for admission)

- Physical signs that may be seen in anorexia nervosa are listed below.

Hypothermia
» Bradycardia
Orthostatic pulse and blood pressure
Dull, thinning scalp hair
» Lanugo hair
» Emaciated, wears oversized clothes
Gastrointestinal side effects: constipation/bloating
» Flat affect
Cold extremities, acrocyanosis
Primary or secondary amenorrhea

If an eating disorder is suspected, the first priority is to determine medical stability and to evaluate for complications of an eating disorder. Patients can be medically unstable despite being at a normal body mass index (BMI). Rapid weight loss or poor oral intake prior to admission can lead to medical instability. A close follow-up appointment in 1–2 weeks is recommended, especially if there is rapid weight loss. The most common reason requiring admission is bradycardia.

## Medical Instability and Criteria for Acute Admission

- Resting heart rate \(< 50/\mathrm{min}\)
- Electrocardiogram (ECG) abnormalities, e.g. prolonged QTc
Significant postural and/or haemodynamic changes
Hypotension
Systolic BP drop \(>20\mathrm{mmHg}\) from lying to standing position
- Diastolic BP drop \(>10\mathrm{mmHg}\) from lying to standing position
- Heart rate increase of \(>30 / \mathrm{min}\) from lying to standing position
- Dehydration
- Temperature \(< 35.5^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\)
- Electrolyte abnormalities

# Eating Disorders

If considering psychological reasons for admission, including acute food refusal or safety concerns, please consider discussing it with the adolescent medicine physician on call.

## Suggested Investigations

ECG
Full blood count, renal panel, serum calcium, serum magnesium, serum phosphate, random blood glucose, LFT, TFT and in females, serum luteinizing hormone (LH), follicle stimulating hormone (FSH), oestradiol, vitamin D level
- Bone Mineral Density (BMD) — to consult adolescent medicine physician

## On admission to the General Paediatric Ward

Place on Eating Disorder (ED) protocol level 1 unless directed by adolescent medicine physician

Refer to the Adolescent Medicine (ADME) team — consisting of adolescent medicine physician, adolescent resource nurse and dietician

- Keep complete rest in bed
- Monitor vital parameters as per ED level 1 depending on medical status
Continuous cardiac monitoring
Postural blood pressure and heart rate twice daily
- Blood investigation and/or ECG as above if not already done
- Daily renal panel, serum calcium, serum magnesium, serum phosphate for the first 5 days and then as directed by the ADME team
- Suicide precautions if high risk for suicide or self-harm

## Refeeding Syndrome

The clinical manifestations of refeeding syndrome are caused by fatal shifts in fluids and electrolytes into the cells with rapid refeeding of starved patients in a catabolic state. The increased glucose load from refeeding can cause a surge of insulin secretion that stimulates glycogen, fat and protein synthesis. There is activation of the ATPase symporter, which transports glucose into the cells. Magnesium and phosphate are also taken up into the cells, and water follows by osmosis. These processes, coupled with a total body depletion of these electrolytes, result in a decrease in the serum levels of phosphate, potassium and magnesium. Refeeding hypophosphataemia is the most common with a nadir presenting at first few days of refeeding. Monitoring for the clinical signs of fluid shift, such as lower limb oedema, is important in the first few days of refeeding.

Patients who are admitted for medical instability are at risk of the refeeding syndrome. Their nutritional intake and meal plans should be medically supervised and ordered by the dietician to reduce the risk of the refeeding syndrome. Extra food that is not supplied by the hospital is thus strongly discouraged in the first week of admission. Frequent monitoring of serum electrolytes, in particular, serum phosphate, potassium, magnesium is recommended daily, especially for the first 5 days of refeeding.

b4854_Sec-05.indd 125

12-Dec-22 10:56:37 AM

### Goals of Treatment

- Medical stabilisation through nutritional rehabilitation with adequate weight gain and regular eating patterns.
- Monitoring for refeeding syndrome and early recognition of electrolyte abnormalities to prevent progression to refeeding syndrome.
- The average length of hospital stay is 10 to 14 days depending on the degree of medical instability and progress of nutritional rehabilitation.
- Reinforce to the family the seriousness of the condition — mortality rate 1.8–5.9% described.
- Psychoeducation for patient and family.
- Psychological assessment of the eating disorder, family assessment and introduction to outpatient treatment.

Family-based treatment (FBT) or Maudsley approach is now considered first-line treatment for adolescents with anorexia nervosa and is associated with faster and higher rates of recovery as well as lower rates of relapse. In our KKH study, patients with anorexia nervosa treated with FBT had faster and improved rates of weight restoration compared to patients not on FBT.

- Family (parental) involvement and empowering caregivers to renourish their adolescent.
- Non-blaming approach that focuses on rapid weight restoration to reduce physical and psychological effects of malnutrition on the adolescent.
- Best prognosis if there is rapid weight restoration with good engagement with treatment (achieving 2 kg weight gain in first 4 weeks of treatment with weight restoration within 6 months of presentation).
- Optimal care from multidisciplinary team approach
- Emphasises weight and growth restoration and resumption of menses or puberty
- Emphasises cessation of disordered eating pattern and bodyimage distortion with restoration of normal meal patterns that promote health and social norms

# CHAPTER 18

# Adolescent Sexual Health

Tan Sher Kit, Juliet; Karen Donceras Nadua; Seet Meei Jiun; Lee Hwee Chyen;
Courtney Davis; Chew Chu Shan, Elaine; Kumudhini Rajasegaran

# Introduction

Adolescent reproductive and sexual health is an important part of adolescent development,
which if neglected, may result in unmet sexual and reproductive healthcare needs. Hence,
healthcare professionals play an important role in supporting young people's sexual
development by providing accurate information and appropriate health services, as well
as promoting positive health behaviours.

The domains of sex and sexuality are important components to explore as part of
an adolescent's biopsychosocial assessment. Potential topics that may be beneficial to
discuss with the youth include his/her sexual history, any history of coercive sexual activity
or abuse, prior usage of contraception, menstrual history and history of pregnancy, if
applicable, and if he/she has had any prior sexually transmitted infections.

# Legal and Ethical Aspects of Sex with a Minor

In Singapore, sexual penetration of a minor below 16 years of age, with or without consent,
is considered an offence. The law has also placed a legal responsibility on anyone who is
aware of such a case to report it to the police.

Illegal sexual activity with a minor includes

- penile-vaginal penetration
- penile-anal penetration
- digital penetration
- penetration by a foreign body
- performing or receiving oral sex from a minor

As medical professionals, we are legally obliged to make a police report if:

- A minor discloses that he/she has been a participant in these sexual acts, regardless
of whether they were consensual or non-consensual, and also regardless of the
age of the alleged perpetrator.
- There is an incidental finding of pregnancy in an adolescent or a child, even in
the absence of any history of sexual activity or assault.
- There is an incidental finding of a sexually transmitted infection in a child, even
in the absence of any history of sexual activity or assault.

The disclosure of sexual activity in adolescents and making a police report can be a
difficult process for the adolescents and their families and has to be handled sensitively

128

b4854_Sec-05.indd 128

12-Dec-22 10:56:37 AM

and contextualised to every adolescent's unique medical and social situation. Junior doctors are advised to get advice from the senior doctor-in-charge and/or adolescent medicine team if unsure on how to manage the disclosure.

When one encounters a teenager who has engaged in sexual activity, screening for sexually transmitted diseases should be carried out after disclosure has been made to both adolescents and parents. Education and counselling on the legal age for sex, safe sex and contraception should be provided. A urine pregnancy test should also be offered to screen for pregnancy.

## Sexually Transmitted Infections

### Background

A sexually transmitted infection (STI) is an infection passed from one person to another through sexual contact. These infections can be transmitted through vaginal intercourse, oral and anal sex, or skin-to-skin contact. Anyone who is sexually active may be at risk of contracting STIs. All partners should be tested and treated accordingly on epidemiological grounds.

Early detection and treatment of STIs is crucial, as each STI can cause a range of health problems and future complications. These include pelvic inflammatory disease, infertility, ectopic pregnancy and increased risks of certain cancers (e.g. HPV-related pre-cancers or cancers). It is recommended that all sexually active adolescents get screened for STIs, even if asymptomatic at presentation.

### Evaluation for STIs

The sexual history of adolescent patients should be routinely obtained from the patients during both acute and routine healthcare encounters. During history taking, adolescents should be screened for risk factors and symptoms of STIs. It is also important to clarify with adolescents when using terms, such as 'sexually active' and 'sexual intercourse', as adolescents may have a differing understanding of these terms and details surrounding their sexual encounters and this may have implications on their STI risk.

### Symptoms of STI

Many STIs have only mild symptoms or no symptoms at all. Clinical presentation of STIs in adolescents are similar to those in adults. Symptoms, if present, may include:

- Penile or vaginal discharge
- Dysuria
- Pelvic pain
- Rashes
- Blisters or painless sores on the genital area, anus, tongue or throat
- Painless and/or warty growths on the ano-genital region, groin or thighs
- Jaundice, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting
- Swollen glands, fevers and body aches

# The Baby Bear Book

## Common STIs

For treatment options, please refer to the Appendix II for Drug Doses.

## Chlamydia

*Chlamydia trachomatis* can be carried in the female cervix, male urethra, and throat and rectum of both sexes. Individuals with chlamydia infections can be asymptomatic or can present with vaginal/penile discharge, abdominal pain, dyspareunia or urinary symptoms. Untreated chlamydial infections can result in cervicitis (women) and epididymitis (men) and for women, in particular, can lead to pelvic inflammatory disease (PID), infertility, chronic pelvic pain and increased risk of ectopic pregnancy.

Of note, chlamydia, which is often asymptomatic in older adolescent females and women, can present with abnormal vaginal discharge in younger females.

### Testing

- Chlamydia PCR (urine, or endocervical/pharyngeal/rectal/urethral swab)

## Gonorrhoea

Individuals with gonorrhoea infections may be asymptomatic or may present with urethritis (males) or cervicitis/salpingitis (females). Untreated infections can lead to disseminated gonorrhoeal infections, including that of septic arthritis, pelvic inflammatory disease and potential infertility.

### Testing

- Gonorrhoea PCR (urine, or endocervical/pharyngeal/rectal/urethral swab)

Chlamydia and gonorrhoea are always tested together in view of the high prevalence of co-infection.

If chlamydial infection has not been excluded in a patient with confirmed gonorrhoeal infection, or if symptoms are consistent with cervicitis/urethritis/cervicitis in a sexually active adolescent with a high risk of non-adherence to follow up, patients should be treated for concurrent chlamydia and gonorrhoeal infections.

## Trichomonas Vaginalis

Primary infections with trichomonas can be asymptomatic or occur with a green–greyish vaginal discharge and itch.

### Testing

- Vaginal swab for Trichomonas, Gardnerella, Candida (VP3)

## Genital Herpes

Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV) Type II is the most common cause of genital herpes. Primary infections are usually asymptomatic. Secondary infections often present with painful genital ulcers or vesicles, penile or vaginal discharge, fever and lethargy.

Triggers for HSV recurrence can include stress and fatigue. Prodromes can include a tingling, burning sensation at the location of eruption.

# Adolescent Sexual Health

# Testing

- HSV PCR (vaginal/endocervical/penile swab)

# Treatment

Although there is no cure for genital herpes, medications can ease symptoms and reduce the risk of spreading the infection. Please refer to the Appendix II for Drug Doses for more information.

# Genital Warts

Genital Human Papilloma Virus (HPV) is the most common sexually transmitted infection. Most HPV infections are asymptomatic, but can also present as warts, typically located on the penile, scrotal, vulva and perianal region. The diagnosis of genital warts is made clinically.

HPV infections and risk of cervical and other HPV-related cancers can be prevented with HPV vaccination, which can be offered to individuals (both male and female) from the ages of 9 to 26 (see Prevention of STIs).

# Bacterial Vaginosis

Bacterial vaginosis (BV) is a condition resulting from changes in bacterial flora in the vagina, resulting in an increase in pH and a higher concentration of anaerobic bacterial. It can arise and resolve spontaneously in women, regardless of sexual activity. It is characterised by a whitish vaginal discharge with a ‘fishy’ odour and is otherwise often asymptomatic.

# Syphilis

Syphilis is a systemic infection caused by *Treponema pallidum* and is almost exclusively spread by direct contact with infectious lesions. Syphilitic sores are often found around the penile, vaginal, anal, rectal or oral region. These sores can be painless and firm. If syphilis infection is suspected or confirmed, referral to a specialist is recommended.

# Initial Screening

- Treponema Pallidum Antibody (CMIA)

# Viral Infections: HIV, Hepatitis B, Hepatitis C

These blood-borne viruses can also be transmitted through mucous membrane exposures and exchange of genital fluids during sexual intercourse. Infections can be asymptomatic for all three viruses. Hepatitis B and C infections may present as acute hepatitis syndrome with symptoms such as fever, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting and jaundice. HIV infection may present with acute retroviral syndrome with non-specific mononucleosis-like symptoms, such as fever, malaise, lymphadenopathy and skin rash, or diagnosed during a presentation of an opportunistic infection as part of the acquired immunodeficiency syndrome (AIDS).

# Testing

- HIV — HIV antibody screen

# The Baby Bear Book

- Hepatitis B — Hepatitis B immunity profile (HepB surface antigen, HepB surface antibodies) and Hepatitis B Core Total Antibody
- Hepatitis C — Hepatitis C Total Antibody (CMIA)

Treatment is in consultation with paediatric infectious disease physician and gastroenterologist.

## Prevention of STIs

Post-exposure prophylaxis
- Safe sex practices — in particular, barrier methods
Vaccination

Vaccines are available for prevention of some STIs. These include:

1. Hepatitis B vaccine — this vaccine is initially given as part of the National Childhood Immunisation Program. Immunity during adolescence may wane, and repeat vaccination may be required. Hepatitis B surface antibody levels should be measured in sexually active adolescents and is recommended to be above \(10\mathrm{mIU / ml}\).
2. Human Papilloma Virus vaccine — both males and females age 9–26 can be given this vaccine. This vaccine is part of the National Childhood Immunisation Program for females and is given in an HPB-school based program for females aged 13–16 years. Males are not included in the National childhood/adult immunisation program and should only be offered HPV9 if high-risk.

## Contraception

A minor should be informed of the law in relation to sexual activity. In general, an adolescent is strongly encouraged to involve a parent/guardian in decisions regarding contraception in an open, joint decision-making process.

Adolescents should be informed about all methods of contraception, including emergency contraception (EC) and highlighting the benefits of long-acting reversible contraception (LARC), such as the contraceptive implants and intrauterine devices (IUD). The correct and consistent use of condoms should be advised to reduce the risk of transmission of STIs.

If an adolescent is keen to discuss contraceptive options, consulting with the Adolescent Gynaecology team can be helpful in deciding the most appropriate option.

## Adolescent Pregnancy

Worldwide, adolescent pregnancy is a major contributor to maternal and child morbidity and mortality. Pregnant teens are at higher risk of living in poverty, limited employment opportunities, experiencing violence within an intimate relationship, and having daughters whom themselves are at higher risk of teenage pregnancy.

b4854_Seo-05.indd 132

12-Dec-22 10:56:37 AM

# Adolescent Sexual Health

Infant mortality is also significantly higher amongst babies of adolescent mothers than non-adolescent mothers. Infants born to adolescent mothers are at higher risk of numerous medical complications, such as stillbirth, prematurity and low birth weight.

Prevention of unplanned adolescent pregnancies is an increasing global priority for medical and public health professionals. As medical professionals, we can help reduce the risk of adolescent pregnancies through timely identification of adolescents at risk of early sexual activity initiation, and also providing effective sexual health education to this group of youth.

## Management

When a diagnosis of pregnancy has been made, the adolescent should be informed in a prompt and confidential manner. A doctor should discuss with the pregnant teen how and when she plans on sharing the news with her parents/trusted adult, as well as the father of the baby.

Appropriate resources and support should be provided and options available to the pregnant teen should be discussed, taking a caring and non judgemental approach. These options include parenthood, future adoption and termination or pregnancy.

In our institution, single pregnant teens who are less than 21 years of age and decide on keeping their pregnancy should be referred to a dedicated clinic for multidisciplinary care and prenatal follow-up.

## Termination of Pregnancy

Termination of pregnancy (TOP) or abortion is the removal of pregnancy tissue/products of conception/foetus and placenta from the uterus. Some of the reasons why women request for TOP include unplanned pregnancy, contraception failure, having an abnormal foetus and having a medical condition that will endanger the life of the pregnant woman.

## Legal Requirements

There is no defined minimum age for the TOP procedure in Singapore nor is there a legal requirement for parental consent for minors under the age of 16. While parental consent is not required, adolescents should be encouraged to involve their parents or a trusted adult in this decision-making process, unless the adolescent feels that involving a parent in the process would be harmful for their well-being.

Abortion is prohibited after 24 weeks of gestation unless the mother's life is in danger or the pregnant woman is carrying an abnormal foetus that is not compatible with life.

## Process

*Pre-abortion counselling* is mandatory by law for all pregnant women seeking treatment for termination of pregnancy in Singapore. Medical and surgical options can be explored with the adolescent.

An authorised medical practitioner should provide post-abortion counselling to a woman who has had her pregnancy terminated, and it should be done on the day when the abortion procedure is carried out.

Contraception should be discussed and can be started immediately after TOP.

# CHAPTER 19

# Anaphylaxis

Goh Si Hui; Lee May Ping, Samantha; Goh Eng Neo, Anne

## Definition

Anaphylaxis is a severe systemic hypersensitivity reaction. It is commonly IgE-mediated but can also be induced by other immunological mechanisms or direct mast cell activation.

## Diagnosis

Anaphylaxis is a clinical diagnosis. Anaphylaxis is highly likely when *any* **one** of the following two criteria is fulfilled.

1. Sudden onset (minutes to several hours) of an illness with involvement of the skin, mucosal tissue or both (e.g. hives, itching, flushing, swollen lips/tongue/uvula)

And at least one of the following:

Airway/Breathing: Respiratory compromise (e.g. shortness of breath, wheeze, stridor, reduced peak expiratory flow (PEF), hypoxemia).
- Circulation: Reduced blood pressure (BP) or symptoms of end-organ dysfunction (e.g. hypotonia, collapse, syncope, incontinence).
- Other: Severe gastrointestinal symptoms (e.g. severe crampy abdominal pain, repetitive vomiting), especially after exposure to non-food allergens.

2. Acute onset (minutes to several hours) of hypotension or bronchospasm or laryngeal involvement after exposure to a *known or highly probable allergen* for that patient, even in the absence of typical skin involvement.

## Common Causes of Anaphylaxis

In children, the top cause of anaphylaxis is food, followed by drugs and stinging insects.

- Food: Hen's egg, cow's milk, shellfish, peanut and treenuts, wheat.

In tropical climates, orally ingested mite-contaminated wheat flour products can trigger anaphylaxis.

136

b4854_Sec-06.indd 136

12-Dec-22 10:57:00 AM

# Anaphylaxis

137

- Medications: Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs), antibiotics (e.g. beta-lactam)
- Stinging insect: Bees, wasps

Approximately 15% of anaphylaxis is idiopathic after thorough history and investigations.

## Risk Factors and Co-Factors

- Risk factors for severe anaphylaxis are age (infants, adolescents), concomitant asthma, cardiovascular disease, mast cell disease, consumption of beta-blockers and ACE-inhibitors.
- Co-factors that amplify anaphylaxis are exercise, acute infection, emotional stress, sleep deprivation, pre-menstrual status (in females).

## Investigations

### Inpatient Investigations

Anaphylaxis is a clinical diagnosis. Serum tryptase level is not required in the diagnosis of anaphylaxis but can be particularly useful in cases of perioperative anaphylaxis, anaphylaxis of atypical clinical presentation or unknown triggers.

Tryptase is best taken at 1 to 2 hours (peak), and not longer than 6 hours, after the onset of reaction. Blood sample is taken in a plain serum tube. Acute tryptase level is compared to baseline level drawn 24 hours after event. Raised tryptase supports the diagnosis of anaphylaxis, although normal levels *do not* exclude the occurrence of anaphylaxis.

In cases of perioperative anaphylaxis, strict serial time-sensitive sampling of serum tryptase levels is essential (Sample 1: immediately after resuscitation. Sample 2: 1 to 2 hours after event, maximum 6 hours. Sample 3, baseline: 24 hours after event).

### Outpatient Investigations

Skin prick tests (SPT) and specific IgE levels help to identify IgE-mediated triggers. Tests should only be performed at least 4 weeks after anaphylaxis.

# Anaphylaxis

Advice includes how to avoid exposure to likely allergen and how to identify and treat future allergic reactions.

## Adrenaline Autoinjector

Patients at risk of anaphylaxis should be provided with at least 1 adrenaline autoinjector.

These include:

History of anaphylaxis
- Specific foods associated with severe reactions, e.g. nut
- Allergic reaction to trace amounts of allergen
- Allergen is not easily avoided
- Patient with comorbidities, e.g. asthma
- Patients at continuing risk of anaphylaxis, e.g. food-dependent exercise-induced anaphylaxis, idiopathic anaphylaxis
- Limited access to emergency care

Adrenaline autoinjector dose

- EpiPen (adrenaline \(0.3\mathrm{mg}\)) if weight is \(>25\mathrm{kg}\)
- EpiPen Junior (adrenaline \(0.15\mathrm{mg}\)) if weight is \(10 - 25\mathrm{kg}\)
- Or comparable injectable adrenaline product with specific instruction

# CHAPTER 20

# Food Allergy

Chong Kok Wee; Goh Eng Neo, Anne

## Introduction

Food allergy is increasingly common in children, with prevalence of up to 10% reported in Westernised countries. Food allergy can be broadly classified into immediate (IgE mediated), delayed (cell mediated) and mixed (IgE and non-IgE mediated). Food allergies involve an immune response and should not be confused with intolerance (e.g. lactose intolerance), toxic (food poisoning) or pharmacologic (e.g. caffeine) adverse reactions.

## Immediate (IgE mediated) Food Allergy

The most commonly implicated foods are hen's eggs, cow's milk, peanut, tree nuts, shellfish, wheat, fish, soy and seeds, with the highest incidence locally being hen's eggs. Symptoms of an immediate food allergy occur usually within minutes (up to 2 hours). They range from cutaneous symptoms of hives and angioedema (mild and commonest) to acute vomiting/abdominal pain to anaphylaxis (with cardiorespiratory system involvement).

A clinical diagnosis of immediate food allergy can be made with a history consistent with IgE-mediated food allergy (onset, symptomology) coupled with a positive allergy test. When one or the other is missing or equivocal, the diagnosis should be confirmed by an oral food challenge (OFC) conducted in the hospital by trained personnel. A positive skin prick test or food-specific IgE alone only represents sensitisation and does not equate to clinical allergy. An OFC remains the gold standard for diagnosis of immediate food allergy. The use of component resolved diagnostics (notably IgE to Ara h 2 in peanuts) has increased the reliability of predicting OFC outcomes in recent years.

Egg and milk allergies have a high rate of natural resolution in childhood, whilst peanut, tree nuts, shellfish and seeds usually persist into adulthood.

![img-48.jpeg](img-48.jpeg)

Figure 6.2 Diagnosis of immediate food allergy.

140

b4854_Sec-06.indd 140

12-Dec-22 10:57:05 AM

# Food Allergy

The mainstay of management is strict allergen avoidance and counselling on the recognition and treatment of allergic reactions from accidental exposures. Mild to moderate (cutaneous and gastrointestinal symptoms) symptoms should be treated with a second-generation antihistamine while the first-line treatment of anaphylaxis is intra-muscular adrenaline. A dietitian review is invaluable in the education on allergen avoidance as well as assessing the nutritional status, for early and individualised intervention.

Owing to a growing body of evidence, there is a gradual shift of earlier introduction of 'allergenic food products', especially in high risk, atopic children. These strategies for prevention of food allergies can be discussed with the attending paediatric allergist.

Food immunotherapy (desensitisation) used to be largely performed under research settings in academic centres, but it has been gaining traction and is now offered by allergists experienced in food immunotherapy in an increasing number of countries. Discussing the risks and benefits and setting expectations that it is not a cure to food allergy, are paramount in the decision to embark on food immunotherapy.

## Delayed Food Allergy

Delayed food allergy generally presents with gastrointestinal symptoms as it can affect any part of the gastrointestinal tract, ranging from benign allergic proctocolitis to enteropathy and severe enterocolitis.

Table 6.1 Presentations of Delayed Food Allergy.

|   | FPIAP | FPE | FPIES (Acute)  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Age of onset | Days to 6 mth | 2 to 24 mth | Days to 1 yr  |
|  Clinical Presentation | Intermittent bloody stools in otherwise healthy, thriving infants | Chronic/ intermittent diarrhoea/ vomiting/ constipation, faltering growth | Profuse vomiting, pallor, ± lethargy 1–4 hr post food ingestion. May have diarrhoea 5–10 hr post ingestion. 15–20% present in acute shock  |
|  Most common triggers | Cow's milk/ soy through breastmilk or direct ingestion | Cow's milk, soy, egg, wheat | Cow's milk, soy, rice, wheat, fish  |

FPIAP: food protein-induced allergic proctocolitis; FPE: food protein-induced enteropathy; FPIES: food protein-induced enterocolitis syndrome.

The diagnosis of delayed food allergy is based on:

1. Exclusion of other differentials (e.g. infections, gastrointestinal disorders amongst others).
2. Compatible history suggesting a temporal relationship between new food introduction and a delayed onset of symptoms.
3. Trial of strict dietary elimination of the suspect food allergen (under guidance of physician and dietitian), resulting in resolution of symptoms.
4. Recurrence of symptoms with allergen re-introduction.

b4854_Sec-06.indd 141

12-Dec-22 10:57:05 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

Management includes dietary elimination of the offending food, nutritional counselling and support, and regular re-assessments. Treatment of acute FPIES (which may mimic anaphylaxis or septic shock) is aggressive fluid resuscitation. Intra-muscular adrenaline has no role in the treatment of acute FPIES.

The prognosis of FPIAP is good, with most infants achieving tolerance before 1 year of age. The long-term prognosis of the rest of delayed food allergies is also generally good, with most resolving in early childhood.

# CHAPTER 21

# Drug Allergy

Tan Liling, Lynette; Loh Wenyin

## Introduction

An adverse drug reaction (ADR) is defined as any noxious, unintended and undesired effect of a drug that occurs at doses used for prevention, diagnosis or treatment.

Drug hypersensitivity reactions (DHRs) are adverse effects of drugs that clinically resemble allergic reactions. Drug allergies are DHRs for which a definite immunological mechanism is demonstrated.

True DHR is uncommon in the paediatric population. Childhood viral exanthems are often misinterpreted as DHR to various medications, such as antipyretics and antibiotics. It is important to confirm the diagnosis of DHR because reported antibiotic allergies are associated with increased use of broad-spectrum antibiotics, longer hospital stays and increased healthcare costs.

## Evaluation

The goals of evaluation are either to confirm the diagnosis of a DHR and find a safe alternative or to confirm tolerance to the drug and exclude a DHR. A thorough history is essential in the evaluation of patients with suspected drug allergies.

If a drug-induced allergic reaction is suspected, it is important to distinguish between immediate (<1 hour; sometimes up to 6 hours) and non-immediate (>1 hour) reactions in order to decide on subsequent tests and management.

A useful test for detecting IgE-mediated drug reactions caused by large-molecular-weight biologicals and penicillin is the immediate hypersensitivity skin test. A positive skin prick test (SPT) or intradermal test (IDT) strongly suggests DHR in the presence of clinical reaction. A negative skin test, however, does not exclude DHR, and the patient would benefit from a drug provocation test to confirm diagnosis.

Drug provocation tests (DPTs) remain the gold standard for diagnosis of DHR. DPTs should not be performed if the reaction history is consistent with a severe non-IgE-mediated reaction (e.g. SJS/TEN, DRESS, hepatitis, haemolytic anaemia).

## Management

Management would depend on the severity of the reaction. In cases of severe reactions and absence of confirmatory tests, implicated drugs must be avoided.

|  I | IgE | Mast cell and basophil degranulation | Urticaria Angioedema Anaphylaxis | Within 1–6 h after the last intake  |
|  II | IgG and complement | IgG and complement-dependent cytotoxicity | Cytopenia | 5–15 d after the start  |

|  IVc | Cytotoxic T cells | Keratinocyte death mediated by CD4 or CD8 | MPE SJS/TENS Pustular exanthema | 4–28 d after the start for SJS/TENS  |
|  IVd | T cells | Neutrophilic inflammation | AGEP | 1–2 d after the start  |

MPE: maculopapular exanthema; DRESS: drug rash with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms; SJS: Stevens-Johnson syndrome; TENS: Toxic epidermal necrolysis; AGEP: acute generalised exanthematous pustulosis

## Beta-Lactam Hypersensitivity

Beta-lactam antibiotics include penicillin, cephalosporin, carbapenems (e.g. meropenem) and monobactam (e.g. aztreonam). They are the most common cause of drug hypersensitivity reactions in the paediatric population. Clinical history is not reliable as a diagnostic tool, and DPTs remain the gold standard for confirmation or exclusion of beta-lactam hypersensitivity (Figure 6.3). Studies have shown that 90% of children with suspected beta-lactam hypersensitivity do not react at DPT.

Immediate-reading of skin tests (SPT & IDT) may be useful for the evaluation of immediate reactions and delayed-reading of skin tests may be performed for evaluation of non-immediate reactions. However, owing to the low sensitivity of the tests, DPTs may sometimes be performed without prior skin tests, particularly in low risk patients, i.e. mild skin reactions. There is increasing data that this simplified approach is safe in selected patients.

# Drug Allergy

![img-49.jpeg](img-49.jpeg)

Figure 6.3 Algorithm for the diagnosis of hypersensitivity reactions to beta-lactam antibiotics.

SPT: skin prick test; IDT: intradermal test; DPT: drug provocation test; PT: patch test; SCAR: severe cutaneous adverse reaction such as Stevens–Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis, drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms and acute generalised exanthematous pustulosis.

*In vitro tests (e.g. specific IgE assay) can be considered if skin tests are not possible. However, sensitivity may vary depending on various factors and is generally lower than skin tests.
**Mild maculopapular exanthemas (more or less widespread rash; <1 week duration) or delayed urticaria
\*\*\*PT is the investigation of choice in contact dermatitis; they may also be useful in maculopapular exanthemas, flexural exanthemas and fixed drug eruptions.
***Skin testing (PT followed by delayed reading IDT) can be considered to evaluate culprit amongst several used drugs.

While various components of beta-lactams are all potentially immunogenic, cross-reactivity related to the common beta-lactam ring is rare in subjects with an IgE-mediated hypersensitivity and rarely described in those with T-cell mediated hypersensitivity. More frequently, cross-reactivity amongst beta-lactams is related to structural similarities amongst their side chain structures. Studies have shown that in patients with IgE-mediated penicillin allergy, cross-reactivity with similar side chain cephalosporin may be as high as 33.3%, whereas cross-reactivity with different side chain cephalosporins was <0.2%. Also, <1% of patients with IgE-mediated penicillin allergy cross-react with carbapenems and monobactam. This suggests that confirmed allergy to a particular drug does not necessarily extend to avoidance of all beta-lactams and further evaluation is strongly recommended.

# Drug Allergy

# NSAID Hypersensitivity

Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) hypersensitivity can be immune-mediated or as a result of cyclooxygenase (COX) inhibition. A drug provocation test is needed to confirm the diagnosis regardless of the underlying mechanism.

Seven per cent of patients with NSAID hypersensitivity also react to paracetamol.

Patients with cross-reactive NSAID and paracetamol hypersensitivity may benefit from a drug provocation test to a selective COX-2 inhibitor, e.g. celecoxib, etoricoxib.

# Perioperative Immediate Hypersensitivity Reactions

Perioperative immediate hypersensitivity reactions (POH) are one of the most complex clinical problems in the evaluation of drug allergy. The combination of the surgical procedure, simultaneous administration of several drugs and numerous differential diagnoses often make determining the trigger(s) very challenging. Perioperative anaphylaxis in the paediatric population range from 1 in 2100 to 1 in 37000. Neuromuscular blocking agents and antibiotics are often reported as the most common causes of POH. Locally, the incidence of perioperative anaphylaxis is 1 in 2500 with the most common identified trigger being antibiotics. The clinical presentation of POH can vary from mild cutaneous symptoms to life-threatening anaphylaxis. Identifying the culprit drug and finding safe alternatives are imperative for safe future anaesthesia and require a systematic approach by both allergists and anaesthetists with investigations performed in a timely manner. Complete evaluation should include measurement of the dynamic tryptase to diagnose perioperative anaphylaxis as well as skin tests (SPT, IDT) to identify the triggering drug and safe alternatives. Drug provocation tests may be performed if skin tests are negative. Skin tests in patients without a history of a drug reaction are not recommended.

4. Goh SH, Chong KW, Chiang WC, et al. (2021) Outcome of drug provocation testing in children with suspected beta-lactam hypersensitivity. Asia Pac Allergy 11(1):e3.

# CHAPTER 22

# Urticaria

Lee May Ping, Samantha; Ong Lin Xin

## Introduction

Urticaria is a condition characterised by the development of wheals (hives), angioedema or both.

Wheal (hive) consists of three typical features:

- A superficial central swelling of variable size and shape, surrounded by reflex erythema
An itching or sometimes burning sensation
- A fleeting nature, with the skin returning to its normal appearance, usually within 30 minutes to 24 hours

Angioedema is characterised by:

- A sudden, pronounced swelling of the lower dermis and subcutis or mucous membranes
- Tingling, burning, tightness and sometimes painful, rather than itchy
- Slower resolution than that of wheals (can take up to 72 hours)

## Acute Urticaria

The most common cause of acute urticaria in children is infection (especially viral), followed by food allergy, drug allergy or idiopathic/spontaneous. Other possible causes include insect stings, latex allergy, plant or animal contacts. Acute urticaria is usually self-limiting. Diagnosis can be made from a detailed clinical history and physical examination and does not usually require any investigations. The only exception is when it is due to IgE-mediated food or drug allergy, where referral to an allergist for further work up is necessary.

Table 6.3 Classification of Urticaria

|  Types | Definition  |
| --- | --- |
|  ACUTE | Appearance of wheals, angioedema or both, for ≤6 wk duration  |
|  CHRONIC | Appearance of wheals, angioedema or both, daily or most days of the week, for > 6 wk duration  |

148

b4854_Sec-06.indd 148

12-Dec-22 10:57:11 AM

# Urticaria Subtypes

## Chronic Urticaria

Chronic urticaria typically occurs spontaneously and the cause is usually unknown. It is self-limiting but may persist for many years in some children. Further investigations are usually not necessary and should only be reserved for those with protracted or refractory disease. All patients with chronic urticaria should be followed up by an allergist or dermatologist.

## Differential Diagnoses of Chronic Urticaria

- Only wheals (hives):

Urticarial vasculitis
Cutaneous mastocytosis
» Autoimmune conditions (i.e. systemic lupus erythematosus, systemic onset juvenile idiopathic arthritis)
» Autoinflammatory syndromes (i.e. Schnitzler's syndrome, Cryopyrin-associated periodic syndrome)

- Only angioedema:

Hereditary angioedema (HAE)
» ACE-inhibitor induced angioedema

Note: To rule out other medical causes of oedema (i.e. cardiovascular/renal/hepatic/lymphatic/malnutrition).

## Management

The management of urticaria consists of two important approaches. First, the identification and elimination of the underlying causes and avoidance of eliciting triggers (if any). Second, treatment is aimed at providing symptomatic relief. The recommended first-line treatment is with second generation, non-sedating H₁-antihistamines (i.e. cetirizine, levocetirizine, desloratadine, loratadine, fexofenadine, rupatadine, bilastine). Higher

|  • Spontaneous appearance of wheals, angioedema or both for ≤6 wk due to unknown cause | • Appearance of wheals, angioedema or both for > 6 wk, induced by physical stimuli » Symptomatic dermographism* » Cholinergic urticaria » Delayed pressure urticaria » Heat urticaria » Cold urticaria » Solar urticaria » Vibratory angioedema » Aquagenic urticaria • Can coexist with chronic spontaneous urticaria *commonest cause of CIndU in children  |

At present, there is no role for oral corticosteroids in the management of urticaria and is not recommended except for acute severe exacerbation of chronic urticaria.

# CHAPTER 23

# Primary Immunodeficiencies (Inborn Errors of Immunity)

Chan Su-Wan, Bianca; Liew Woei Kang

## Introduction

Primary Immunodeficiencies (PIDs), now known as Inborn Errors of Immunity (IEI), are a heterogeneous group of more than 400 distinct disorders that impair the normal functioning of the immune system. IEI most commonly manifest as an increased susceptibility to infectious diseases, but they can also present with autoimmunity, autoinflammation, allergies or malignancies. Whilst most IEI are individually rare, they are collectively more common than generally thought, with an estimated prevalence of 1 in every 1200 to 2000 individuals. Most IEI have a well-defined genetic cause, and new genetic disorders causing immune defects are discovered each year.

IEI can be classified into the following 10 groups based on the International Union of Immunological Societies (IUIS) Classification.

Table 6.5 Classification of Inborn Errors of Immunity

|  International Union of Immunological Societies (IUIS) Classification  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|   | Group | Example  |
|  1 | Combined immunodeficiencies (CID) | Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)  |
|  2 | Combined immunodeficiencies with syndromic features | Wiskott Aldrich Syndrome (WAS)  |
|  3 | Predominantly antibody deficiencies | Bruton's Agammaglobulinaemia  |
|  4 | Diseases of immune dysregulation | Familial haemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis  |
|  5 | Congenital defects of phagocytes | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD)  |
|  6 | Defects in intrinsic and innate immunity | Mendelian Susceptibility to Mycobacterial Disease (MSMD)  |
|  7 | Autoinflammatory diseases | Familial Mediterranean Fever  |
|  8 | Complement deficiencies | Properdin deficiency  |
|  9 | Bone marrow failure | Dyskeratosis Congenita  |
|  10 | Phenocopies of inborn errors of immunity | Thymoma with hypogammaglobulinemia (Good syndrome)  |

# The Baby Bear Book

## When to Evaluate for Inborn Errors of Immunity?

Although healthy children may have frequent, monthly self-limiting upper respiratory tract infections each year, children with uncharacteristically recurrent, severe or unusual infections should be evaluated for an underlying IEI.

Common presentations include:

- Recurrent bacterial sinopulmonary infections \(\rightarrow\) antibody deficiency
- Invasive infections, poor wound healing \(\rightarrow\) phagocyte defects
- Severe or disseminated mycobacterial infection \(\rightarrow\) MSMD, CGD, SCID
- Severe fungal or viral infections \(\rightarrow\) SCID, combined immunodeficiency
- Recurrent, severe or unusual herpesvirus infection \(\rightarrow\) NK cell defect
- Severe Neisseria infection \(\rightarrow\) complement defect
- Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis \(\rightarrow\) STAT1 or IL17 pathway defect

## Red Flags for Inborn Errors of Immunity

*(Adapted from Jeffery Modell Foundation: 10 Warning signs of PID)*

Table 6.6 Red Flags for Inborn Errors of Immunity

|  1 | Family history | Positive for early unexplained death, recurrent or severe infections, or specific immunodeficiency diagnoses. Consanguinity, autoimmunity.  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  2 | Frequent infections | Elevated frequency of documented infections including •Four or more new ear infections within 1 yr. •Two or more serious sinus infections within 1 yr. •Two or more pneumonias within 1 yr.  |
|  3 | Chronic infection | Persistent sinusitis, otitis media, bronchiectasis, recurrent abscess  |
|  4 | Severe infection | Bacteraemia, osteomyelitis, meningitis, especially if recurrent  |
|  5 | Complications of infection | Cerebral abscess complicating sinusitis, acute respiratory distress syndrome from common respiratory viruses  |
|  6 | Site of infection | Unusual sites, e.g. liver or brain abscess  |
|  7 | Infecting organism | Opportunistic, recurrent or unusual pathogens, e.g. *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Nocardia*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, post-natal severe cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection, disseminated mycobacterium infection  |
|  8 | Response to therapy | Poor response or recurring infection after antimicrobial discontinuation or needing IV antibiotics to clear infection  |
|  9 | Other signs | Failure to thrive, severe dermatitis, recurrent diarrhoea with or without colitis, autoimmunity  |

# Primary Immunodeficiencies (Inborn Errors of Immunity)

Children with IEI can also present with non-infectious issues:

- Very early onset inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) (<6 years old)
- Severe eczema (neonatal onset, resistant to therapy, complicated by severe infection)
- Dermatologic manifestations (ectodermal dysplasia, neutrophilic pustular dermatosis, cutaneous granulomas, abnormal skin/hair pigmentation, cold-induced urticaria)
- Early onset autoimmune diseases
- Early onset endocrinopathies
Haemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis
Lymphoproliferative disorders
- Syndromic features (skeletal dysplasia, DiGeorge, ataxia, developmental delay)
- Pulmonary manifestations (pulmonary alveolar proteinosis, pulmonary capillaritis)

## How to Evaluate for Inborn Errors of Immunity?

The selection of immunologic tests must be guided by clinical suspicion based on a thorough history and physical examination. A good infection history including sites and microorganisms involved and treatment required, as well as a detailed family history of early childhood deaths, severe infections, autoimmunity and involvement of male relatives, should be obtained. Previous medical records, growth charts and blood counts should be reviewed. A full blood count with differential, immunoglobulin IgG, IgA, IgM and IgE levels, and a human immunodeficiency virus (HIV) screen to rule out secondary causes, would be the first step in the immune evaluation and would help guide subsequent testing.

Table 6.7 Immune Testing

|   | Quality | Function | Pattern of Illness  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Antibody defects [e.g. X-linked Bruton's Agammaglobulinaemia, Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID)] *also check for asplenia | • B lymphocyte number (CD19 or CD20) • Naïve/Memory B cells • Immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) • IgG subclass levels | • Class-switching IgM → IgG, IgA • Specific antibodies - to vaccines (e.g. Tetanus, HIB, Pneumococcal, Measles, Varicella) - to previous infections | • Sinopulmonary infection (pyogenic, encapsulated bacteria) • Severe gastrointestinal (Enteroviruses, Giardia lamblia) • Autoimmunity (cytopenia, IBD)  |
|  Cell-mediated immunity e.g. DiGeorge *also check HIV serology | • Absolute Lymphocyte Counts • T-cell lymphocyte subsets: Total CD3, Helper CD4, Cytotoxic CD8 • Naïve (CD45RA) and Memory (CD45RO) T cells | • Lymphocyte proliferation to mitogens — Phytohaemagglutinin (PHA), Concaulin A (ConA), Anti-CD3 + Anti-CD28 | • Pneumonia/Pneumonitis (pyogenic bacteria, fungi, mycobacteria viruses) • Severe viral infections (CMV) • Gastroenteritis (viruses, Giardia sp.) • Dermatitis  |

# Genetic Testing for Disease Confirmation/Search for Cause of Immune Abnormalities

1. Next Generation Sequencing using PID Panels — higher sensitivity for targeted genes
2. Whole Exome/Whole Genome Sequencing — more comprehensive if gene unknown

# Important Considerations — Possible Secondary Causes of Immune Deficiency to Exclude

1. Medications (e.g. anti-epileptic, chemotherapeutic, immune-modulatory, steroids)
2. Physiologic losses of immunoglobulins/lymphocytes (e.g. enteropathy, chylothorax)

# Primary Immunodeficiencies (Inborn Errors of Immunity)

155

3. Nutritional deficits and excess, physiologic and psychosocial stress
4. Infection-induced immune suppression (HIV, CMV, sepsis)
5. Anatomical abnormalities (e.g. congenital lung defects, perianal fistulas, ciliary dyskinesia)
6. Atopy, breakdown of skin/mucosal barriers (e.g. poorly controlled eczema, indwelling catheters)
7. Prematurity
8. Other underlying disease conditions

## Approach to Recurrent Infections

# 1. Recurrent lower respiratory tract infections or bronchiectasis

a. Consider impaired respiratory barrier like cystic fibrosis, primary ciliary dyskinesia, congenital cystic adenomatoid malformation (CCAM)
b. Aspiration pneumonia/gastroesophageal reflux (GER)/swallowing dysfunction
c. Asthma
d. Chronic bacteria bronchitis
e. Inborn Error of Immunity:

i. Antibody defect, Phagocytic defect, Hyper-IgE syndrome, Combined immunodeficiency, complement deficiency

f. Immunology Tests:

i. FBC, Immunoglobulins IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE, Complements C3, C4, CH50
ii. Vaccine antibodies
iii. T/B/NK lymphocyte subsets if viral/fungal/tuberculosis (TB)

# 2. Recurrent skin abscesses

a. Consider impaired skin barrier like atopic dermatitis, ichthyosis, burns, percutaneous catheter
b. If perianal abscess, consider perianal fistula of Crohn's disease, perianal dermatitis from allergic enteropathy
c. Staphylococcus aureus skin colonisation and auto-inoculation
d. Inborn Errors of Immunity:

i. Phagocytes disorders, e.g. Congenital neutropenia, chronic granulomatous disease, leukocyte adhesion defect, Hyper-IgE syndrome, T cell lymphopenia

e. Immunology Tests:

i. FBC, Immunoglobulins IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE, Neutrophil oxidation test, e.g. NBT/DHR, nasal/skin swab culture, T Lymphocyte subsets

## Important Inborn Errors of Immunity

# 1. Bruton's Agammaglobulinaemia (X-linked Agammaglobulinaemia)

a. Males, Xq21.3-22, familial
b. Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, bronchiectasis
c. Bacteria (Streptococcus, Pseudomonas), Severe Enterovirus

b4854_Sec-06.indd 155

12-Dec-22 10:57:11 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

d. No palpable lymph nodes or tonsillar tissues
e. Immunology tests

i. All Immunoglobulin classes \(< 2\) SDs for age
ii. Lymphocyte subsets \(< 2\%\) CD19 or CD20 B cells
iii. Absent isohaemagglutinins, poor response to vaccines
iv. Genetics—mutations in BTK tyrosine kinase

f. Treatment with lifelong immunoglobulin replacement

# 2. Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID)

a. Paediatric Emergency
b. Early presentation — average age at diagnosis \(< 6\) months old
c. Family history of early infant death from infections
d. Most frequent manifestations:

i. Interstitial pneumonitis — common respiratory viruses/CMV/ pneumocystis pneumonia (PCP)
ii. Disseminated Mycobacterium bovis infection
iii. Oral candidiasis
iv. Persistent diarrhoea
v. Failure to thrive

e. Immunology tests

i. Immunoglobulin classes \(< 2\) SDs for age
ii. Absent or very low T Lymphocyte subsets CD \(3 < 300\)
iii. Severely reduced T-cell Receptor Excision Circles (TREC) and Naive T cells
iv. B and NK lymphocyte subsets affected depending on SCID genetics
v. Lymphocyte proliferation to mitogens less than \(10\%\) healthy control
vi. Genetics \(\rightarrow 15\) known SCID gene mutations

f. Definitive treatment by haematopoietic stem cell transplant or gene therapy

# 3. Chronic Granulomatous disease

a. Hallmark is early onset of severe recurrent bacterial and fungal infections
b. Skin infections; pneumonia/lung abscesses; suppurative lymphadenitis; diarrhoea secondary to colitis; perianal or perirectal abscesses; hepatic or splenic abscesses; osteomyelitis, septicaemia
c. Catalase positive organism: Staphylococcus aureus, Burkholderia cepacia, Aspergillus sp, Nocardia sp and Serratia marcescens
d. Development of non-infective granulomas in the skin, lung, gastrointestinal or genitourinary tract
e. Immunology tests

i. Neutrophil oxidation tests — NBT or DHR
ii. Genetics—X-linked, Autosomal recessive subtypes

f. Treatment by haematopoietic stem cell transplant, interferon gamma, fungal/ TB prophylaxis

# Primary Immunodeficiencies (Inborn Errors of Immunity)

# Principles of Management of IEI

1. Treat active infection — obtain samples for microbiology, culture-directed antimicrobials
2. Prevent new infections — prophylactic antibiotics, vaccinations, environmental preventive measures, intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG), if needed
3. Replace deficient components, if possible — IVIG, enzyme replacement for ADA
4. Immunomodulation of inflammation with small molecule inhibitors (e.g. JAK inhibitors), biologics (e.g. Rituximab) or immunosuppressive medications (e.g. steroids)
5. Haemotopoietic Stem Cell Transplant if curative (may not resolve nonhaematopoietic involvement of gastrointestinal tract, skin, etc.)

# Bibliography

1. Chinn IK, Orange JS. (2019) Immunodeficiency disorders. Pediatr Rev 40(5) 229-242.

2. Tangye SG, Al-Herz W, Bousfiha A, et al. (2020) Human inborn errors of immunity: 2019 update on the Classification from the International Union of Immunological Societies Expert Committee. J Clin Immunol 40:24-64.

# CHAPTER 24

# Chest Pain

Tan Teng Hong

## Introduction

Chest pain in children is least likely to be cardiac in origin, and often idiopathic (23–45%). It is also rarely associated with life-threatening disease in the paediatric population.

Patients younger than 12 years are more likely to have a cardiorespiratory aetiology of their chest pain compared with older children, who are more likely to have a psychogenic cause. Female patients are more likely to be diagnosed with psychogenic chest pain or costochondritis. Recurrent symptoms occur in 45–69% of patients; up to 19% may have symptoms lasting for >3 years.

## History

Table 7.1 Pertinent History in Paediatric Chest Pain

|  Nature of chest pain | Time of onset and events leading to onset of pain, duration, frequency, nature, intensity, location, radiation, precipitating and relieving factors (including relationship to meals and posture), impact of pain on child’s lifestyle and activity level, history of trauma.  |
| --- | --- |
|  Systems assessment | Symptoms of chronic disease, e.g. fever, malaise, fatigue, weight loss, night sweats.  |
|  Past medical and surgical history | In particular, any cardiac surgery or disease; previous evaluation, treatment and diagnoses in patients with recurrent chest pain.  |
|  Medications |   |
|  Family history | In particular of syncope, sudden death or cardiovascular disease.  |
|  Social history | Smoking, alcohol or illicit drugs, recent family or peer problems, school performance, including sports and physical fitness, etc.  |

## Physical Examination

A thorough and complete physical examination is fundamental to an accurate diagnosis of chest pain. In particular:

Vital signs including blood pressure (BP)
- General appearance, i.e. cyanotic, distressed, anxious, sweaty, poor perfusion
Evidence of trauma (e.g. bruising)
- Rash or joint swelling (collagen vascular disease)

Abdominal examination (hepatomegaly, tenderness)

Table 7.2 Red Flags in Chest Pain

|  Cardiac | - Underlying congenital or acquired heart disease - Arrhythmias - Crushing sternal chest pain with or without radiation to left arm or neck - Exercise-induced chest pain - Persistent tachycardia - Persistent hypertension - Hypotension - Gallop rhythm - Syncope  |
| --- | --- |
|  Respiratory | - Haemoptysis - Dyspnoea - Rales - Cyanosis  |

## Differential Diagnoses

1. Musculoskeletal (very common in paediatric patients; pain is sharp, nagging and localised but may radiate)

- Costochondritis — mild to severe, localised, reproducible on palpation and coughing; may have a history of recent upper respiratory tract infection (URTI)
Muscle strain
Trauma
Non-accidental injury (child abuse) — especially in younger children
- Fibromyalgia — pain is continuous and lasts longer than 3 months; tenderness may be elicited over multiple points

# The Baby Bear Book

- Precordial Catch or Texidor's Twinge — sharp pain occurring at rest, usually over the cardiac apex or left lower sternal edge; short duration; frequency varies; worsens on deep inspiration
- Tietze's Syndrome — observed after minor trauma; localised, moderate and associated with visible swelling; swelling may remit and recur; associated with an elevated Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate (ESR)
- Slipping Rib Syndrome — occurs when the eighth, ninth or tenth rib overrides the rib above; onset sudden, may radiate to chest or abdomen
- Xiphoid-Cartilage Syndrome — occurs after vigorous running; pain or 'stitch' at insertion of xiphoid into the abdominal musculature; pressure on xiphoid may reproduce the pain
- Others: Tumours, connective tissue disorders, acute chest syndrome in sickle cell anaemia

# 2. Cardiac

- Congenital heart disease, in particular:

Conditions with left ventricular outflow tract obstruction, e.g. aortic stenosis, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM)
Mitral valve prolapse — chest pain probably results from papillary muscle dysfunction and subendocardial ischaemia
Coronary artery anomalies

- Acquired lesions — cardiomyopathy, endocarditis, myocarditis, acute myocardial infarction (AMI), rheumatic fever, accelerated atherosclerotic coronary artery disease, dissecting aneurysm, pericarditis, Kawasaki Disease
- Arrhythmias — atrial fibrillation (AF), atrial flutter, supraventricular tachycardia (SVT), ventricular tachycardia (VT)

# 3. Respiratory

Pneumothorax
Pneumonia
- Pleural effusion
- Pulmonary embolism — especially in adolescent females on oral contraceptives
Foreign body inhalation
- Chronic cough
- Chronic pulmonary diseases, e.g. cystic fibrosis, chronic asthma (with exercise-induced symptoms)

# 4. Gastrointestinal

- Oesophagitis
- Mallory-Weiss tear
Gastroesophageal reflux
Hiatus hernia
- Referred pain from gastritis, peptic ulcer disease, cholecystitis, pancreatitis
Foreign body ingestion

# 5. Psychogenic

- Hyperventilation syndrome
- Somatoform disorder
- Stress

# 6. Others

- Breast disease — puberty, menstruation, pregnancy, mastitis may cause pain in females
Mediastinal tumours

Herpes zoster - intercostal neuralgia pain may occur days before appearance of vesicles
- Cigarette-smoking (with or without chronic cough), cocaine use (palpitations, coronary vasospasm, AMI, caffeine overdose, accidental ingestion or inhaled mercury vapour and trichloroethane aerosol)

## Major Threats to Life

- Myocardial ischaemia or infarction
Pericarditis with tamponade
Aortic dissection or rupture
- Pneumothorax, pneumomediastinum or pneumopericardium
Pulmonary embolus
Perforated or haemorrhaging peptic ulcer

## Initial Investigations

- Electrocardiogram (ECG)

Arrhythmias: SVTs, AF, atrial flutter, VTs
Acute ischaemic changes: ST segment depression or elevation, symmetrical T wave inversion or tall, pointed, upright T waves; rarely Q waves
HCM: Left or biventricular hypertrophy
Pericarditis: Decreased QRS voltages, generalised ST segment elevation

- Chest radiograph (CXR)

To exclude pulmonary causes of chest pain, e.g. pneumothorax, pneumonia, pleural effusion
If pulmonary congestion or cardiomegaly present, a thorough cardiac workup is required
Others: Rib abnormalities, fractures, markedly dilated aorta (aortic dissection in patient with Marfan syndrome)

## Specific Investigations, If Indicated

- Cardiac: ECG, Holter monitor, exercise stress test, cardiac enzymes (CK, CKMB, Troponin)
- Pulmonary: Computed Tomography (CT) chest, Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) chest, ventilation-perfusion (VQ) scan, lung function test with and without methacholine challenge, bronchoscopy

- Musculoskeletal: Skeletal radiographs, CT spine, MRI spine, nuclear bone scan

## Indications for Admission

- Suspected coronary artery disease, pleural effusion, myocarditis, pericarditis or aortic dissection.
- Severe chest pain of unknown etiology.

## Indications for Referral to Paediatric Cardiologist

- Severe recurrent chest pain
- Associated with sinister symptoms, e.g. syncope, abnormal heart rhythm (with or without exercise)
- Chest pain during and immediately after exercise, and not clearly musculoskeletal
- Suspicious of congenital or acquired heart disease
Abnormal cardiovascular examination (e.g. murmur)
Abnormal initial investigations (ECG, CXR)

# CHAPTER 25

# Syncope

Chan Jiahui, Charmaine; Tan Teng Hong

## Introduction

Syncope is defined as an abrupt, transient loss of consciousness with the inability to maintain postural tone, followed by a rapid and spontaneous complete recovery. It is presumed to be due to transient global cerebral hypoperfusion. It does not include seizures, shock, coma or other states of altered consciousness, which may be prolonged in duration or due to a mechanism other than cerebral hypoperfusion. Up to 15% of children experience at least one episode of syncope by the end of adolescence.

Thorough evaluation of syncope is warranted to establish the aetiology of syncope with enough confidence to provide a reliable assessment of prognosis, recurrence risk and treatment options. The workup of syncope includes a meticulous history, a detailed physical examination, an electrocardiogram (ECG), risk stratification and appropriately directed investigations.

The age of the patient gives clues to the most likely cause of syncope. Infants should always be aggressively investigated for the cause of syncope, while in adolescents, the most likely causes are vasovagal syncope or orthostatic hypotension.

## Causes of Syncope

### Life-Threatening Causes

# 1. Cardiac syncope

- Most important cause of syncope to exclude.
- Syncope due to bradycardia, tachycardia or hypotension from low cardiac output, blood flow obstruction, vasodilation or acute vascular dissection.
Arrhythmias

Tachyarrhythmias: Wolff-Parkinson-White (WPW), catecholaminergic polymorphic ventricular tachycardia (VT), idiopathic VT/ventricular fibrillation (VF), arrhythmogenic right ventricular cardiomyopathy (ARVC)
» Bradyarrhythmias: Atrioventricular (AV) conduction disorder, sinus node dysfunction
» Ion channel abnormalities: Long QT syndrome (LQTS), Brugada syndrome

- Structural heart disease

Coronary artery disease
» Outflow tract obstruction: E.g. severe aortic stenosis, severe pulmonary stenosis

165

b4854_Sec-07.indd 165

12-Dec-22 3:15:32 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy
Dilated cardiomyopathy
Myocarditis
Aortic dissection
Left atrial myxoma
» Prosthetic valve dysfunction
» Severe pulmonary hypertension

2. Heat stroke

3. Anaphylaxis

## Reflex Syncope (Neutrally Mediated)

- This is due to increased parasympathetic and reduced sympathetic activity, causing vasodilation, bradycardia or both.
- In situations such as standing, venous pooling results in reduced venous return to the heart. The heart may compensate with vigorous cardiac contraction, and it is postulated that this results in increased vagal tone.

1. Vasovagal syncope ('common faint')

- Most common cause of syncope in children.
- Triggers can usually be identified, such as stress or pain.
- Associated with sweating, pallor, nausea or warmth.

2. Situational syncope

- Reflex syncope associated with a specific action, e.g. micturition, defecation, coughing, sneezing, post-exercise, post-prandial, weight lifting or playing brass instrument.

3. Carotid sinus syncope

- Uncommon in children; due to carotid sinus hyperactivity, resulting in sinus pause and hypotension.

## Orthostatic Hypotension

- Syncope from postural change, related to an abrupt drop in blood pressure (BP).
- Defined as a drop in systolic BP \(\geq 20\mathrm{mmHg}\) or diastolic BP \(\geq 10\mathrm{mmHg}\) when standing from a sitting or lying position.
- May be caused by volume depletion, anaemia, pregnancy, drugs (e.g. calcium channel blockers, vasodilators, diuretics).

## Other Causes of Syncope

- Metabolic: Hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia, hypomagnesaemia, hypoxia, imbalances in the sodium, potassium or chloride levels.
- Drugs

## Differential Diagnoses and Mimics for Syncope

- Breath-holding spells

# Syncope

167

- Neurology: Seizure, migraine
- Psychiatric: Conversion disorder, hyperventilation, malingering

## History Taking

It is the most important tool for diagnosis and risk stratification in the evaluation of syncope. The patient, and any eye witnesses, should be asked about the event.

**Presyncope:** Make note of any prodrome (e.g. nausea, light-headedness, darkening of vision, palpitations, chest pain), triggers (e.g. pain, emotional stress), situations in which the syncope occurred (exercise-induced, postural-related, acute arousal or loud noise).

**Syncope:** Duration, circumstances of the event, whether there was total loss of consciousness, any seizure-like activity

**Post-syncope:** Symptoms such as fatigue, dizziness, urine or faecal incontinence, time to recovery

## Background Medical History

- History of congenital heart disease or post-cardiac surgery, or primary arrhythmias
History of syncope/near syncope; history of near drowning
Metabolic disorder
- Medications/drugs
- Family history of sudden death, arrhythmias, congenital deafness, pacemakers, implantable cardioverter-defibrillators
- Need to consider conditions such as hypertrophic or dilated cardiomyopathies, long QT syndrome, Marfan syndrome, mitral valve prolapse, etc.

## Physical Examination

- Vital signs — pulse rate and blood pressure, significant postural hypotension. Syncope and tachycardia on standing up is an indicator of orthostasis and volume depletion.
- Cardiovascular evaluation: Structural cardiac lesions or rhythm abnormalities. Note any clubbing or cyanosis.
Full neurological evaluation.

## Investigations

Full blood count
- Renal panel and electrolytes, serum glucose
- Urine pregnancy test (female adolescents)

b4854_Sec-07.indd 167

12-Dec-22 3:15:32 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Arterial blood gases if hypoxia suspected
- ECG — mandatory

## Abnormal ECG Findings

Bradyarrhythmia with sinus pauses, high grade atrioventricular (AV) conduction block, ventricular tachyarrhythmia, prolonged QT, delta waves, signs of Brugada syndrome, Epsilon wave (characteristic of ARVC), signs of left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH), signs of right ventricular hypertrophy (RVH), signs of myocardial ischaemia.

## Further Tests

If a cardiac cause is suspected, Holter monitoring, echocardiography, transtelephonic ECG, cardiac catheterisation and electrophysiological studies might be indicated. Exercise stress testing is indicated in exercise-induced syncope to exclude provocable ischaemia, chronotropic incompetence or ventricular tachycardia.

If a neurological cause is suspected, may require further investigations with electroencephalogram (EEG), neuroimaging. Psychiatric evaluation may be necessary for conversion disorder or hyperventilation.

Reflex syncope can be diagnosed quite confidently by history alone, with normal physical examination and ECG. Tilt table test may be done in cases of recurrent syncope where reflex syncope is suspected.

## Management

### Indications for Admission/Further Evaluation

- Red flags for cardiac cause of syncope (see Table 7.3)
- Persistent abnormal neurological findings
- Orthostatic hypotension with continued postural syncope that does not resolve with fluid therapy

Table 7.3 Characteristics with Increased Likelihood of Cardiac and Non-Cardiac Causes of Syncope

|  More likely cardiac cause | More likely non cardiac cause  |
| --- | --- |
|  Prodrome: Brief or none; complain of palpitations or chest pain | Prodrome: Lightheaded, feeling warm, nausea, darkening of vision  |
|  Triggers: Loud noise (LQTS) | Triggers: Pain, dehydration, micturition, defecation, cough, laugh  |
|  Syncope on exertion Syncope in supine position | Syncope only in standing position Syncope on positional change  |
|  History of cardiac condition | No history of cardiac condition  |
|  Abnormal cardiac examination/ECG | Normal cardiac examination/ECG  |
|  Positive family history of cardiac condition or sudden death (<50 yr old) | No significant family history  |
