# Syncope

## Management of Vasovagal Syncope

- Education about diagnosis, avoidance of triggers (e.g. crowded areas, dehydration), early identification of prodrome, manoeuvres to prevent syncope (e.g. leg crossing, squatting, handgrip and arm tensing)
- Ensure adequate fluid and salt intake
- Medications (seldom required):

I) Midodrine: A prodrug that is metabolised to desglymidodrine, a peripherally mediated alpha agonist, to ameliorate the reduction in peripheral sympathetic neural outflow responsible for venous pooling and vasodepression in vasovagal syncope. It is reasonable to be trialled in patients with recurrent vasovagal syncope with no history of hypertension, heart failure or urinary retention. (Class IIa recommendations.)

II) Fludrocortisone, beta blockers have not been shown to be effective in paediatric trials on treatment for vasovagal syncope.
III) Other drugs, e.g. disopyramide, theophylline, serotonin reuptake inhibitors have been tried.

- Cardiac pacing — has been used in patients with malignant neurocardiogenic syncope (recurrent syncope with significant injury or drug refractory syncope) and documented spontaneous cardio-inhibitory response during monitoring.

## Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome (POTS)

- This is a clinical syndrome characterised by all of the following:

1) Frequent symptoms on standing (e.g. light-headedness, palpitations, tremulousness, fatigue, weakness, blurred vision)
2) Increase in heart rate \(\geq 30 / \mathrm{min}\) from supine to standing (or \(\geq 40 / \mathrm{min}\) in 12 to 19 years old)
3) Absence of orthostatic hypotension

- No actual syncope; tends to present from 15 to 25 years old, mostly in females.
- Chronic condition with low quality of life reported.
Management:

Thorough history and physical examination, and 12-lead ECG
» Select patients may benefit from doing a full blood count, thyroid function test, 24 h Holter, echocardiography and exercise stress testing
Multidisciplinary approach
Regular structured and progressive exercise program
» Acute decompensation from dehydration: May benefit from short-term IV drip
» Medications: Consider fludrocortisone, midodrine, low dose propranolol (Class IIb recommendations)

# CHAPTER 26

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

Chan Jiahui, Charmaine; Tan Teng Hong

## General Principles

- Symptoms may vary depending on the age of the patient. A young child may complain of stomach or chest pain. Older children will be able to give a history of palpitations.
- Urgency of the work-up depends on symptom severity. Children with a history of congenital heart disease or syncope need prompt investigation.
- Obtain a 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) with a long rhythm strip for any suspected arrhythmia before making a diagnosis or instituting management.
- In an acute arrhythmia with haemodynamic compromise, a good rule of thumb is to cardiovert for a tachyarrhythmia and resuscitate for bradyarrhythmia.
- In the paediatric population, primary arrhythmias tends to be the result rather than cause of emergencies.
Children at risk of primary cardiac arrhythmias include those with myocarditis, cardiomyopathy, post-cardiac surgery, congenital heart disease and drug ingestion.
- Urgently treat unstable rhythms — those that compromise cardiac output and those with potential to deteriorate into a lethal rhythm.
- If the arrhythmia is stable, there is time to call the cardiologist to assess patient and advise on treatment.
- Treating a stable patient with an arrhythmia might not only be unnecessary but may also worsen the situation.

## Benign Arrhythmias

### Sinus Arrhythmia

- This is a normal physiological phenomenon and occurs due to reflex changes in vagal tone during the different stages of respiration — heart rate increases during inspiration and decreases during expiration.
- ECG characteristics:

» Variation in PP interval with gradual lengthening and shortening in a cyclical fashion, usually corresponding to the phases of the respiratory cycle.
» Normal sinus P wave with no change in P wave morphology.
Constant PR interval.

171

12-Dec-22 3:15:32 PM



# The Baby Bear Book

![img-51.jpeg](img-51.jpeg)

Figure 7.1 Sinus arrhythmia.

- Management: Does not require further evaluation.

## Wandering Atrial Rhythm (Wandering Atrial Pacemaker)

Commonly seen in \(25 - 35\%\) of children, especially during lower heart rate.
- Occurs when there are three or more ectopic foci within the atrial myocardium serving as the dominant pacemaker. Results in an irregularly irregular rhythm.
- ECG characteristics:

- $\geq 3$ distinctively different P wave morphologies

- Ventricular rate not tachycardic for age

- Management: Does not require further evaluation. If heart rate is tachycardic, this is called multifocal atrial tachycardia, which will require further evaluation and treatment.

![img-52.jpeg](img-52.jpeg)

Figure 7.2 Wandering atrial rhythm. Arrowheads indicate position of P waves. Note varying P wave morphology and PP intervals.

## Junctional Rhythm

- Can be seen intermittently in sinus arrhythmia, especially during periods of low heart rate. It may be a normal phenomenon in children. Occurs when the electrical activity of the sinoatrial (SA) node is blocked or slower than the automaticity of the atrioventricular (AV) node/His bundle.
- ECG characteristics:

Narrow QRS complex with regular rhythm
Rate 40-100/min (varies with age)

- P waves — frequently not seen. If seen, P wave morphology will be different from that originating from the SA node. It is often seen as inverted in lead II.
- Atrioventricular dissociation may be seen, with QRS rate \(>\mathrm{P}\) (atrial) rate.

![img-53.jpeg](img-53.jpeg)

Figure 7.3 Junctional rhythm in an asymptomatic boy. Note the absence of a P wave preceding each QRS complex. The rate here is about 60/min.

b4854_Sec-07.indd 172

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

- Management: Further evaluation is indicated if the rate is too fast for an escape rhythm, e.g. Junctional Ectopic Tachycardia (JET; rate tachycardic for age), or if it is an escape rhythm due to a diseased SA node, or AV conduction block.

## Premature Atrial Contractions (PACs)

- Generally, benign and frequently seen in normal, asymptomatic children. Some patients may complain of palpitations or skipped beats.
- Due to ectopic pacemaker sites in the atria not from the SA node.
- ECG characteristics:

Premature P wave (PP interval is shorter than preceding PP interval). P waves may sometimes be hidden in ST segment, or the preceding T wave, appearing as a 'notch' in the T wave.
P wave morphology different from sinus P wave morphology.
Narrow QRS complexes. In some cases, the PAC can conduct aberrantly, making it difficult to distinguish from PVCs. If the PAC arrives too early, it may not be conducted through to the ventricles, resulting in a 'blocked PAC'.
» Sinus pause. This can be compensatory (i.e. \(2 \times\) cycle length of 2 sinus PP intervals), less than compensatory or more than 2 sinus beat cycle lengths.

- PACs may be seen in patients who have:

Myocarditis
Tumours
Electrolyte abnormalities
» Drugs, e.g. beta agonists, sympathomimetics, tricyclic antidepressants, chemotherapeutic agents, monoamine oxidase inhibitors
» Indwelling central venous lines with tip/line in the right atrium

- Management: Further evaluation is not needed unless the PACs are associated with some other condition that warrants investigation or treatment.

![img-54.jpeg](img-54.jpeg)

Figure 7.4 Premature atrial contractions. Arrowheads indicate the premature P waves. The AV conduction of the premature beats here is normal.

## Premature Ventricular Contractions (PVCs)

- PVCs are very common and frequently asymptomatic and benign. Frequent PVCs may point to an underlying cardiac condition or may result in PVC-induced cardiomyopathies. They may also result in more dangerous cardiac arrhythmias.
- PVCs are due to an ectopic focus in the ventricle that bypasses the His-Purkinje system and directly depolarises the ventricles.
- ECG characteristics:

b4854_Sec-07.indd 173

12-Dec-22 3:15:35 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

![img-55.jpeg](img-55.jpeg)

Figure 7.5 Two ventricular premature complexes (arrowheads) are seen in this rhythm.

» Premature QRS complex (RR interval shorter than preceding RR interval)
Wide QRS complexes, with abnormal QRS morphology
» Discordant ST segment and T wave changes
» Compensatory pause following the contraction (the next sinus P wave occurs exactly 2 PP intervals after the last sinus beat)
» Classification: (i) Unifocal or polymorphic; (ii) bigeminy, trigeminy, quadrigeminy, couplet, triplets (\(\geq 3\) consecutive PVCs at a rate \(>100 / \mathrm{min} = \mathrm{VT}\))

- Management: Refer to cardiology for further evaluation, especially if there is a history or family history of cardiac conditions/sudden deaths, patient is symptomatic with palpitations, syncope or exercise-induced symptoms, abnormal cardiac examination, ECG findings of polymorphic PVCs, frequent PVCs.

- Patients will usually be evaluated with an echocardiography, Holter monitoring, and exercise stress testing.

## Bradycardia and Conduction Disorders

### Sinus Bradycardia

- Rhythm originates from SA node-P wave upright in leads I, II and aVF
- Rate less than normal for age
- Causes of sinus bradycardia:

» Cardiac: Sinus node dysfunction, long QT syndrome, myocarditis, postcardiac surgery, myocardial infarction
Non cardiac: Hypoxia, obstructive sleep apnoea, infections (relative bradycardia, e.g. malaria, dengue, typhoid fever), raised intracranial pressure and other central nervous system (CNS) conditions, hypothyroidism, anorexia nervosa, hypothermia, drugs (e.g. beta blockers, amiodarone, opioids, dexmedetomidine, corticosteroids)

- Management: Evaluate and treat the cause of sinus bradycardia accordingly.

- Unstable sinus bradycardia: Refer to Chapter 2 on Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation

![img-56.jpeg](img-56.jpeg)

Figure 7.6 Sinus bradycardia.

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

» Pharmacological treatment

Adrenaline \(0.01\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (0.1 ml/kg of 1:10,000) intravenous (IV) bolus
Adrenaline infusion \(0.1 - 1.0\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\)
Atropine \(0.02\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (min \(0.1\mathrm{mg})\) IV may be repeated once
Isoprenaline infusion \(0.01 - 2.0\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\)

» Pacing, temporary or permanent

# Sinus Arrest

- Sudden absence of atrial activity.
- The longest PP interval is not a multiple of the shortest PP interval.
- Escape rhythms of atrial, junctional or ventricular origin are sometimes seen.
Presents with syncope.
- Indications for therapy are the same as in sinus bradycardia.

# First Degree Atrioventricular (AV) Block

- Prolonged PR interval (age dependant) due to delay in conduction from atria to ventricles (Table 7.4). All impulses are conducted.

- Generally asymptomatic and does not progress to a complete heart block. There are exceptions such as in rheumatic fever and endocarditis.
- Causes: High vagal tone (physiological, most common), drugs (e.g. beta blockers, calcium channel blockers), cardiomyopathies, some muscular dystrophies, endocarditis, rheumatic fever.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Management: Further evaluation not required if history and examination not suggestive of cardiac or systemic disease. Rarely, very prolonged PR interval causing symptomatic bradycardia may benefit from pacing.

![img-58.jpeg](img-58.jpeg)

Figure 7.8 First-degree atrioventricular block with a PR interval of 0.4 sec.

## Mobitz Type I Second-Degree AV Block (Wenckebach)

- Generally asymptomatic and benign, unlikely to progress to a complete heart block.
- Due to increasing delay in conduction through AV node.
- ECG characteristics:

Progressive PR interval prolongation until a P wave is not conducted.
Irregular rhythm, QRS complexes with 'group beating'.
PP interval is constant.

- Causes: Increased vagal tone (physiological, most common), drugs (beta blockers, calcium channel blockers, digoxin, amiodarone), post-operative cardiac surgery, myocarditis.
- Management: Does not require further evaluation or treatment unless symptomatic.

![img-59.jpeg](img-59.jpeg)

Figure 7.9 Mobitz type I second-degree atrioventricular block. The arrowheads indicate the P dropped beats.

## Mobitz Type II Second-Degree AV Block

- Usually caused by a block below the AV node. There is a higher risk of progression to a complete heart block and sudden death. May be symptomatic with lightheadedness, presyncope and syncope, although may also be asymptomatic.
- ECG characteristics:

Intermittent non-conducted P waves, without progressive lengthening of PR interval.
Constant PP and PR intervals.

- Causes: Drugs (flecainide, procainamide, quinidine), myocarditis, endocarditis, hypothyroidism, hyperkalaemia, infiltrative diseases, e.g. sarcoidosis, neuromuscular disease, post-cardiac surgery.

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

Management:

» Requires close monitoring. Need to evaluate for underlying cardiac disease (echocardiography, consider cardiac MRI)
Unstable - refer to management of unstable bradycardia.
» Stable — if no reversible causes identified, permanent pacemaker is definitive treatment.

![img-60.jpeg](img-60.jpeg)

Figure 7.10 Mobitz type II second-degree atrioventricular block. The arrowheads indicate the P waves that were not conducted. Note constant PR interval.

## Third-Degree (Complete) AV Block

- Complete absence of atrioventricular conduction, i.e. none of the supraventricular impulses are able to conduct through to the ventricles. Patients may present with fatigue, exercise intolerance, congestive heart failure, presyncope or syncope, or may be asymptomatic. They are at a higher risk of ventricular standstill or sudden cardiac death.
- ECG characteristics:

» Complete dissociation of P wave and QRS complex.
» Regular PP intervals, regular RR intervals. Atrial rate higher than ventricular rate.
» QRS morphology and heart rate vary according to location of the escape pacemaker: the higher the pacemaker, the faster the rate and narrower the QRS complex.

- Causes:

Congenital, e.g. babies born to mothers with collagen vascular disease, congenital heart disease, e.g. congenitally corrected transposition of great arteries, genetic.
» Acquired: Post-operative repair of congenital heart diseasemay require a permanent pacemaker if complete AV block persists more than 2 weeks, myocarditis, rheumatic fever—usually temporary and tend to resolve in < 1 week.

Management:

Refer to Cardiology.
Unstable - see management for unstable sinus bradycardia.

![img-61.jpeg](img-61.jpeg)

Figure 7.11 Complete heart block. Note atrioventricular dissociation.

# The Baby Bear Book

- Indications for permanent pacemaker insertion: HR <55/min (infant) or <50/min (above infancy), wide QRS complex escape rhythm, ventricular dysfunction, complex PVCs, abrupt ventricular pauses, symptomatic bradycardia.

## Tachyarrhythmias

Supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) is an umbrella term used to categorise any tachyarrhythmia arising above the level of bundle of His, including atrial and atrioventricular tachycardias.

## Paroxysmal Supraventricular Tachycardia (SVT)

- Most common significant arrhythmia in childhood. Starts and stops abruptly.
- Tachycardia from accessory pathway (AV re-entrant SVT) is the most common SVT seen in infancy and early childhood. AV nodal re-entrant SVT becomes more common in later childhood and adolescence.
- Due to re-entrant mechanism involving the AV node.
- ECG characteristics:

Regular fixed-rate tachycardia, typically narrow complex (exceptions include aberrancy, antidromic conduction, pre-existing bundle branch block).
P waves may be hidden, or retrograde.
\(\gg\) Wolff-Parkinson-White: Diagnosed when the accessory pathway is evident on ECG as a shortened PR interval, with slurred upstroke of the QRS complex (delta wave).

- SVT may be difficult to differentiate from sinus tachycardia. See Table 7.5 for general guidelines that may help to distinguish between them.

Table 7.5 General Guidelines that Distinguish Sinus Tachycardia from Supraventricular Tachycardia (SVT)

|   | Sinus Tachycardia | Supraventricular Tachycardia  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Rate | <180/min | >220/min  |
|  History | Consistent with volume loss, fever, infection | Non-specific — irritability, poor feeding, tachypnoea, sweating, pallor  |
|  Physical Examination | Consistent with dehydration, fever, sepsis, bloodloss Clear lungs (creps in chest infection) No hepatomegaly | Poor perfusion Signs of heart failure Fine crepitations in lungs Hepatomegaly  |
|  CXR | Non-specific, no cardiomegaly, normal, or, e.g. pneumonia as source of fever/sepsis | Cardiomegaly, pulmonary congestion or oedema  |
|  ECG | Rarely helpful, usually normal | Monotonous rhythm — fairly fixed rate despite changes in activity. Sudden termination/initiation. Terminate with heart block  |

If SVT persists: Call cardiologist.

Other anti-arrhythmic medications include:

Intravenous propranolol \(0.1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose over 10 min — caution if myocardial function is at least moderately depressed.
Intravenous verapamil \(0.1 - 0.2\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) (max. \(5 - 10\mathrm{mg})\) over \(5\mathrm{min}\) then \(5\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\). (Do NOT give in infants \(< 1\) year old)
Per oral sotalol start at \(2\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) Q8H and increase dose by 1- \(2\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) with 3 days interval between dosage increments. Max.: \(10\mathrm{mg / kg / day};320\mathrm{mg / day}\)
Per oral flecainide — 2–3 mg/kg/dose (max. 100 mg) stat and Q12H
Intravenous amiodarone — 25 mcg/kg/min for 4 h, then 5–15 mcg/kg/min. Watch for hypotension and bradycardia.

Do 12-lead ECG post-conversion to sinus rhythm
Haemodynamically unstable:

Synchronised direct current (DC) cardioversion under sedation: Initial \(0.5 - 1\mathrm{J / kg}\), then \(2\mathrm{J / kg}\) for subsequent shocks.
Consider intravenous adenosine while preparing for cardioversion and if IV access readily available.

## Atrial Flutter

- Uncommon in the paediatric population. Usually seen in children with underlying congenital heart disease. It is due to a macro re-entrant pathway in the atria. May account for up to one-third of foetal tachyarrhythmias. In infants, the prognosis is good once rhythm has converted back to sinus, and recurrence is rare.
- ECG characteristics:

- Narrow complex tachycardia, ventricular rhythm may be regular or irregular.

![img-64.jpeg](img-64.jpeg)

Figure 7.14 Atrial flutter in an infant. Regular, narrow complex tachycardia, rate 200/min. 'Saw tooth' inverted flutter waves seen in lead II, rate 400/min, indicating 2:1 AV conduction block. Adenosine was given, unmasking the flutter waves. Note that flutter waves do not return to isoelectric point.

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

Saw tooth' inverted flutter waves seen in leads II, III, aVF, with loss of isoelectric baseline.
Atrial rate ranges from 300 to \(600 / \mathrm{min}\); ventricular rate is slower and depends on the degree of AV conduction block. (E.g. 2:1 block when atrial rate is 300/ min means a ventricular rate of \(150 / \mathrm{min}\).)

- Causes: Congenital heart disease (e.g. Ebstein anomaly, atrioventricular septal defect), post-cardiac surgeries, especially when atrial scars are created (e.g. Mustard, Senning, Fontan, TAPVD repair), cardiomyopathies, hyperthyroidism, muscular dystrophy.

Management:

Unstable—synchronised cardioversion: Initial \(0.5 - 1\mathrm{J / kg}\), subsequent shocks \(2\mathrm{J / kg}\)
Stable:

Rate control: IV beta blockers, IV Verapamil (avoid in infants \(< 1\) year old), IV amiodarone
Rhythm control: Rapid atrial pacing — especially in post-op cases where there are pacing wires. Set pacemaker to RAP mode. AAI pacing; set atrial rate higher than the flutter rate by \(10 - 15\%\).
Synchronised cardioversion.

Note: Adenosine does NOT treat atrial flutter. It slows down AV conduction and may unmask flutter waves.
Anticoagulation therapy due to higher risk of thromboembolic events
Flutter \(\geq 48\) hours or unknown duration: Warfarin 3 weeks before and 4 weeks after.
Flutter \(< 48\) hours, low risk of thromboembolism: Anticoagulation for DC cardioversion only.
If immediate cardioversion is needed: Start anticoagulation \(\rightarrow\) Transoesophageal Echocardiography (TEE) \(\rightarrow\) may continue anticoagulation 4 weeks after.

# Focal Atrial Tachycardia

- A form of atrial tachycardia due to a single ectopic focus originating from the atrium.
- Uncommon in paediatric population and may be paroxysmal or sustained.
- May be difficult to differentiate from sinus tachycardia or AV re-entrant tachycardias.

![img-65.jpeg](img-65.jpeg)

Figure 7.15 Focal atrial tachycardia. This is a slow, regular narrow complex tachycardia of around 100/min. IV adenosine was given where the arrowhead points. Uniform P waves can be seen thereafter, indicating this is not a AV re-entrant tachycardia.

# The Baby Bear Book

ECG characteristics:

Narrow complex tachycardia, atrial rate \(>100 / \mathrm{min}\)
P wave morphology differs from sinus P wave morphology — compare with previous ECGs if possible.
» May have 'warm-up' or 'cool down' phenomenon: Tachycardia gradually speeds up at onset and slows down at termination.

- Causes: Congenital heart disease, post-cardiac surgery, hypoxia, pulmonary disease, stimulants, metabolic disturbances

Management:

» Rate control with IV beta blockers or calcium channel blockers.
IV Esmolol infusion \(0.05\mathrm{mg / kg / min}\) up to \(0.2\mathrm{mg / kg / min}\) . Watch for hypotension. Typically given up to \(48\mathrm{h}\)
IV Propranolol \(0.1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(5\mathrm{mg})\) over \(10\mathrm{min}\), then \(0.1 - 0.3\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(5 - 15\mathrm{mg})\) Q6-8H
IV Amiodarone \(5\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(300\mathrm{mg})\) over \(60\mathrm{min}\) (loading dose); infusion \(5 - 15\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\)
» Avoid catecholamines where possible.

## Multifocal Atrial Tachycardia (MAT)

- A rapid, irregular atrial rhythm caused by multiple ectopic foci in the atria.
- Rare in infant and paediatric population, occurs in \(< 1\%\) of paediatric SVT.
ECG characteristics:

At least 3 distinct P wave morphologies in the same lead.
Irregularly irregular PP, PR and RR intervals
Tachycardia for age, HR \(>100 / \mathrm{min}\)

- Causes: Associated with severe pulmonary disease, congenital heart disease, metabolic disturbances, drugs, RASopathies

Management:

Treat underlying causes/diseases.
» Correct electrolyte abnormalities.
» Pharmacological treatment include beta blockers, calcium channel blockers, amiodarone.

![img-66.jpeg](img-66.jpeg)

Figure 7.16 Multiatrial focal tachycardia. Note the irregularly irregular rhythm, with a rate ranging from 180 to 270/min. The arrowheads show at least three different P wave morphologies.

# Cardiac Arrhythmias

## Ventricular Tachycardia (VT)

- Wide complex tachycardia originating in the ventricles.
- Can be haemodynamically unstable and potentially life-threatening.
- In children, a wide complex tachycardia should be treated as VT until proven otherwise.
- Sustained VT: Haemodynamic compromise requiring intervention, or duration \(>30\) s. Non-sustained VT: \(\geq 3\) consecutive ventricular complexes, spontaneously abort \(< 30\) s.
ECG characteristics:

Wide QRS complex (for age), HR \(>100 / \mathrm{min}\)
Complete AV dissociation, ventricular rate faster than atrial rate
Presence of capture beats or fusion beats, extreme (northwest) axis deviation, positive or negative concordance throughout chest leads V1-6

- Differentials include: SVT with aberrancy, SVT with pre-existing bundle branch block, antidromic SVT.
- Causes: Congenital heart disease, post-cardiac surgery, myocardial ischaemia, myocarditis, cardiomyopathy, long QT syndrome, metabolic disturbances
Management (as per APLS):

Correct any treatable causes.
Pulseless VT should be managed as VF; defibrillate with \(4\mathrm{J / kg}\)
Unstable: Synchronised DC cardioversion: 0.5-1 J/kg (first shock), 2 J/kg (subsequent).
Stable: IV Amiodarone \(5\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. \(300\mathrm{mg}\)) over \(60\mathrm{min}\) (loading dose); may repeat up to three times \((15\mathrm{mg / kg})\) . \(5 - 15\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\) maintenance infusion.
Watch for hypotension. Standby Calcium chloride infusion.
IV Lignocaine \(1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (bolus). May repeat in 10-15 min for 2 doses. Maintenance level: \(1.5 - 5.0~\mathrm{mg / L}\)
IV Magnesium \(50\mathrm{mg / kg}\) (max. 2 g) may be given for Torsades de Pointes.

## Post-Operative Arrhythmia

- Common arrhythmias seen in post-cardiac surgery patients include AV block, SVT and junctional ectopic tachycardia.
- Many patients come out of the operating theatre with atrial and ventricular pacing wires.

## Atrial ECG Study

- Connect all ECG leads as usual except for V2 lead (or any chest lead that is running on the rhythm strip).
- Connect V2 lead to the atrial pacing wire.
- The P waves are recorded as sharp, tall spikes on the V2 tracing.
- This helps identify P waves during a tachycardia and demonstrates the association between P and QRS complex.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Junctional Ectopic Tachycardia (JET)

- This is a transient arrhythmia and usually resolves within 24–72 hours post-operatively. However, it may compromise cardiac output in patients.
- Higher risk in cardiac surgeries close to the AV node.
- Due to abnormal automaticity of tissues at the AV node — His bundle complex
- ECG characteristics:

- Narrow complex tachycardia.
- Atrioventricular dissociation, ventricular rate higher than atrial rate.
- Retrograde P waves may be seen — atrial ECG can be performed to identify the relationship of P and QRS.

- Management:

- Inform Cardiology — echocardiography may be performed to rule out effusion and check function.
- Ensure adequate sedation.
- Aim normothermia, can cool to 34–36°C if still in JET.
- Correct electrolyte abnormalities, hypovolaemia, anaemia.
- Wean off catecholaminergic agents if possible.
- Medications:

☐ Intravenous dexmedetomidine: 0.2–1 mcg/kg/h. Higher infusion rate of 3 mcg/kg/h reported to be useful for conversion of JET to sinus rhythm. Reserve loading only for extreme situation. Loading dose: IV 1 mcg/kg over 15 min. Main side effects are hypotension and bradycardia (including complete heart block)

☐ Intravenous amiodarone: 25 mcg/kg/min (= loading over 4 h), then maintenance infusion 5–15 mcg/kg/min; alternatively, intravenous boluses of 1 mg/kg over 10 min (max. 5 mg/kg over 60 min), then maintenance infusion 5–15 mcg/kg/min; alternatively, loading dose: IV 5 mg/kg over 10 min (repeat once if needed), followed by maintenance infusion: 5–15 mcg/kg/min for 48–72 hours. Watch for hypotension and bradycardia. Ensure that calcium chloride is available by the bedside.

☐ Atrial or AV sequential pacing may be started to restore synchrony once the JET rate is reduced. This modality cannot be used if JET rates are ≥170–180/ min since pacing at higher rates will compromise diastolic filling.

# CHAPTER 27

# Cardiomyopathy and Heart Failure

Zhang Zhewei, Dyan; Sreekanthan Sundararaghavan

## Introduction

Heart failure in children (aged 0 to 18 years) can be defined broadly as the failure of the heart to supply blood to either systemic or pulmonary circulation at an appropriate rate of flow or to receive venous return at an appropriate filling pressure, resulting in adverse effects on the heart, circulation and patient. As paediatric heart failure is a relatively uncommon condition, most practitioners in primary care or emergency departments have little practical experience with its presentation or management in children. The clinical manifestations might be dissimilar to those of adults and quite variable. Because 87% of cases of new-onset heart failure only reach a diagnosis when the patient is in a state of severe decompensation, and <50% of children who present with symptomatic heart failure survive beyond 5 years without heart transplantation, early diagnosis and effective treatment remain significant challenges that should be addressed.

## Causes

Heart failure can occur in children as a consequence of varied causes, including cardiac (Table 7.6) and non-cardiac causes.

- Heart failure due to cardiac causes include congenital cardiac malformations and primary cardiomyopathies.
- Congenital cardiac malformations typically present with heart failure early in life, resulting from abnormal chamber morphology, valvular function or circulatory connections.
- Primary cardiomyopathies, which result from disorders affecting the myocardium, may be apparent at birth, but usually manifests later in infancy, childhood or during adult life.
- Non-cardiac causes include anaemia, hypothyroidism and systemic lupus erythematosus, which typically occurs later in childhood.
- Right heart failure due to pulmonary hypertension can be due to diverse diseases, predominantly that of idiopathic pulmonary arterial hypertension, developmental lung disease or in association with congenital heart diseases in the paediatric population. Onset can occur at any age.
- Primary cardiomyopathies are the main cause of heart failure in children with a structurally normal heart, and the incidence of heart failure varies amongst the

186

morphological types of heart failure. Dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM) is the most common myopathic process leading to heart failure in children.

| --- | --- | --- | --- |

|  Long-term complications | Progressive heart failure, sudden death | Arrhythmia, worsening LVOTO, sudden death | Arrhythmia, sudden death  |

|  » Myocarditis  |
|  » Cardiomyopathies  |

HLHS: hypoplastic left heart syndrome; PDA: patent ductus arteriosus; SVT: supraventricular tachycardia; TAPVD: total anomalous pulmonary venous drainage

## Clinical Features

Symptoms of heart failure in children may be similar regardless of the underlying aetiology, but physical signs might be more specific. Systemic manifestations are mediated by sympathetic and central neurological pathways, activated in response to pulmonary congestion and/or decreased systemic perfusion. Clinical features of heart failure unique to children are:

- Possible coexistence of structural congenital heart lesions, with simultaneous pulmonary over-circulation and systemic under-perfusion (when two circulations are linked in parallel by an intracardiac shunt or a patent ductus arteriosus).
- A change in symptom complexes over time from infancy through adolescence. In infants and young children, these are primarily respiratory and feeding difficulties (which are similar to the metabolic demands of physical exertion seen in older children).
- Symptoms of isolated right heart failure in children may be non-specific and varied in presentation.

# The Baby Bear Book

## History

See Table 7.9 for the typical features of heart failure in children, categorised as common and less common. The New York Heart Association (NYHA) classification of functional class is best suited to quantify changes in functional capacity in patients with established chronic heart failure. The Modified Ross classification (Table 7.10) has been applied to younger children for the same purpose.

Table 7.9. Symptoms Characteristic of Heart Failure in Children

|  Symptoms Characteristic of Heart Failure in Children  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|   | Common | Less Common  |
|  Infants and young children | • Tachypnoea • Feeding difficulty (reflux, vomiting, feeding refusal) • Diaphoresis • Pallor | • Cyanosis • Palpitations • Syncope • Facial oedema • Dependent oedema • Ascites  |
|  Older children and adolescents | • Fatigue • Effort intolerance • Dyspnoea • Orthopnoea • Abdominal pain • Nausea • Vomiting | • Palpitations • Chest pain • Dependent oedema • Ascites  |

Table 7.10 The Modified Ross Classification

|  Classification of Symptomatic Severity in Paediatric Heart Failure  |   |
| --- | --- |
|  Class of Symptoms | Symptoms Noted on History  |
|  I | Asymptomatic  |
|  II | Infants: Mild tachypnoea or diaphoresis with feeding; no growth failure Older children: Dyspnoea on moderate exertion  |
|  III | Infants: Marked tachypnoea or diaphoresis with feeding; growth failure Older children: Dyspnoea on mild or minimal exertion  |
|  IV | Tachypnoea, diaphoresis or respiratory distress at rest  |

## Clinical Examination

The cardinal signs of cardiac failure are cardiomegaly, tachycardia, tachypnoea and hepatomegaly. Poor feeding and chest retractions also feature prominently in the younger infants. The signs and symptoms of heart failure are related to three factors: impaired myocardial function with decreased systemic perfusion, pulmonary congestion and systemic venous congestion.

# Cardiomyopathy and Heart Failure

## Signs of Impaired Myocardial Function

Cardiomegaly

A fairly consistent sign of impaired cardiac function, secondary to ventricular dilatation and/or hypertrophy.
May be absent in early stages, especially with myocarditis, arrhythmias, restrictive disorders and pulmonary venous obstruction.

Tachycardia

Persistently raised heart rate \(>160 / \mathrm{min}\) in infants and \(>100 / \mathrm{min}\) in older children.
Consider supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) if heart rate \(>220 / \mathrm{min}\) in infants and \(>180 / \mathrm{min}\) in older children.

S3 and/or S4 (gallop rhythm)
- Poor peripheral perfusion — cool extremities, pallor
Changes in arterial pulse

Weak peripheral pulses
\(\gg\) Bounding pulses in PDA, large AV fistula, high output failure
Pulsus paradoxus — accentuation of the normal fall in blood pressure on inspiration. Seen in cardiac tamponade. Can be detected by palpation of the peripheral pulses or by looking at the waveform on the pulse oximeter or invasive blood pressure monitor.
Pulsus alternans — alternating strong and weak beats with a constant beat-to-beat interval.

- Failure to thrive
Signs of sympathetic overdrive, e.g. diaphoresis, peripheral vasoconstriction, irritability.

## Signs of Pulmonary Congestion

- Dyspnoea, tachypnoea and chest retractions

Major presenting manifestations of cardiac failure in infants. Fairly early signs
Result in poor feeding — inability to or taking longer to finish feeds (>30 minutes), increasing symptoms during and after feeds.

Cough - a chronic, hacking cough
- Crepitations
- Rhonchi

May be present in left ventricular failure.
This, together with cough and crepitations, may make differentiation from bronchiolitis, pneumonia or asthma difficult. Superimposed pulmonary infections can and do occur in patients with congenital heart defects.
Factors that should bring up the possibility of heart failure: Prior history of significant cardiac defect or heart failure, chronicity of symptoms, absence of fever, failure to respond to treatment, cardiomegaly, hepatomegaly.

# The Baby Bear Book

## Signs of Systemic Venous Congestion

Hepatomegaly

Most consistent sign of systemic venous congestion.
Can be tender, especially if congestion is severe or acute and older children can present with vomiting and abdominal pain mimicking gastritis or an acute abdomen.

- Increased neck vein distension and pulsation

- Difficult to observe in infants.

- Peripheral oedema — an infrequent finding in children. Consider right heart failure when this occurs in isolation.

## Investigations

Diagnostic testing is always indicated in children with suspected heart failure. The child presenting with symptoms and signs of heart failure requires urgent assessment to establish the diagnosis, rapidly determine their haemodynamic status and identify any reversible causes of heart failure. See Figure 7.17 for a simplified diagnostic approach to cardiomyopathy.

- **Chest radiography (CXR)**

Chest radiography is indicated as the first-line investigation in children with suspected heart failure.
Findings: Cardiomegaly, pulmonary plethora, pulmonary oedema

MRI: magnetic resonance imaging; MRS: magnetic resonance spectroscopy

# Cardiomyopathy and Heart Failure

# Electrocardiography (ECG)

All patients should have 12-lead ECG performed at the time of presentation with heart failure, to exclude features of congenital or ischaemic heart disease, arrhythmia and pre-excitation.
Findings: Sinus tachycardia, axis deviation, P pulmonale, non-specific T-wave and ST segment changes, LV or RV hypertrophy, first-degree heart block
Specific ECG features for the underlying cardiac defect might be present. A specific arrhythmic cause of heart failure might be identified, such as incessant tachycardia (usually ectopic atrial tachycardia), atrioventricular block or ventricular pre-excitation.

# Echocardiography

Delineate underlying structural defect(s)
Assess cardiac chamber dilatation and hypertrophy
Demonstrate decreased myocardial contractility and cardiac function
Determine response to therapy

# Biochemical and routine laboratory testing

Blood gas:

Acidosis (usually metabolic, mixed metabolic and respiratory in severe pulmonary oedema)
Slight decrease in \(\mathrm{PaO}_2\) in patients with left-to-right shunt lesions (due to pulmonary congestion, intrapulmonary right-to-left shunting and ventilation-perfusion mismatch); marked hypoxaemia in patients with underlying cyanotic heart defects.
Infants with mild and moderate heart failure tend to have a respiratory alkalosis (lower than normal \(\mathrm{PaCO}_2\)). However in severe heart failure or if there is a co-existing lung disorder (e.g. pneumonia), the \(\mathrm{PaCO}_2\) may be increased.
Useful to guide need for further respiratory support (e.g. CPAP or intubation and ventilation)

Urea, electrolytes and creatinine

Hyponatraemia reflects increased water retention; hypochloraemia and increase in bicarbonate occurs secondary to diuretic use; potassium levels may be elevated due to cation shift from the intracellular stores; hypokalaemia from the use of loop diuretics.
As a baseline prior to commencing treatment and a measure of renal perfusion.

Liver function test (LFT)

☐ An evaluation of liver end-organ perfusion/injury owing to low cardiac output

Full blood count (FBC)

☐ **Findings:** Anaemia, abnormal RBC indices may indicate haemoglobinopathies. Cyclic neutropenia occurs in males with Barth syndrome.

# The Baby Bear Book

Erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR)

Indicates inflammatory disorder/infection.

# - Biomarkers

Natriuretic peptide biomarkers

Brain natriuretic peptide (BNP) or amino terminal (NT-proBNP) are established as a valuable aid to the identification of cardiac disease failure in children presenting with non-specific respiratory symptoms and to evaluate the degree of heart failure severity and treatment response.
Serial BNP or NT-proBNP measurements can be used in children to guide therapeutic intervention or to monitor heart failure status.

Cardiac troponins

Cardiac troponins are likely to be more elevated in heart failure resulting from acute myocarditis.
Troponins can be elevated in cardiomyopathy with increasing levels correlating with severity.

# - Infection markers in suspected myocarditis

Blood for PCR (parvovirus, adenovirus, Epstein-Barr Virus, Cytomegalovirus, Herpes Simplex Virus, Human Herpesvirus (HHV) 6-8, Influenza A & B, mycoplasma)
Nasopharyngeal/tracheal aspirate/swab for parvovirus, respiratory viruses
Stool/rectal swab for viral PCR (coxsackievirus, echovirus)

# - Metabolic and genetic testing

Early and accurate identification of a metabolic or genetic aetiology for heart failure may allow for life-saving disease-specific management, identify family members at risk and provide guidance for reproductive counselling.
Primary screening investigations for metabolic disorders include plasma lactate, pyruvate, ammonia, plasma amino acids, acylcarnitine profile, urine amino acids, urine organic acids, urine mucopolysaccharide screen (glycosaminoglycans).
Subspecialty consultation with genetic and/or metabolic services is recommended to guide further testing such as muscle biopsy or specific gene testing, molecular or cytogenetic testing.

# - Family assessment

A detailed family history with pedigree (3 generations) is important in establishing familial cardiomyopathy. Excluding familial cardiomyopathy is crucial, especially when the presentation is in the foetus or newborn.
Perform ECG and echocardiography in all first-degree relatives.

## Management

In the acute management of heart failure, the patients can be thought of as having symptoms related to fluid overload, systemic under-perfusion or both (Figure 7.18).

# Cardiomyopathy and Heart Failure

![img-68.jpeg](img-68.jpeg)

Figure 7.18 Patterns of presentation recognised in acute decompensated heart failure. Redrawn and modified from Stevenson LW, et al. (2002) Treatment of congestive heart failure. JAMA; 287:2209–2210.

The early management of children with heart failure should address these problems. It is important to note that indiscriminate administration of intravenous fluid resuscitation is contraindicated and will worsen the condition of children with heart failure symptoms.

See Figure 7.19 for an approach to the management of acute decompensated heart failure (ADHF) and Figure 7.20 for guidance to the introduction of oral maintenance therapy in chronic heart failure.

## Treat the Underlying Cause of the Heart Failure, If Possible

- Surgery or transcatheter therapy for structural heart defect, after stabilisation.
- Pericardiocentesis for pericardial effusion.
- Adenosine, other anti-arrhythmic agents or cardioversion for arrhythmias causing heart failure.

## General Measures

- Bed rest, limit activities.
- Nurse propped up or sitting up.
Thermo-neutral environment; control fever.
- Tube feeding in small infants.
- Fluid restriction is necessary in admitted patients, especially if dilutional hyponatraemia is present or in overtly fluid-overloaded patients.
- Sedation may be used with caution in view of risk of respiratory depression, under supervised settings. Oral chloral hydrate \(10 - 20\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose, 6-8 hourly/

# Cardiomyopathy and Heart Failure

as needed. In older patients, IV morphine infusion 10–20 mcg/kg/h can be considered.

- Correct any negative inotropic factors, e.g. acidosis, hypoglycaemia, hypocalcaemia and anaemia.
- Oxygen — caution in patients with left-to-right shunt (e.g. VSD); Oxygen causes pulmonary vasodilation and thus increases the shunt, aggravating pulmonary congestion and pulmonary oedema.
- Non-invasive ventilatory support with CPAP or mechanical ventilation, if necessary.

## Diuretics

- Frusemide IV/PO \(1\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\), 6-12 hourly (max. dose \(20 - 40\mathrm{mg / dose}\) in patient with normal renal function). In selected patients, continuous IV infusion at \(0.1 - 1.0\mathrm{mg / kg / h}\).
- Spironolactone as an adjunct (mineralocorticoid receptor antagonist), and a potassium-sparing diuretic PO \(1\mathrm{mg / kg / dose}\) (max. \(25\mathrm{mg}\)) 8-12 hourly.
- Monitor urine output and serum electrolytes.

## Inotropic Agents

Milrinone

A phosphodiesterase III inhibitor that is both a vasodilator and an inotropic agent. Useful in situations in which both these effects are desirable, e.g. postcardiac surgery, myocarditis.
IV infusion \(0.25 - 0.75\mathrm{mcg / kg / min}\)
First choice agent for ADHF requiring moderate inotropic support

Dobutamine

A \(\beta\)-adrenergic receptor agonist.
Lower doses promote renal vasodilation.
Higher doses discouraged, as they promote tachycardia.

## Angiotensin-Converting Enzyme Inhibitors (ACEi)

- The use of ACEi therapy is indicated in children with heart failure due to primary heart muscle disease of the systemic left ventricle.
- Contraindicated in patients with left-to-right shunts.
- In advanced heart failure, ACEi therapy introduction should occur after stabilisation of heart failure symptoms with diuretics and simultaneous to inotropic support withdrawal.
- Captopril \((0.1\mathrm{mg / kg / dose},8\) hourly) is the typical first choice for most infants and enalapril \((0.1\mathrm{mg / kg / dose},12\) hourly) being an appropriate choice for those older than 2 years.
- Up-titration can proceed safely over 3–10 days in most inpatients and can be more gradual in outpatients. Target dose of captopril is \(3\mathrm{mg/kg/day}\) and enalapril \(0.5\mathrm{mg/kg/dose}\).

# The Baby Bear Book

## Beta-Adrenergic Receptor Antagonist

- Benefits of beta blockers in the treatment of adults with heart failure demonstrated in \(>20\) randomised controlled trials. Only few uncontrolled studies indicating benefits in children.
- Contraindication: Decompensated heart failure, heart block, bradycardia, asthma.
- Caution: Reduced heart function during initial stages of therapy, hypotension, bradycardia.
- Carvedilol is the first choice for most children with normal systolic blood pressure; start at PO \(0.1\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose (max. \(3.125\mathrm{mg}\)) 12 hourly. If tolerated, double the dose every 1-2 weeks to max. of \(0.4 - 0.6\mathrm{mg / kg}\) dose (adult 12.5-25 mg) 12 hourly.
- Metoprolol is the usual choice for children with reduced systolic blood pressure; for patients \(< 50\mathrm{kg}\), start at PO \(0.5\mathrm{mg/kg/day}\) 12 hourly, doubling 2 weekly to a max. of \(4\mathrm{mg/kg/day}\); for patients \(>50\mathrm{kg}\), start at \(12.5\mathrm{mg/dose}\) 12 hourly, doubling 2 weekly to a max. of \(100\mathrm{mg}\) 12 hourly.

## Newer Therapeutic Options

- Levosimendan

A calcium sensitising agent. Both an inotropic agent and peripheral vasodilator that helps in afterload reduction without increasing myocardial oxygen consumption.
» Limited studies in paediatric population for heart failure. Some positive effect has been shown in intra-operative settings post-cardiopulmonary bypass.
» Consider if unresponsive to traditional inotropic therapy. Used mostly as potential rescue strategy to avoid mechanical circulatory support.

- Sacubitril/Valsartan (Entresto®)

Improvement in cardiovascular outcomes demonstrated in chronic heart failure management in the adult population.
In the ongoing PANORAMA-HF trial (at the time of writing), preliminary results show a reduction in NT-proBNP in the treatment of symptomatic heart failure with systemic left ventricular systolic dysfunction in paediatric patients beyond 1 year of age. Long-term efficacy in the paediatric population remains to be evaluated.
Not to be taken within 36 hours of taking an ACE-inhibitor.

## Options for Intractable Heart Failure

- Options available in selected patients include extracorporeal membrane oxygenation (ECMO), ventricular assist devices (VAD) and referral for heart transplant assessment. Intra-aortic balloon pump (IAPB) has been used in young adults.

## Bibliography

1. Chang AC, Towbin JA. (2006) *Heart Failure in Children and Young Adults: From Molecular Mechanisms to Medical and Surgical Strategies*. Sanders Elsevier.

# CHAPTER 28

# Infective Endocarditis

Tan Teng Hong

## Definition

Infective endocarditis (IE) is the inflammation of the valvular or mural endocardium caused by microorganisms (bacteria or fungi) involving either the heart or the great vessels (i.e. endarteritis — infection of the lining of blood vessels); the pathology also may include abscess formation.

## At Risk Patients

- • Congenital heart disease (CHD)
  - » >90% of IE cases occur in individuals who have heart disease, usually congenital
  - » Post-operative cardiac surgery
    - □ Cardiac surgery itself is an important risk factor for IE.
    - □ Highest risk in children who had repair or palliation of cyanotic CHD.
    - □ Incidence of IE in the first post-operative month is low for most defects and increases with time after surgery.
    - □ When prosthetic valves or conduits are used in surgical repairs, the risk for IE is high even in the immediate (first 2 weeks) post-operative period.
    - □ Corrective surgery for isolated ventricular septal defect (VSD), secundum atrial septal defect (ASD) or patent ductus arteriosus (PDA) with documentation of no residual leak, risk for IE is the same as for the general population, 6 months after surgery.
- • Degenerative or rheumatic heart disease
- • Normal hearts with
  - » Central indwelling venous catheters
  - » *Staphylococcus aureus* bacteraemia
  - » Intravenous drug abuse

Patients with congenital or acquired immunodeficiencies are not at higher risk for IE.

# Infective Endocarditis

## Causative Organisms

- Most common organisms are gram-positive cocci — Viridans group streptococci, staphylococci and enterococci.
- Less commonly, gram-negative bacilli — HACEK group (Haemophilus species, Actinobacillus actinomycetemcomitans, Cardiobacterium hominis, Eikenella corrodens and Kingella species).
- IE associated with indwelling central catheters, prosthetic valves or materials — S. aureus, coagulase-negative staphylococci.
- Newborn infants — S. aureus, coagulase-negative staphylococci, Candida species, group B streptococcus and Streptococcus pneumoniae.
- Fungi — the most common fungus is Candida, followed by Aspergillus.

## Clinical Features

Due to four underlying phenomena:

1. Bacteraemia (or fungaemia) — fever, myalgia, arthralgia, malaise.
2. Valvulitis — new heart murmur or change in murmur, congestive heart failure (CHF).
3. Embolic phenomenon — petechiae, hepatosplenomegaly, splinter haemorrhages, Janeway lesions (painless haemorrhagic lesions on palms and soles), Osler nodes (painful lesions at fingertips), pulmonary emboli, mycotic aneurysm, intracranial haemorrhage and conjunctival haemorrhages.
4. Immunologic responses — glomerulonephritis, Osler nodes, Roth's spots.

## Presentation

- Indolent (subacute bacterial endocarditis)

Prolonged low-grade fever
Variety of somatic complaints, including fatigue, weakness, arthralgias, myalgias, weight loss, rigors and diaphoresis

- Fulminant (acute bacterial endocarditis)

High, spiking fever
Rapidly changing symptoms
» Acutely ill

- Cardiac signs are variable (see Table 7.11):

» Valvular destruction, regurgitant murmurs
Cyanotic CHD in a child who has undergone a systemic-pulmonary shunt procedure may present with declining systemic oxygen saturation, reflecting a graft infection with obstruction of flow.
Patients with right-sided, catheter-related infection may have few or no specific cardiovascular signs but may present with primarily pulmonary symptoms and signs related to septic pulmonary embolisation.

Table 7.11 Presenting Signs in Children with Infective Endocarditis

|  Sign | %  |
| --- | --- |
|  Fever | 99  |
|  Petechiae | 21  |
|  Changing murmur | 21  |
|  Dental caries | 14  |
|  Hepatosplenomegaly | 14  |
|  Congestive heart failure | 9  |
|  Splenomegaly | 7  |
|  Splinter haemorrhages | 5  |
|  Retinal haemorrhages (Roth spots) | 5  |
|  Osler nodes | 4  |
|  Arthritis | 3  |

## Investigations

- Blood Cultures

The likelihood of culturing the causative organism is directly related to the volume of blood obtained (1-3 ml in infants; 5-7 ml in older children).
It is not necessary to time blood sampling with fever because bacteraemia in IE is usually continuous.
» Three blood cultures are obtained by separate venepunctures on the first day, and if there is no growth by the second day of incubation, two more should be obtained.
In patients who are not acutely ill and whose blood cultures are still negative, draw three aerobic blood cultures over the first day and hold antibiotics; if cultures are negative after 24 hours, draw two cultures and incubate for 2 weeks.
For patients with acute IE, three separate venepunctures for blood cultures, with 1 hour separating the first and the last, before administering empiric antibiotics.
Request forms for the blood cultures should indicate that IE is suspected to ensure that the laboratory will incubate the cultures for at least 2 weeks.
If fastidious or unusual organisms are suspected, the microbiology laboratory should be consulted.
Culture of arterial blood is not more useful than venepuncture.
In a stable patient suspected to have IE and treated with antibiotics for \(< 4\) days but with no prior blood culture, stopping the antibiotic before taking blood cultures can be considered.

# Infective Endocarditis

203

- Miscellaneous Laboratory Tests

Anaemia — can be due to haemolysis or anaemia of chronic disease
Leukocytosis — not a consistent feature of IE
Elevated acute-phase reactants, e.g., ESR, CRP
Renal/urine: Haematuria, red blood cell casts, proteinuria and renal insufficiency, immune complex glomerulonephritis

- Transthoracic Echocardiography (TTE)

Findings:

Identify vegetations (hallmark)
Determine the site and extent of infection
Baseline evaluation of ventricular performance and cardiac chamber dimension
Monitor serial cardiac function
Pericardial effusion or myocardial abscess

Inadequate in some circumstances:

Obese or very muscular adolescent
Post-cardiac surgery patients - presence of compromised respiratory function or pulmonary hyperinflation
Patients with poor echogenicity
Complex cardiac anatomy
Absence of vegetations on echocardiography does not rule out IE
Conversely, an echogenic mass can represent a sterile thrombus, sterile prosthetic material or normal anatomic variation rather than an infected vegetation.

- Transoesophageal Echocardiography (TEE)

TTE is usually adequate in children and may not need transoesophageal echocardiogram.
TEE should be reserved for those who have poor echo windows, in whom prosthetic valves cause 'shadowing' and TTE view appears normal despite a high index of suspicion for IE.

## Diagnosis

The utility of the Modified Duke Criteria (Table 7.12) in diagnosing IE in paediatric patients has been established.

## Management

Acute management: Antibiotics (Table 7.13), treatment of CHF, cardiac surgery if severe valvular regurgitation, aortic root abscess, large vegetation or embolic phenomena.

Long-term management: Management of residual lesions or sequelae.

# The Baby Bear Book

Table 7.12 Modified Duke Criteria for the Diagnosis of Infective Endocarditis

|  Major Criteria 1. Blood culture positive for IE a. Typical microorganisms consistent with IE from two separate blood cultures: - Viridans streptococci, Streptococcus bovis, HACEK group; OR - Community-acquired Staphylococcus aureus or enterococci, in the absence of a primary focus; OR b. Microorganisms consistent with IE from persistently positive blood cultures, defined as follows: - At least two positive cultures of blood samples drawn >12 hr apart; OR - All of 3 or a majority of ≥4 separate cultures of blood (with first and last sample drawn ≥1 hr apart) c. Single culture positive for Coxiella burnetii, or antiphase I IgG antibody titre >1:800 2. Evidence of endocardial involvement on echocardiogram a. Echocardiogram positive for IE (TEE recommended in patients with prosthetic valves, rated at least 'possible IE' by clinical criteria, or complicated IE [paravalvular abscess]; TTE as first test in other patients), defined as follows: - Oscillating intracardiac mass on valve or supporting structures, in the path of regurgitant jets, or on implanted material in the absence of an alternative anatomic explanation; OR - Abscess; OR - New partial dehiscence of prosthetic valve b. New valvular regurgitation (worsening or changing of pre-existing murmur not sufficient) Minor Criteria - Predisposition, predisposing heart condition or IV drug use - Fever, temperature ≥38°C - Vascular phenomenon: Major arterial emboli, septic pulmonary infarcts, mycotic aneurysm, intracranial haemorrhage, conjunctival haemorrhage, Janeway lesion - Immunologic phenomenon: Glomerulonephritis, Osler nodes, Roth spots, rheumatoid factor - Microbiological evidence: Positive blood culture that does not meet major criteria or serologic evidence of active infection with organism consistent with IE - Echocardiographic minor criteria eliminated Definite IE Pathological Criteria - Microorganisms demonstrated by culture or histologic examination of vegetation, vegetation that has embolised, or intracardiac abscess; OR - Pathologic lesions; vegetation or intracardiac abscess confirmed by histologic examination showing active IE Clinical Criteria - Two major criteria; OR - One major and three minor criteria; OR - Five minor criteria Possible IE - One major and one minor criterion; OR - Three minor criteria Rejected - Firm alternative diagnosis; OR - Resolution of symptoms with antibiotic therapy for ≤4 d; OR - No pathologic evidence of IE at surgery or autopsy, with antibiotic therapy for ≤4 d; OR Does not meet criteria for possible IE, as above  |

# Infective Endocarditis

## General Principles

- Prolonged course of intravenous therapy for at least 4 weeks duration or 3 weeks afebrile.
- Consider 6 weeks antibiotics if:
  » Prosthetic valve IE
  » Highly virulent organisms (e.g. S. aureus)
  » Relative antibiotic resistance of organism
- Dual or more antibiotics for synergistic effect.
- Outpatient antibiotic therapy can be considered in uncomplicated cases on a case-by-case basis, after the initial hospital treatment.

## Surgery

- Decisions regarding surgery in patients with IE should be individualised, with input from both the cardiologist and the cardiothoracic surgeon.
- There is limited information concerning the effects of surgical timing on the outcomes in children, but some authors have suggested excellent results with surgical intervention during the active phase of infection.
- If a patient with IE is receiving long-term oral anticoagulation, warfarin therapy should be discontinued and replaced by heparin immediately after the diagnosis of IE has been established in the event that surgical intervention is required.

## Indications for Surgical Intervention

- Most common indication — severe valvular insufficiency
- Progressive cardiac failure (usually due to valvular regurgitation)
- Persistent bacteraemia after 1–2 weeks of appropriate antibiotic therapy
- Perivalvular extension of infection/abscess formation
- Valve dehiscence, perforation, rupture or fistula
- Fungal endocarditis
- Prosthetic valve IE (particularly early prosthetic valve IE, that is, <12 months after valve replacement)
- Ruptured sinus of Valsalva or ventricular septum
- Significant embolic events
- Especially when the aortic or mitral valve is involved, that is, left-sided vegetations
- Soft indications — anterior mitral valve vegetation (particularly with size >10 mm), or persistent vegetation after systemic embolisation, or an increase in vegetation size despite appropriate antimicrobial therapy
- Patients may eventually require surgical intervention for chronic valvular stenosis or regurgitation caused by previous IE
- IE related to a haemodynamically trivial VSD should potentially be considered for surgical repair following successful treatment of the infection.

# Infective Endocarditis

## Prognosis

### Mortality

- Estimated ~11–17%

### Complications of IE

- Congestive heart failure (\(\sim 7\%\), secondary to severe valvular regurgitation)
- Embolic events: cerebral (stroke \(\sim 8\%\)), pulmonary (\(\sim 10\%\)), renal, coronary, gastrointestinal tract
- Periannular extension of abscess
- Arrhythmias \((\sim 8\%)\), heart block
- Prosthetic device dysfunction
- Valvular dehiscence
- Graft or shunt occlusion
- Persistent bacteraemia or fungaemia
- Metastatic infection: renal abscess (\(\sim 3\%\)), osteomyelitis (1%)
- Mycotic aneurysms
- Glomerulonephritis or renal failure

## Prophylaxis

The most recent revision of the American Heart Association (AHA) guidelines on infective endocarditis prophylaxis occurred in 2015 (see Table 7.14). The new guidelines suggested to shift the disproportionately large focus on antibiotic prophylaxis to an emphasis on oral hygiene, and that prophylaxis should be targeted at conditions that are associated with the highest probability of adverse outcomes from infective endocarditis.

## Conclusions

Even though IE is a rare diagnosis in childhood, a high index of suspicion in persistently febrile patients is necessary, especially in the context of congenital heart disease.

4. Ferrieri P, Gewitz MH, Gerber MA, et al. (2002) Unique features of infective endocarditis in childhood. Circulation 105:2115-2126.

# CHAPTER 29

# Kawasaki Disease

Kotecha Monika Kantilal; Choo Tze Liang, Jonathan

## Introduction

Kawasaki disease (KD) is an acute febrile systemic inflammatory/vasculitic disease of unknown aetiology, affecting predominantly children younger than 5 years. It was first described by Dr Tomisaku Kawasaki in 1967 and is now considered to be the commonest form of acquired heart disease in developed countries.

In Singapore, the incidence is estimated at 51.4 per 100,000 children younger than 5 years old per year (KKH and NUH databases, 2012). This incidence is one of the highest outside Japan, and very similar to that of Hong Kong and Taiwan. At KK Women's and Children's Hospital (KKH), we see an average of about 100 to 120 new cases a year. The incidence of KD appears to be increasing due to overall improved recognition and a more frequent diagnosis of incomplete KD.

## Aetiology

The aetiology and pathogenesis of KD remain largely unknown. Acute systemic vasculitis in KD shows inflammatory cells infiltrate the affected tissues. Infectious triggers for KD are reported in several studies. Several genome-wide association studies have identified the variants of ITPKC, FCGR 2A, BLK, and CD40 genes conferring susceptibility to KD. Other determinants, such as the climate, air currents and environmental factors have been studied. However, these studies have provided inconsistent results.

## Diagnosis

There is no specific diagnostic test for KD. KD is diagnosed clinically using the criteria originally described by Dr Kawasaki and modified by the AHA Committee on Rheumatic Fever, Endocarditis and Kawasaki Disease. Laboratory tests may be used to support a diagnosis of KD. In case of suspected KD, hospitalisation is recommended to perform a thorough evaluation and to confirm diagnosis.

## Diagnostic Criteria

Fever persistent for at least 5 days† and with the presence of at least four of the five following clinical features‡:

- Rash — polymorphous exanthem. The rash may be accentuated in the perineum where it is often associated with local desquamation. Redness or induration at

211

b4854_Sec-07.indd 211

12-Dec-22 3:15:53 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

the BCG vaccination scar may be present. The rash in KD is never vesicular or bullous.

- Bilateral conjunctivitis — painless hyperaemia, non-suppurative and usually sparing the limbus of the eye. Anterior uveitis seen on slit lamp examination strongly supports the diagnosis of KD in the first week of illness.
- Changes in lips and oral mucosa — erythema and cracking of lips, strawberry tongue, diffuse injection of oral and pharyngeal mucosa. Exudative pharyngitis and ulcerative intraoral lesions are not features typical of KD.
- Cervical lymphadenopathy \((\geq 1.5\mathrm{cm}\) in diameter) — unilateral or bilateral, usually the former. Generalised lymphadenopathy is not a feature of KD.
- Changes in extremities — acute: Erythema and oedema of hands and feet. convalescent: Skin desquamation of the tips of the fingers and toes (late sign).

† In the presence of classic features, the diagnosis of complete KD can be made by an experienced physician before 5 days of fever.

‡ Patients with fever and less than four other principal signs can be diagnosed as incomplete KD (see below).

## Other Significant Clinical Findings

- Cardiovascular: Pericarditis, myocarditis, endocarditis, arrhythmia, angina pectoris, signs of cardiac failure or shock
- Respiratory: Cough, rhinorrhoea
- Skin and Joints

Late (1-2 months): Beau's lines-transverse groove across the nail bed
» Arthralgia and arthritis

Neurological

» Striking irritability, inconsolable crying
» Facial palsy, sensorineural hearing loss or other mononeuritis (rare)
» Aseptic meningitis — mononuclear pleocytosis in the CSF
» Encephalopathy, seizures

Gastrointestinal

» Diarrhoea, vomiting, abdominal pain
Hepatitis
» Hydrops of the gallbladder
» Acute pancreatitis, cholestatic jaundice
» Retropharyngeal phlegmon
» Paralytic ileus

Renal

» Sterile pyuria is common — urethral origin
» Occasional proteinuria
» Urethritis
» Testicular swelling

Vascular:

» Aneurysms of peripheral arteries, e.g. axillary, or femoral (rare)

# Kawasaki Disease

» Raynaud's phenomenon
» Peripheral gangrene

## Investigations

- Haematological

» Full blood count (FBC) — increase white blood cells, maximal elevation of polymorphonuclear cells. Normocytic normochromic anaemia. Thrombocytosis, typically in the 2nd and 3rd week, normalisation in 4–8 weeks.
» Inflammatory parameters — elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), slow normalisation. Elevated C-reactive protein (CRP), fast normalisation.
» Liver function tests (LFT) — increase in alanine amino transaminases, gamma-glutamyl transpeptidase, bilirubin. Hypoalbuminemia in more severe and prolonged illness.
» Urea, creatinine, electrolytes (Renal panel) — hyponatraemia.
» Anti-streptolysin-O titre (ASOT)
» Cardiac enzymes and troponin — may be elevated if myocarditis/ coronary ischaemia with thrombosis.

- Urinalysis and culture sensitivity — urine white blood cells more than 10/high powered field (sterile pyuria).
- Cerebrospinal fluid examination — aseptic meningitis (normal glucose/proteins, white blood cells 125,000–300,000/mm³)
- Chest radiograph — cardiomegaly, pulmonary infiltrates, pulmonary nodule
- Electrocardiogram: Prolonged PR interval, nonspecific ST and T wave changes, arrhythmias, abnormal T wave changes, abnormal Q waves, low voltages
- Echocardiogram — 2D Echocardiogram is performed at day 14 of illness. Early 2D echocardiogram (before 14 days) may be performed as per the algorithm below. However, a normal echocardiogram does not exclude the diagnosis of KD as coronary artery changes rarely develop in the early phase.

» 2D echocardiogram may show coronary artery changes, mitral regurgitation/aortic and tricuspid regurgitation or pericardial effusion in the acute phase. Aortic dilation may be seen in a later phase.

» Classification of coronary artery aneurysms in the acute phase (day 14 or earlier) of Kawasaki disease:

□ Dilation: Z-score: ≥2 to <2.5
□ Small aneurysm: Z-score ≥2.5 to <5
□ Medium aneurysm: Z-score ≥5 to <10
□ Giant aneurysm: Z-score ≥10 or aneurysm showing an internal diameter ≥8 mm (in children ≥5 years).

- Other imaging modalities like Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) of the vascular tree or coronary angiography or Computed Tomography (CT) may be performed in selected patients.

# The Baby Bear Book

The following investigations are recommended in all patients with KD: FBC, CRP, ESR (note ESR cannot be used for trending after IVIG administration), Renal panel, LFT, ASOT, Urinalysis and culture, COVID-19 swab (in context of current pandemic), ECG (to be done before discharge).

## Incomplete Kawasaki Disease

When the clinical presentation is suggestive of KD but does not meet the full criteria, the diagnosis of incomplete KD requires further supportive investigations (Figure 7.21).

One should consider incomplete KD in:

- A child with fever for \(\geq 5\) days with 2 or 3 of the principal clinical features.
- Infants under 6 months of age with prolonged fever and irritability.
- Infants with prolonged fever and aseptic meningitis.
- Infants or children with prolonged fever and unexplained lymphadenitis, retro tonsillar collection or shock.

Incomplete KD can present a significant diagnostic dilemma; however, once the diagnosis is made, the treatment for incomplete KD is similar to complete KD. Infants and adolescents who present with an incomplete picture may be at a higher risk for cardiac complications since treatment may be delayed.

**Kawasaki disease shock syndrome (KDSS)** presents with haemodynamic instability, including hypotension or clinical signs of inadequate perfusion. Patients with KDSS are likely to have lymphadenopathy, coronary involvement and less identifiable foci in comparison to patients with toxic shock syndrome.

## Differential Diagnosis

The differential diagnosis in a child with possible KD, presenting with conjunctivitis and rash together with prolonged fever include:

- Infectious:

Streptococcal and staphylococcal toxin-mediated diseases (scarlet fever, toxic shock syndrome)
Measles, adenovirus infection and other viral infections
With epidemiologic risk factors:

Leptospirosis
Yersinia pseudotuberculosis infection (rare)
Rickettsia infection (rare in Singapore)

Non-infectious:

Drug reactions, Stevens-Johnson syndrome
Macrophage activating syndrome
» Autoimmune or immune complex diseases — Systemic Lupus Erythematosus (SLE), Juvenile Rheumatoid Arthritis (JRA), polyarteritis nodosa, Reiter's syndrome, Behcet's disease, inflammatory bowel disease, post-infectious

# The Baby Bear Book

## Clinical Course

The course of KD can be described in three clinical phases:

- Acute phase — lasting 7 to 14 days, characterised by fever and inflammatory changes.
- Subacute phase — typically lasts from approximately day 10 to day 25 after onset of illness. Fever, rash and lymphadenopathy resolve, but irritability, anorexia and conjunctival injection persist. Peeling of skin fingers and toes appears; may have arthritis and arthralgia or myocardial dysfunction. Thrombocytosis is common. Conjunctivitis may still be present. Coronary artery changes peak in this phase. In this phase, there is risk of sudden death.
- Convalescent phase — begins when all clinical signs disappear and continues until acute phase reactants return to normal, usually 4 to 10 weeks after onset. Risk of acute cardiovascular events in view of endothelial dysfunction.

A small subgroup of patients with KD (10 to 15%) have persistent or recrudescent disease after the initial treatment.

Up to 1 to 3% of patients may have relapses or recurrences of KD after recovering from the first episode.

## Acute Management

Treatment should be started for:

- All children meeting criteria for complete Kawasaki disease.
- All children with sufficient clinical criteria/laboratory criteria to suspect incomplete Kawasaki disease, excluding alternative diagnosis.

Treatment started within 10 days of the onset of symptoms and if possible, within 7 days, is most effective, but in presence of ongoing signs (and/or laboratory evidence) of inflammation, it can be initiated beyond 10 days. In general, IVIG is not prescribed for the patient with a history consistent with KD but in whom the fever has subsided for many days, as IVIG is unlikely to prevent coronary disease after the acute inflammation has subsided.

## First-line Treatment

- **Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG)**

Intravenous immunoglobulin is the primary treatment for patients with KD. Giving IVIG in the acute phase reduces the risk of coronary aneurysms by at least 3 to 5 fold. Note: IVIG reduces the risk but does NOT completely prevent development of coronary abnormalities. Dose: 2 g/kg as a single infusion. Infuse over 8 to 12 hours; start at a very low infusion rate and increase gradually. The infusion rate can be ordered as 0.5 ml/kg/h × 15 min, then 1.0 ml/kg/h × 15 min, then 2.0 ml/kg/h × 15 min, then 4.0 ml/kg/h till completion.

Monitoring during infusion

Temperature, pulse and respiration (TPR), BP Q5min × 3, then

TPR, BP Q10min × 3, then

TPR, BP Q15min × 3, then

TPR, BP Q30min × 3, then

b4854_Seo-07.indd 216

12-Dec-22 3:15:54 PM

# Kawasaki Disease

TPR, BP hourly till completion

The infusion site should also be monitored for extravasation during treatment.

- Complications and side effects include chills and rigors, hypotension, drug reactions including anaphylaxis, infections (blood product from pooled donors), aseptic meningitis, haemolytic anaemia (oral steroids may be required for Coombs positive haemolytic anaemia), abnormal liver function tests including jaundice, acute renal failure, thrombocytopenia, pulmonary oedema.

## Second-line Treatment

In approximately 10% of patients with KD, there is persistent or recrudescent of fever more than 36 hours after completion of initial IVIG infusion. The following options can be considered in such cases of IVIG failure/resistance as first line therapy:

- Repeat infusion of IVIG \(2\mathrm{g / kg}\) over 10 to 12 hours can be administered.
- Intravenous Methylprednisolone \(15 - 30\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) for 1-3 days with or without tapering steroids. Side effects are bradycardia, hypertension, hyperglycaemia and hypothermia. During administration, a close monitoring of vital signs, ECG and blood pressure is required.
- Oral prednisolone \(2\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) till defervescence, weaning over 2 to 3 week period.

More studies are required to establish the role of steroids as an adjunct in primary treatment or as rescue for KD patients with refractory or recrudescent KD.

## Other Options if Second IVIG/Steroids Fail

- Infliximab
Cyclosporine
Cyclophosphamide

## Antiplatelet Medications

# Aspirin

Aspirin is used for its anti-inflammatory and anti-pyretic effects in the acute phase, in a moderate to high dose, till they subside. Subsequently in the subacute and convalescent phases, the dose of aspirin is reduced to an antiplatelet dose. Low dose aspirin after the acute phase is probably more important when the risk of coronary artery thrombosis is higher; established coronary vasculitis occurs concomitantly with marked thrombocytosis and a hypercoagulable state.

Some studies show that anti-inflammatory doses of aspirin have no added benefit over and above the anti-inflammatory effect of IVIG. Therefore, it may be reasonable to use only antiplatelet low-dose (3–5 mg/kg/day) aspirin. Should a patient present late when acute inflammation has subsided, low dose aspirin can be started.

Aspirin therapy can be stopped after 6 to 8 weeks if the latest echocardiogram shows normal coronary arteries. In patients with persistent coronary abnormalities, aspirin is continued long term.

b4854 Sec-07.indd 217

12-Dec-22 3:15:54 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Dose: Acute phase — moderate dose (30–50 mg/kg/day) to high dose (80–100 mg/kg/day) until afebrile
» Subacute/convalescent phase — low dose 3–5 mg/kg/day (antiplatelet dose) as a single dose with meal.
» Complications of aspirin therapy — allergy, gastritis, gastrointestinal bleeding, chronic salicylism and Reye's syndrome.

# • Clopidogrel:

Clopidogrel is an alternative to patients who are resistant/allergic to aspirin. It is also given as an antiplatelet drug instead of aspirin in those with transaminitis. Clopidogrel can be converted back to aspirin when transaminitis resolves. In children who have contracted varicella while on aspirin or intending to have varicella vaccination, clopidogrel can be replaced for aspirin for 6 weeks. In children who have influenza with acute KD, clopidogrel can be also used instead of aspirin. Clopidogrel is also used as a dual anti-platelet agent (together with aspirin) for moderate aneurysms and as triple therapy for giant aneurysm with thrombosis.

» Dose: 0.2 mg/kg/day in children <24 months and 1 mg/kg/day in children >24 months as a single dose.
» Side effects: Thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, agranulocytosis and severe liver dysfunction may appear early. Others are toxic epidermal necrolysis, gastric ulcer.

# • Dipyridamole

Alternative to aspirin if taking ibuprofen/resistant/allergic to aspirin/risk of Reye syndrome.

# Anticoagulation/thrombolysis (in Consultation with Cardiologist/

# Haematologist):

- Low molecular weight heparin (LMWH)/heparin is initiated for rapidly expanding coronary artery aneurysms or large/giant aneurysms (>8 mm or Z-score of ≥10). This is dual therapy with low dose aspirin (antiplatelet). Refer to institutional anticoagulation guidelines for titration and target levels.
- For near occlusive/occlusive thrombus, to consider thrombolysis with recombinant tissue plasminogen activator or use of glycoprotein IIb/IIIa inhibitor, such as the monoclonal antibody abciximab as per the managing cardiologist's discretion.
- Subsequently for large/giant aneurysms coronary artery with thrombosis, triple therapy with aspirin and clopidogrel (2nd antiplatelet) and LMWH/Warfarin is recommended. Target INR when on warfarin is 2 to 3.

# Supportive Measures

- Maintain fluid balance closely.
- Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs can antagonise the antiplatelet effects of aspirin and is hence avoided.
- Consider need for lansoprazole/omeprazole if high dose aspirin or steroids are given.

# Kawasaki Disease

## Follow-Up of Patients with Kawasaki Disease

Long-term management is aimed at prevention of thrombosis and early detection of thrombosis or stenosis when they occur. This is dependent on the degree of coronary artery involvement and subsequent resolution.

|  Coronary artery description |   | Follow-up interval (from disease onset) | Anti-platelet / anti-coagulation | ECG 2D echocardiogram  |

## Other Measures

- Consider repeating FBC, LFT and CRP during follow up.
- All inactivated vaccines, rotavirus, oral typhoid, intranasal anti-flu, BCG vaccines and yellow fever vaccine may be administered at any time after administration of IVIG in KD patients.
- The MMR, Varicella and MMRV vaccines should be delayed for 11 months after the administration of IVIG in KD patients to avoid a reduced immune response as a result of the specific high antibody titre against the antigens contained in IVIG.
- Varicella-zoster vaccines and annual influenza vaccine are recommended for children on aspirin.
- No restriction in physical activities is recommended for children with normal coronary arteries post-KD. However, competitive sports, endurance training and strenuous exercise is discouraged for those with abnormal coronary arteries. Contact sports are contraindicated in children on anticoagulation.
- The need for further advanced cardiac imaging, for example, CT imaging of coronary arteries or cardiac MRI and stress electrocardiography/echocardiography is dependent on the coronary arterial risk and will be determined by the managing cardiologist.
- General measures to lower cardiovascular risk (such as lipid lowering, control of hypertension, smoking cessation) and support for patients and their families to pursue a healthy lifestyle (healthy diet, exercise and maintain ideal body weight) need to be emphasised. A lipid profile may be considered every 5 years.
- The use of statins in children \(>8\) years can be considered; however, there are no guidelines for target lipid levels for children with KD.
