# CHAPTER 30

# Normal Child Development

Tan Hui Yin, Jessica; Rachana Koura

# Introduction

The first years of development are crucial for lifelong learning and development. Milestones follow predictable courses, and later developmental skills build on previous ones achieved. Developmental screening should be done at every visit and a good understanding of normal development is crucial for the paediatrician to be able to recognise delayed/abnormal development promptly, for which early intervention would be beneficial. Studies show that beginning intervention earlier in a child's developmental course leads to improved outcomes and can improve engagement of a family in the child's developmental progress.

Parental concerns regarding development should always be addressed as early detection of delays can be picked up and acted upon. Conversely, just because a parent has no concerns does not mean that developmental issues do not exist. Using the tables provided below helps to provide a framework for assessing developmental milestones.

Children with abnormal development should be reviewed by a developmental paediatrician. Pre-schoolers with abnormal development should be referred to the Child Development Unit (CDU) at KKH or NUH. Primary school-aged children over 6 years should be referred to Child and Adolescent Mental Wellness Service (CAMWS) in KKH or the Child Guidance Clinic run by the Institute of Mental Health (IMH) for concerns of attention deficit disorder with or without hyperactivity or other mental health issues. School-going children should be referred to the educational psychologists at the Ministry of Education (MOE) if they require learning needs assessment and to the Dyslexia Association of Singapore for concerns of dyslexia.

# Terminology

- **Developmental screening** — Developmental screening refers to the use of a standardised test to identify asymptomatic children who are at risk for a developmental disorder; children who screen positive should undergo a developmental-behavioural evaluation.
- **Developmental surveillance** — Developmental surveillance is the process through which children who have developmental delay or are at risk for developmental delay are identified. Developmental surveillance occurs at preventive care visits and consists of eliciting parental concerns, identifying risk

224

b4854_Sec-08.indd 224

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

factors, monitoring development and collaborating with other providers and professionals.

## Benefits of Surveillance and Screening

The combination of surveillance and screening for developmental — behavioural problems in children increases early identification, enabling earlier intervention, which is associated with improved outcome.

The response to developmental–behavioural interventions is greatest in early childhood. Children who are identified after school entry miss the opportunity to participate in early developmental or early childhood services. Children with undetected developmental delays are at increased risk for social and emotional problems, early school problems and school failure.

## Approach to Developmental Screening

### History

#### Developmental History

- Milestones achieved in each of the main developmental areas and current level of functioning within each domain
  - Gross motor — Movements using large muscles
  - Fine motor — Movements using the hands and smaller muscles, often involving skills of daily living
  - Speech/language — Receptive and expressive communication, speech and non-verbal communication
  - Social-emotional and behavioural — Eye contact, joint attention, communicative intent, social appropriateness, attachment, play and interaction with others
  - Cognitive — Reasoning, memory and problem-solving skills
- Any developmental regression
- Atypical development, e.g. perseverations/obsessions/compulsions, rigidity and poor task transitioning, motor mannerisms, sensory issues, idiosyncratic language (echolalia, self-commentary, neologisms)
- Behaviour in different settings, e.g. home vs. school, any concerns from school teachers
- School performance, e.g. academic, behaviour, socialisation skills with peers, reports from teachers

#### Antenatal and Neonatal History

- Gestational age (e.g. prematurity and corrected age)
- Birth weight

b4854_Sec-08.indd 225

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

226

# The Baby Bear Book

- APGAR Score
- Perinatal events
- Cord thyroid-stimulating hormone level
- Inborn Errors of Metabolism (IEM) screen
- Intrauterine infection/maternal serology
- Maternal medical history — e.g. thyroid disorder/drug abuse

# Past medical History

- Chronic medical illness
- Co-morbid conditions, e.g. anxiety, depression, low self-esteem, self-harm, aggression
Significant head injury/meningitis/seizures
- Otitis media
- Hearing or visual impairment

# Dietary and Sleep Habits

- Fussy eaters/poor intake
- Screen time
- Obstructive sleep apnoea, poor sleep

# Drug History

- Various drugs can affect behaviour (e.g. steroids, beta-adrenergic receptor agonists, anticonvulsants, montelukast, methylxanthines)

# Social History

- Recent stressors, family dynamics, main caregiver(s), spoken language at home
- Environmental deprivation, domestic violence, child abuse, financial difficulty

# Family History

- Consanguinity
- Any developmental disorders, hearing impairment, seizures, neurological disorders, genetic/metabolic disorders, psychiatric illness

# Clinical Examination

- Assess systematically each of the main developmental areas (see Table 8.1)
- Growth parameters (weight, height, occipital-frontal circumference (OFC)) — review health booklet to look at growth trend
- Any dysmorphic features/neurocutaneous stigmata
- Systems examination
Full neurological examination (tone, reflexes, gait, cranial nerves, cerebellar) and examination of spine, hips
- Behavioural observations during consult

b4854_Sec-08.indd 226

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

# Investigations

Investigations should only be done when clinically indicated.

# Prematurity

The following guidelines apply to children born prematurely:

(i) For pre-term infants born before 35 weeks, it is recommended that hospital specialists or private paediatricians follow them up for growth monitoring and developmental screenings for the first 2 years of life, instead of at primary care. Clinicians should use the corrected age (i.e. chronological age minus the number of weeks the child was born early, taking 40 weeks as full term), for their growth monitoring and developmental screenings for the first 24 months.
(ii) For infants born between 35 and 37 weeks, growth monitoring should also be conducted using the corrected age till 24 months. However, developmental screening for infants born at or after 35 weeks should be based on chronological age.

# Developmental Domains

The main domains of development are:

1. Gross motor
2. Vision and fine motor
3. Hearing and speech and language
4. Personal social
5. Cognitive ability

- Normal: The normal milestones by age refer to the 50th percentile norms by which \(50\%\) of children have achieved them.
- Red flags (RF): RF refer to the 90th percentile cut off by which \(90\%\) of children have achieved them. The presence of 'red flags' is an indication of delay with a need for further evaluation.
- Assess the child by testing one domain first and finding the threshold between what the child can and cannot do.
- For each domain, the child's developmental level is determined by the highest level task achieved and the next level that was not achieved (i.e. if a child uses 2-word phrase but not a 3-word phrase then their speech level is at a 2-year level and less than a 3-year level).
- At any point in time of a child's development, if they lose skills they once had (regression), this is a 'red flag' and immediate referral for further evaluation should be made promptly.

# CHAPTER 31

# Developmental Disorders

Lourdes Mary Daniel; Wong Chui Mae

# Patterns of Abnormal Development

- Developmental Delay:
  » Development follows the normal sequence but is delayed.
  » May be a specific or global developmental delay.
- Developmental Disorder:
  » Development does not follow the normal pattern and is aberrant or bizarre, e.g. persistence of primitive reflexes, atypical developmental profiles (e.g. a child with autism who has language skills, but is unable to use it for social interaction and communication).
- Developmental Arrest or Regression:
  » A period of normal development is followed by a failure to acquire new skills or loss of previously established skills.
  » This may signify a serious underlying condition, e.g. neurodegenerative/metabolic disorder, Landau–Kleffner Syndrome, Rett Syndrome.

# An Approach to Developmental and Behavioural Disorders

A detailed history and physical examination are invaluable. Specialised investigations play only a minor role in the evaluation process.

# History

# Developmental and Behavioural History

- Gross and fine motor milestones
- Language milestones — Receptive, expressive and non-verbal
- Current functioning in each developmental domain
- Speech — Articulation, phonology and fluency
- Social-emotional awareness, e.g. eye contact, joint attention, communicative intent, social appropriateness, response to family members vs. strangers, etc.
- Play (Parten's social play stage, pretend play abilities)
- Behaviour in different settings, e.g. home vs. school
- Developmental regression

234

b4854_Sec-08.indd 234

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

# Developmental Disorders

- School performance, e.g. academics (including reading and handwriting) behaviour, socialisation skills with peers
- Atypical development, e.g. perseverations/obsessions/compulsions, rigidity and poor task transitioning, motor mannerisms, sensory issues, idiosyncratic language (echolalia, odd prosody, self-commentary, neologisms), etc.
- Reports from professionals working with the child, e.g. external therapists, teachers
- Co-morbid conditions, e.g. anxiety, depression, low self-esteem, self-harm, aggression
- Activities of daily living
- Early temperament in infancy, e.g. social responsiveness, feeding, sleeping, crying
- Dietary and sleep habits, e.g. nutritional deficiencies, growth problems, fussy feeding, obstructive sleep apnoea, poor sleep

# Antenatal and Neonatal History

- APGAR scores, prematurity, perinatal events, intrauterine infection, neonatal screening results (hearing, thyroid stimulating hormone, metabolic screening), maternal drugs, alcohol intake

# Medical History

- Chronic medical illness, seizures including absence seizures (staring spells), otitis media, significant head injury, unexplained febrile illnesses, hearing or visual impairment, etc.

# Drug History

- Drugs (e.g. steroids, beta-adrenergic receptor agonists, methylxanthines, anticonvulsants) that can affect behaviour

# Social History

- Recent stressors, family dynamics, main caregiver(s), spoken language at home, environmental deprivation, exposure to adverse childhood experiences (e.g. exposure to domestic violence, parental incarceration or drug abuse, child abuse, etc.), financial difficulty

# Family History

- Consanguinity, developmental delays or disorders, including autism and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD), hearing impairment, seizures, neurological disorders, genetic/metabolic disorders, psychiatric illnesses

# Clinical Examination

- Growth parameters — Weight, height, occipito-frontal circumference (OFC)
- Dysmorphic features
- Neurocutaneous stigmata

b4854_Sec-08.indd 235

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

# *The Baby Bear Book*

- Examination of systems
- Full neurological examination (tone, reflexes, gait, cranial nerves, cerebellar signs)
- Spine, hips
- Demonstration of current development, including reading, numeracy and handwriting, at appropriate ages
- Observations including behaviour, interaction and play

## Investigations

Investigations should only be done when clinically indicated. Some investigations that could be considered include:

- Hearing assessment (probably the most useful and essential in any child with language delay)
- Visual assessment
- Thyroid function test (especially in global developmental delay with growth problems)
- Muscle enzymes, urea/electrolytes/glucose, calcium/magnesium
- Inborn Errors of Metabolism (IEM) study or metabolic screen
- Karyotyping, chromosomal microarray analysis, Fragile X analysis
- TORCH screen (in children under 2 years)
- Neuroimaging (Ultrasound/CT/MRI head)
- Electroencephalogram (EEG)

## Red Flags that Warrant Refferal

1. Developmental regression at any age*
2. Not meeting the 90th centile developmental milestones in the child health booklet
3. Persistently low tone or floppiness*
4. Asymmetry of movements or other features suggestive of cerebral palsy, such as increased muscle tone*
5. Concerns about vision: lack of fixing and following from 6 weeks onwards**
6. Concerns about hearing: no response to sounds^
7. No social smile by 3 months
8. Unable to roll and reach out for objects at 6 months
9. Unable to walk independently at 18 months
10. No babbling, pointing or use of gestures by 12 months
11. No imitation of actions or words at 2 years
12. No use of spontaneous (non-echoed/non initiated) phrases by 24 months
13. Persistent tiptoe gait

## Some Specific Conditions

### Speech and Language Delay

Speech and language are components of communication. All children presenting with a language delay should be screened for autistic features and have a hearing test performed.

- Language is a system of symbolic representation used to communicate meanings, feelings, ideas and intentions.
» Receptive language (language processing) refers to auditory processing and comprehension of language.
» Expressive language (language production) refers to non-verbal gestures, vocabulary, phrase length, semantics (appropriate use of single words, phrases and sentences), and syntax (arrangements of words in sentences, clauses and phrases).
» Pragmatic language refers to the appropriate use of verbal and non-verbal language in a given situation.
- Speech is the expression of language in the verbal mode.
» Speech disorders include phonological and articulation difficulties and dysfluency (e.g. stuttering).
- Children frequently present with one of the following:
» Expressive language delay
» Mixed receptive-expressive language delay
» Phonological delay/dysfluency
- Management and prognosis of speech and language delay:
» 70–80% of children with isolated expressive language delay do well with Speech and Language Therapy (SLT) and achieve normal language abilities, especially if detected early.
» Children with mixed language delays show slower response to SLT, especially if there is an underlying cause such as Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD). These children may need ongoing SLT support during primary school years.
» Children with phonological delay or dysfluency tend to also improve with SLT. For children with associated tongue-tie (ankyloglossia), a frenotomy may not be indicated unless there is limited improvement with SLT input.
» Speech and Language intervention can also be provided as part of the Individualised Education Plan for children in an Early Intervention Programme.

b4854_Sec-08.indd 237

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

## Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD)

The core clinical features of ASD comprise persistent deficits in social communication and social interaction, with restricted and repetitive patterns of behaviour, including sensory issues. ASD has an incidence of approximately 1 in 100 children and is four times more common in boys than girls. The risk of autism in a sibling of an autistic child is 10–20%.

### Characteristics

#### Persistent Deficits in Social Communication and Social Interaction

1. Deficits in social–emotional reciprocity
2. Deficits in non-verbal communicative behaviours used for social interaction
3. Deficits in developing, maintaining and understanding relationships

#### Restricted, Repetitive Patterns of Behaviour

1. Stereotyped or repetitive motor movements, use of objects or speech.
2. Insistence on sameness, inflexible adherence to routines or ritualised patterns of behaviour.
3. Highly restricted, fixated interests.
4. Hyper- or hypo-reactivity to sensory input or unusual interest in sensory aspects of the environment.

## M-CHAT

The Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers (M-CHAT) is a validated screening tool to identify toddlers at risk for ASD who may warrant an early developmental assessment (https://m-chat.org/). It should only be used for children aged 16–30 months. If the child's score is 3 or more, the child should be referred for further evaluation.

### Diagnosis and Management

The diagnosis of ASD is made following a detailed autism-specific interview, observation of the child in the clinic setting, information obtained from the preschool or school, and a direct assessment using formal structured assessment tools.

Interventions for ASD include Speech and Language therapy, Occupational therapy, Structured Teaching, Picture Exchange Communication System (PECS), Applied Behaviour Analysis (ABA), etc. Preschool children with suspected or confirmed ASD can be referred to the Early Intervention Programme for Infants and Children (EIPIC). Medication has no place in the regular management of ASD except in certain children who have concurrent ADHD or severe behavioural difficulties. Various diets (gluten-free, casein-free), biomedical therapies and nutritional supplements have not been found to make a difference in well-conducted randomised controlled trials.

## Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (ADHD)

The diagnosis of ADHD is based primarily on reports of characteristic inattention and hyperactivity/impulsivity behaviours from various observers, occurring in different

b4854_Sec-08.indd 238

12-Dec-22 11:00:10 AM

# Developmental Disorders

settings, over an extended period of time (at least 6 months). The symptoms should result in impairment in social and academic functioning in two or more settings (e.g. at home and at school) and should also be present before the age of 12. However, when considering a diagnosis of ADHD, it is important to remember that inattention, hyperactivity and impulsivity are common till 4 years of age.

ADHD has an estimated incidence of 1 in 10–20 children, but there is a concern of over-diagnosis. Often, other factors may result in a young child behaving as though they have ADHD, but once these are addressed, the child’s behaviour may return to normal. These factors include poor sleep, lack of a structure in the household, deprivation, language delay, learning disability, hearing impairment, a major life event (parents divorcing, family bereavement), thyroid disease or subclinical seizures.

## Behavioural Features of ADHD Include

- Inattention, e.g. the child is easily distracted, is careless or forgetful, fails to complete tasks, does not follow through on instructions, has difficulty organising tasks, loses things necessary for activities, does not listen when spoken to directly, etc.
- Hyperactivity, e.g. the child fidgets, will not sit still, runs or climbs excessively in inappropriate situations, has difficulty engaging in leisure activities quietly, is often 'on the go', talks excessively, etc.
- Impulsivity, e.g. the child blurts out answers before questions have been completed, has difficulty waiting for turns, often interrupts or is intrusive, etc.

## ADHD has Three Sub-classifications

- Combined type (inattentive/hyperactive/impulsive): Children with this type of ADHD show all three behaviours. This is the most common form of ADHD.
- Predominantly hyperactive/impulsive type: Children show both hyperactive and impulsive behaviour, but for the most part, they are able to pay attention.
- Predominantly inattentive type (formerly known as attention-deficit disorder \(\{\mathrm{ADD}\}\)): These children are not overly hyperactive. They do not disrupt the classroom or other activities, so their symptoms might not be noticed. This subtype often affects girls rather than boys.

## Diagnosis and Management

- A child or adolescent should meet the DSM-5 criteria for ADHD before being given the diagnosis, with impairment in more than one major setting (i.e. social, academic or occupational) with information obtained primarily from reports of parents/guardians, teachers, other school personnel and health clinicians who are involved in the care of the child. Alternative causes of the behaviours should be excluded.
- Comorbid disorders must be screened for, including emotional or behavioural conditions (e.g. anxiety, depression, etc.), developmental conditions (e.g. learning and language disorders, ASD) and physical conditions (e.g. sleep apnoea and tics).
- In the preschool child, DSM-based rating scales should be used to obtain information.

b4854_Sec-08.indd 239

12-Dec-22 11:00:11 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

- There are several rating scales and questionnaires that can be useful for assessment. These include the Vanderbilt and Du Paul ADHD rating scales (for core ADHD symptoms) and the Child Behaviour Checklist.
- In preschool children, behavioural therapy (both at home and in the classroom) is the first-line treatment. Methylphenidate should only be considered if a child shows limited response to behavioural therapy with ongoing impairment in the child's function.
- In primary school children, medications (both stimulant and non-stimulant) can be prescribed at doses that can achieve maximum effect with minimum side effects. But it is still highly recommended that it be used concurrently with behavioural therapy. The school should also be involved in the treatment plan.
- In secondary school children, treatment would be the same as for primary school children but should be undertaken with the assent of the adolescent.

## Global Developmental Delay (GDD)

Global developmental delay is defined as significant delay in two or more major domains of development (gross motor or fine motor skills, language, cognition, personal and social, activities of daily living) in a child younger than 5 years.

### Causes

In up to half of cases, the cause is not known. The common causes of GDD are:

- Genetic conditions, e.g., Down syndrome, Fragile X syndrome, Di George syndrome, Klinefelter's syndrome, neurofibromatosis, Williams syndrome, Angelman syndrome and Prader-Willi syndrome. Congenital hypothyroidism, phenylketonuria (PKU), other inborn errors of metabolism and other less common syndromes should also be considered.
- Antenatal or perinatal complications, e.g. foetal alcohol syndrome, congenital infections, neonatal encephalopathy
- Prematurity
- Severe refractory epilepsy
- Acquired brain injury, e.g. from trauma, meningitis, encephalitis, heavy metal poisoning
Visual or hearing loss
- Environmental causes, e.g. severe under-stimulation, maltreatment, malnutrition
- Children with ASD often have co-existing GDD, but their primary diagnosis would still be ASD. Conversely, some children with severe GDD or intellectual delay may behave as though they have ASD (with stereotypical or repetitive behaviours) and careful delineation of the correct diagnosis must be made.

### Diagnosis and Management

Children with GDD need to be referred for the appropriate therapies in the delayed domains of development (e.g. Speech, Physio, Occupational or Educational therapy, psychological support). As they often need help from more than one allied health professional, most children with GDD benefit from early referral to the EIPIC centres

b4854_Sec-08.indd 240

12-Dec-22 11:00:11 AM

# Developmental Disorders

in Singapore. The developmental paediatrician would also screen the child for any underlying cause, perform the relevant investigations, and treat the cause, if found.

If the GDD persists, some children may require cognitive testing (IQ testing) or other school readiness assessments prior to primary school entry to guide appropriate school placement and determine the need for support in mainstream school.

## Developmental Coordination Disorder (DCD)

Children with developmental coordination disorder have difficulties with motor coordination, which presents differently according to the age of the child. Their coordinated motor skills are substantially below that expected for the age of the child and the opportunities for learning. They may have the following difficulties:

- Problems with sucking and swallowing during the first year of life
- Delays in sitting up, crawling and walking
- Gross motor incoordination (e.g. jumping, hopping or standing on one foot)
- Fine motor incoordination (e.g. writing, using scissors, tying shoelaces, buttoning)
- Clumsiness

In addition to the physical impairments, DCD is often associated with problems with memory and task-sequencing. This typically results in difficulty remembering instructions, difficulty organising one's time and remembering deadlines, increased propensity to lose things or problems carrying out tasks which require remembering several steps in sequence (such as cooking). Children with DCD are also at risk of low self-esteem.

However, DCD rarely occurs alone — it often co-exists with dyslexia, ADHD and language impairment.

## Diagnosis and Management

The diagnosis is made by synthesis of the developmental and medical history, physical and neurological examination, reports from school and appropriate psychometric assessment, usually by an occupational therapist. The symptoms must begin in the early developmental period. There must be significant impact of the child's condition on his/her day-to-day activities such as the child's performance in school, activities at play, or other activities of daily living. Also, the child's problems with motor coordination cannot result from general medical conditions, such as muscular dystrophy, and cannot result directly from having intellectual disability.

Physical education and perceptual motor training are the best interventions for DCD. Using a computer to take notes may help children who have trouble writing. The non-motor problems associated with DCD would require a more structured learning environment. Co-existing developmental problems need to be screened for and appropriate assessment and intervention provided as needed.

## Dyslexia

Dyslexia is a type of specific learning difficulty identifiable as a neurodevelopmental disorder. It primarily affects the skills involved in accurate and fluent reading and spelling.

b4854_Sec-08.indd 241

12-Dec-22 11:00:11 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

Characteristic features of dyslexia are difficulties in phonological awareness (the ability to hear, identify and manipulate the units of sounds in speech), verbal memory (the ability to remember what is read and heard) and processing speed (the speed of performing simple mental operations). Co-occurring difficulties may be seen in aspects of language, motor coordination, mental calculation, concentration and personal organisation, but these are not, by themselves, markers of dyslexia. It is also a comorbidity of ADHD.

Symptoms of dyslexia can include difficulties in:

- Reading (e.g. reading below age level, hesitant or effortful reading, difficulty in recognising familiar or high frequency words, skipping words or lines, complaining of words or lines of text on a page seeming to move, even with normal standard eye examinations, etc.)
- Comprehension (e.g. difficulty remembering, understanding or extracting important points from text passages)
- Spelling (e.g. spelling below age level, spelling the same word in several different ways, confusing similar sounding words, ['one' and 'won'], reversing letter sequences ['was' and 'saw'], etc.)

In addition, children with dyslexia can also show difficulties in:

- Writing (e.g. messy/badly organised work, difficulty copying, having poor writing skills compared to oral skills, being unable to write down a word even when the letters are dictated, etc.)
- Short-term and/or verbal working memory (e.g. being able to learn and understand how to do a task, but requiring frequent reminders before remembering to do it, having difficulty remembering multiple-step instructions, having excellent long-term memory for movies, experiences, locations and faces, but poor memory for sequences or unfamiliar facts and information)
- Sequencing (e.g. difficulty in reciting the alphabet/numbers, months of the year, days of the week or remembering/executing a list of instructions, not being able to give a good verbal account of events in their correct order)
- Time/Mathematics (e.g. difficulties in telling and managing time, counting, doing calculations using mathematical signs \(\{= , - , < \}\), doing mathematical word problems despite having ability in mathematical numerical operations, mental calculations)

## Diagnosis and Management

The diagnosis of dyslexia should be made through a comprehensive psycho-educational assessment using standardised tests. Longitudinal progress monitoring data measuring response to intervention can also be used. Comorbidities should be actively sought for, so that holistic management can be planned. Early signs of learning difficulties may appear in the preschool years (e.g. difficulty learning names of letters or counting objects), but dyslexia can only be diagnosed reliably after starting formal education.

b4854_Sec-08.indd 242

12-Dec-22 11:00:11 AM

Children with dyslexia will benefit from educational support during their school years. The two main providers of dyslexia intervention are the Ministry of Education (MOE) that runs the School Dyslexia Remediation Program in Primary 3 and Primary 4, and the Dyslexia Association of Singapore, which has an intervention program starting in Primary 1. MOE also runs a Learning Support Programme in every school for children in Primary 1–2 with literacy delay (including dyslexia). Intervention is also provided by some social service agencies and by trained dyslexia teachers in the private sector.

## Common Concomitant Problems in Children with Special Needs and their Families

- • Visual and hearing impairment — minimise any impairment to optimise the child's learning.
- • Epilepsy — ensure appropriateness and compliance of treatment.
- • Orthopaedic problems — physiotherapy and aids to optimise posture and mobility, prevention or reduction of skeletal deformity may help to maximise ability and function.
- • Chest infections — provide good parental education about symptoms and easy access to medical care.
- • Feeding problems — oromotor incoordination, gastroesophageal reflux and tube feeding need to be managed.
- • Bladder and bowel problems — enuresis, encopresis, constipation may affect the functional ability of the child.
- • Behavioural and psychiatric concerns — these may need behavioural support, psychiatric assessment or medication.
- • Social issues — parents and the home environment need to be supported through referrals to social workers, counsellors and community support organisations.
- • Emotional or mental health concerns in the parents — counselling or psychiatric support may be needed.

## Transition to the Next Level of Education

Transition points (from preschool to mainstream primary or special school and from primary to secondary school) are important milestones in the journey of the child with developmental, behavioural or learning difficulties and need to be managed according to the needs of the child. Decisions on appropriate school placements, deferment and identifying parental preferences may be necessary. The need for support in school should be determined and communicated to the school, with parental consent. When children transit from EIPIC to a mainstream school, reassessment of the child may be needed to understand the ongoing therapy needs of the child.

# CHAPTER 32

# Child Abuse and Neglect

Wong Choong Yi, Peter

### What is Child Abuse?

'Child abuse' or 'child maltreatment', as defined by the World Health Organization (WHO),

1. includes all types of physical and/or emotional ill-treatment, sexual abuse, neglect, negligence and commercial or other exploitation,
2. which results in actual or potential harm to the child's health, survival, development or dignity,
3. in the context of a relationship of responsibility, trust or power.

Child abuse may involve actions and words that are harmful, or the **absence** of expected actions or words that are protective or nurturing (neglect). Actions may be abusive, whether or not there is **intention** to cause harm: the definition hinges instead on whether harm occurs or may potentially occur. Perpetrators of abuse are persons in a position of responsibility or power over the child or whom the child trusts (a parent, hired caregiver, teacher, etc.).

Public and professional opinions contribute to determining whether an act is regarded as abusive. For example, as public opinion shifts, a growing list of countries have prohibited the corporal punishment of children (including caning and spanking). An evidence-based approach is helpful: what has reliable research shown about benefits and harms to a child arising from any given practice?

### The Scale of the Problem

A 2018 systematic review reported that 12.0% and 27.0% of girls and boys, respectively, in Europe have experienced physical abuse, as compared to 50.8% and 60.2% of girls and boys, respectively, in Africa. Reasons for the wide variation include differing cultural beliefs and laws. UNICEF estimates that about three-quarters of all children between the ages of 2 and 4, or around 300 million children worldwide, are regularly subjected to violent discipline by their caregivers. Worldwide, 1 in 5 women and 1 in 13 men report having been sexually abused before the age of 18. The vast majority of child abuse cases worldwide are undetected.

### Consequences of Child Abuse

Child abuse and neglect is a significant cause of childhood injuries, disabilities and death.

248

b4854_Sec-09.indd 248

12-Dec-22 11:00:34 AM

# Child Abuse and Neglect

Apart from causing immediate physical injuries, child abuse in its various forms also gives rise to problems in growth, development, physical health and psychological well-being. Sexual abuse may cause sexually transmitted infections and unintended pregnancies. The impact of child abuse may be long-term, lifelong or even intergenerational: victims may in turn be abusive towards future children in their care.

### Adverse Childhood Experiences

Adverse childhood experiences (ACEs) are events or circumstances in the first 18 years of life that are stressful or potentially traumatic:

**Abuse** — 1. physical abuse, 2. emotional abuse, 3. sexual abuse

**Neglect** — 4. physical neglect, 5. emotional neglect

**Household instability** — 6. parental separation or divorce, 7. intimate partner violence against the mother, 8. mental illness in a household member, 9. substance abuse by a household member, 10. incarceration of a household member

The first 5 categories are various forms of child abuse and neglect, and the other 5 frequently co-occur with child abuse and neglect.

ACEs are associated with:

1. Problems **in childhood and adolescence**, such as developmental delay, failure to thrive, recurrent infections, asthma and obesity.
2. Higher rates **in adult life subsequently** of anxiety, depression, suicide attempts, alcoholism, drug abuse, unsafe sex, coronary artery disease, asthma, diabetes, arthritis, autoimmune disease, cerebrovascular disease, cancer and early death.
3. Poorer outcomes in education, employment and income.

The association of ACEs with organic diseases and early death may be accounted for by a combination of neuroendocrine responses to stress, epigenetic changes (DNA methylation and telomere shortening), reprogramming of immune regulatory systems, neurodevelopmental disruption and the impact of risk behaviours.

Victims of child abuse are thus at increased risk of myriad health problems, lifelong disadvantages and shorter life expectancy. Halting abuse and preventing further ACEs will add years to life and life to years for these children.

### Physical Child Abuse: Overview

Physical child abuse may cause various injuries, including bruises, burns, fractures and traumatic brain injury. However, physically abused children may also present without any physical injuries.

The term 'non-accidental injury' (NAI) may be used to refer to a physical injury resulting from child abuse. It is not always an 'intentional injury': for example, a parent may have meant to discipline and not injure the child.

Table 9.1 Red Flags in History

|  Features in the History that are Concerning for Child Abuse — 6 Ds  |
| --- |
|  1. No, inadequate or vague explanation for a serious injury: 'Don't know how he was injured'  |
|  2. Medically implausible explanation given for the injury: Don't see how that mechanism could cause this injury  |
|  3. Explanation given is discordant with the Developmental age of the child  |
|  4. Discordant explanations from Different witnesses  |
|  5. Discordant explanations from the same witness at Different times  |
|  6. Delay in seeking medical attention despite the child being in pain or distress  |

**Recognising possible physical child abuse** requires careful history taking and thorough physical examination, paired with knowledge about what findings are concerning for abuse.

Physical child abuse is often an ongoing process, with recurrence of abuse in up to 70% of cases. A quarter of abused infants have, at previous medical encounters, presented with **sentinel injuries** (most commonly bruises, intra-oral injuries and fractures), which are injuries observed by medical professionals but not initially recognised to be abusive in nature. Recognising and reporting abusive injuries at the first instance may save an infant or child from further, even fatal, abuse.

When an injured child is brought to medical attention, where possible, obtain an account from both the child and the parent/responsible caregiver. Ask them to describe in detail the events surrounding each injury, allowing them to provide an account without interruptions, before asking clarifying questions.

Good documentation practices include clearly recording who gave the account(s), whether the parent was present when a child gave an account, any change in the accounts given over time (instead of just 'correcting' the record), and appropriate use of verbatim quotations (e.g. when a child refers to an action or a body part with colloquial terms).

**Red flags in the history** include inadequate, implausible or inconsistent accounts of the mechanism of injury and delay in presentation.

An injury attributed to a sibling or a pet is concerning. An incidental injury, noticed when a child presents for another complaint, should also receive due consideration. Recurring injuries attributed to accidents should prompt enquiry about any unsafe circumstances or environments.

When abuse is suspected, examination must include undressing the child to look for other injuries, paying particular attention to the scalp, ears, neck, palms, soles, trunk, buttocks and genitalia, and also examining for intra-oral injuries (e.g. a torn lip frenulum or bruising of oral mucosa).

## Physical Child Abuse: Bruises

Bruises are the most common type of injury caused by physical child abuse. However, most bruises in children are accidental, not abusive. Differentiating abusive from accidental

b4854_Sec-09.indd 250

12-Dec-22 11:00:34 AM

# Child Abuse and Neglect

Table 9.2 Features of Bruises that are Concerning for Abuse

|  Age/mobility of the child | - Bruising in an infant aged <9 mth: 'bruising before cruising' - Or, 'TEN4' rule:     - bruising on the Torso, Ear or Neck in a child aged ≤4 yr     - any bruising in an infant aged ≤4 mth - Bruising in a child who is not independently mobile (e.g. due to cerebral palsy)  |
| --- | --- |
|  Pattern of bruising | - Clustering of bruises - Multiple bruises of similar shape and size - Multiple bruises at different stages of healing - Imprints/bruises resembling the shape of an object (e.g. clothes hanger, belt, teeth) - 'Tram track' paired linear bruises (from a rod-shaped object) - Bilateral circumferential bruises on the wrists and ankles (signs of ligature)  |
|  Location of bruises | - Bruises away from bony prominences - Bruises on the face, ears, neck, abdomen, back and buttocks  |

bruises requires careful consideration of explanations given by caregivers, witnesses and the verbal child; of the child's age and mobility; and of features such as the location (Figures 9.1 and 9.2), shape, number and pattern of the bruises. When there is uncertainty, underlying medical conditions (such as haemophilia) may need to be excluded as the reason for bruises.

It is NOT possible for physicians to reliably estimate the **age of a bruise** based on colour or the presence of swelling or tenderness.

Evaluation for bleeding disorders with a full blood count and coagulation studies may be necessary in a child with unexplained bruising or bruises attributed to abuse. These tests may however be omitted where a diagnosis of abuse is clear from accounts obtained and the bruising pattern and where features of bleeding disorders are absent.

## Physical Child Abuse: Fractures

Fractures are the second most common type of injury caused by physical child abuse, after bruises. Most fractures in children are accidental, but the younger the child, the higher the possibility that a fracture is caused by abuse.

Whilst certain fracture types are highly concerning for child abuse, no one fracture type is exclusively caused by either abuse or by accidents (see Table 9.3). Whether the explanation offered is congruent with the fracture sustained is important. A single linear skull fracture in a toddler 'punched by his brother' would be more concerning for abuse than multiple rib fractures in a toddler with a crushing chest injury from a road traffic accident.

**Classic metaphyseal lesions** (CMLs), also described as 'metaphyseal corner fractures' or 'bucket-handle fractures', occur almost exclusively in infants and toddlers under 2 years old. CMLs are planar fractures through the subphyseal metaphyses (next to growth plates) at the ends of long bones. They may be caused by traction, torsion or shear forces generated by to-and-fro shaking or by pulling or twisting of the infant's limbs.

Osteogenesis imperfecta, osteopenia of prematurity, vitamin D deficiency rickets, renal bone disease and other medical conditions may present with multiple or recurrent fractures. These conditions can usually be differentiated from child abuse on the basis of clinical and radiological features and laboratory investigations as needed.

A **skeletal survey** is a series of radiographs that collectively show all the bones of the body, performed to look for clinically unsuspected (occult) fractures (see Table 9.4).

## Physical Child Abuse: Thermal Injuries

Thermal injuries are found in about 10% of abused children. Two-thirds of abusive thermal injuries are scalds. Second most common are burns inflicted by contact with hot objects, such as a clothes iron, heated cutlery, hair curler/straightener, or cigarettes. Whilst most scalds in childhood are accidental, abuse or neglect account for more than 10% of childhood scalds.

| --- | --- |

Table 9.4 Indications for Obtaining a Skeletal Survey

- All children \(< 2\) yr old with obvious abusive injuries
- All children \(< 2\) yr old with suspicious injuries, including:
  - bruises in non-ambulatory infants
  - oral injuries in non-ambulatory infants
  - injuries inconsistent with history provided
- Infants and young toddlers with unexplained intracranial injuries (including haemorrhage and hypoxic-ischaemic injury)
- Infants with unexplained, unexpected sudden death (consult with coroner first)
- An abused child's sibling/household member who is \(< 2\) yr old
- A twin of an abused infant or toddler

**Accidental scalds** are most commonly caused by spills when a child pulls a container of hot liquid off a table top or stove; most often scalding the face, neck or upper body. Typically, the margins are irregular, with splash patterns or flow lines, variable burn depth across scalded areas, and asymmetrical distribution (Figures 9.3 and 9.4).

In contrast, **intentional scalds**, which are most commonly caused by **forced immersion** into hot water, often produce clear proximal margins or 'tidal lines', 'glove' or 'stocking' scalds of one or more limbs, uniform scald depth, symmetrical distribution on both sides of the body, and skin fold sparing. Intentional scalds most often occur on the lower body without head or neck involvement. The most commonly affected areas are the legs, buttocks, genitals and perineum (Figures 9.5 and 9.6).

**Abusive contact burns** inflicted with a **hot object** tend to have sharper demarcations than accidental contact burns, and are more likely to affect multiple sites, often the face and sometimes the back of a child's body. These abusive burns often clearly reflect the shape of the causative object. In contrast, accidental contact burns tend to be single, and usually occur on the palms or the front of a child's body.

# Child Abuse and Neglect

Abuse inflicted with a **lighted cigarette** typically causes **sharply demarcated circular burns** around 1 cm in diameter, often multiple and uniform, sometimes on parts of the body normally covered by clothing. Bullous impetigo is an important differential diagnosis. In contrast, accidental contact with a lighted cigarette tends to cause single, more superficial, less sharply demarcated and non-circular burns, on usually unclothed areas.

## Physical Child Abuse: Abusive Head Trauma

Abusive Head Injury, or Abusive Head Trauma (AHT), is the foremost cause of death from child abuse.

AHT refers to a constellation of injuries to the head and its contents (including the brain and eyes) caused by the directed application of force to an infant or young child. The forces involved may include acceleration/deceleration forces (such as is caused by shaking), blunt force trauma, penetrating trauma and asphyxiation. The injuries often include a 'triad' of subdural haemorrhage (SDH), cerebral oedema and retinal haemorrhages. The absence of this triad does not exclude AHT.

The term 'Shaken Baby Syndrome' is often used in media to refer to AHT, but should be avoided in medical communication and documentation, as it presumes the specific and seldom proven mechanism of shaking.

Infants and toddlers with AHT often present with non-specific symptoms: drowsiness, irritability, poor feeding or vomiting. Up to 50% present with seizures. In most cases, a history of trauma is denied, or the explanation given is incongruous with the presentation. Scalp haematomas and externally visible injuries are often absent. Increased head circumference and signs of raised intracranial pressure (e.g. bulging fontanelles) may be present.

Neuroimaging may reveal acute or chronic subdural haemorrhage/haemorrhages (in >70% of cases), subarachnoid haemorrhage, cerebral ischaemia, cerebral oedema or diffuse axonal injury. Skull fractures may be absent. Complex skull fractures occur more often in AHT compared to non-abusive head trauma, whereas the opposite is true for linear skull fractures.

Evaluation by an ophthalmologist reveals retinal haemorrhages in up to 85% of cases. Retinal haemorrhages are strongly associated with AHT (odds ratio of 28 favouring abuse over non-abuse, in a 2012 meta-analysis by Piteau *et al*). Skeletal survey often reveals associated metaphyseal fractures and rib fractures.

Mortality in AHT ranges from 11% to 35%, and 45% to 90% of survivors have permanent neurological sequelae: most commonly, visual deficits, hearing impairment and cognitive impairment.

## Child Sexual Abuse

Although they overlap, **sexual assault** and **sexual abuse** are not exactly the same thing.

**Sexual assault** has taken place when a person has been involved in a sexual act **without his or her consent**. From a legal point of view, a child *cannot* consent to a sexual act. Engaging in sexual activity with a person **under 16 years of age** is a criminal offence in Singapore, even if the child agreed to the activity. Accordingly, the police may refer anyone under 16 years old, who may have engaged in sexual intercourse, to the paediatric hospital for forensic evaluation. On our part, medical professionals have a legal obligation to notify the police if we suspect that any patient under 16 years old has participated in sexual activity (even if it was consensual and between two young persons of the same age).

**Sexual abuse** refers to a child or young person being **exploited** for sexual gratification. Often, the perpetrator is someone who has authority or influence over the victim, or who has the victim's trust (e.g. a parent, sibling, relative, teacher or sports coach) or someone regarded by the victim as a romantic partner, but who is significantly older than the victim. Nine out of ten perpetrators are male.

The perpetrator may have engaged in **sexual grooming**, e.g. by giving gifts, showing favour, providing emotional support, encouraging interest in sexual acts, or persuading the child or young person that such acts are normal or loving.

Whilst touching or penetrative sex may be involved, sexual abuse does not always entail physical contact. Indecent exposure, showing pornography, recording or asking for indecent images or videos, and exchanging messages of a sexual nature ('sexting') with a young person, are also forms of sexual abuse.

Sexual abuse may come to be suspected or uncovered through:

- Disclosure to a family member, friend, teacher or school counsellor.
- Disclosure to a medical professional during clinical encounters.
- Diagnosis of a sexually transmitted infection.
- Diagnosis of pregnancy.
- Discovery of concerning communication, photographs or recordings on electronic devices or social media.
- Developmentally inappropriate sexual behaviours observed by a parent or professional.

Non-leading, open-ended questions are appropriate when asking a child or adolescent about possible sexual abuse. Prompt referral, even if the abuse was not recent, is necessary. A child's first detailed account of abuse is often the most forensically intact and should ideally be obtained by trained interviewers. Repeating the account may be distressing. Thus, many victims of intra-familial sexual abuse in Singapore are now interviewed jointly by the police, Child Protective Service and a paediatrician.

In Singapore, **forensic examination** for suspected sexual assault or sexual abuse in children and young persons is performed by gynaecologists for female victims and paediatric surgeons for male victims.

The forensic examination includes head-to-toe examination for injuries, anogenital examination and the obtaining of forensic specimens depending on the timing and type

b4854_Sec-09.indd 256

12-Dec-22 11:00:42 AM

# *Child Abuse and Neglect*

257

of sexual contact. Swabs from body surfaces and cavities may be obtained to test for semen, spermatozoa and DNA. Blood and urine samples for toxicology are obtained if drug-facilitated sexual assault is suspected.

This forensic examination is time-sensitive: after sexual intercourse, semen is rarely present in the oral cavity beyond 6 hours, anorectal cavity beyond 24 hours and vaginal cavity beyond 72 hours. Unfortunately, many victims present to medical care long after abuse has taken place. When forensic examination takes place more than 3 to 5 days after sexual intercourse, the collection of forensic specimens may reasonably be omitted. Normal forensic examination does not rule out sexual assault or sexual abuse.

Regardless of time since the alleged sexual abuse/assault, the victim should be tested for sexually transmitted infections (STIs). Locally, these tests comprise serology for HIV, Hepatitis B, Hepatitis C and *Treponema pallidum*; first-catch urine for *Chlamydia trachomatis* and *Neisseria gonorrhoeae* PCR; vaginal swab DNA hybridisation probe test (VP3) for *Candida*, *Gardnerella vaginalis* and *Trichomonas vaginalis*, and occasionally cultures where appropriate.

After specimen collection for STI tests, post-exposure prophylaxis for HIV and Hepatitis B, as well as presumptive antibiotic therapy for *Neisseria gonorrhoeae*, *Chlamydia trachomatis* and *Trichomonas vaginalis* (usually IM ceftriaxone, PO azithromycin and PO metronidazole) are offered if indicated, based on risk assessment.

For adolescent females presenting acutely after a sexual assault, the gynaecologist may offer emergency contraception, after first testing for pregnancy.

In cases where the alleged perpetrator is a household member, it is urgent and important to consider the safety and suitability of the victim remaining at home. Even if the alleged perpetrator has been taken into police custody, there are other concerns, such as how interaction with family members may affect the integrity of forensic history. Immediate removal of the victim from the household for the time being is usually necessary.

Victims often benefit from psychologists' assessment and support. Medical follow-up usually involves repeat serological tests for HIV and Hepatitis C (also Hepatitis B if the victim is non-immune), up to the end of a 6-month window period from the last sexual contact.

## Emotional Abuse

Emotional abuse is also known as psychological abuse or psychological maltreatment.

Amongst all the types of child abuse, emotional abuse is probably the most prevalent, most under-reported, most difficult to assess and least understood. Emotional abuse often goes undetected, even though it is seldom deliberately hidden, as sexual abuse is. When witnessed, it may not be recognised.

Emotional abuse is characterised by a **repeated pattern** of behaviour by a parent or caregiver that causes a child to feel unwanted or unloved or that is detrimental to the child's well-being, including his or her development and socialisation. These harmful behaviours include:

b4854\_Sec-09.indd 257

12-Dec-22 11:00:42 AM

# *The Baby Bear Book*

- **Spurning:** e.g. rejecting the child's bids for attention, excluding the child from family activities, ridiculing, humiliating, scapegoating, belittling, denigrating (including calling a child 'stupid').
- **Terrorising:** e.g. threatening physical harm, threatening deprivation of basic needs, threatening abandonment, damaging the child's loved possessions, making unreasonable demands.
- **Isolating:** e.g. confining a child within a room or within the home, depriving the child of social interactions.
- **Emotional unresponsiveness:** e.g. withholding affection, withholding praise.
- **Corrupting/exploiting:** e.g. encouraging or reinforcing misbehaviour, demanding behaviour inappropriate for the developmental age, using the child to meet the parent's emotional needs.

Isolated incidents of these behaviours do not necessarily constitute emotional abuse, but it is possible for a single incident (e.g. of severe terrorising) to cause great harm to a child.

Emotional abuse may occur on its own, or alongside physical abuse, sexual abuse or neglect. On the other hand, physical abuse, sexual abuse or neglect seldom occur without an accompanying element of emotional abuse. In all forms of child abuse, the greatest harm is often psychological harm, and the harm may not be intended.

Children with intellectual disabilities are at higher risk of emotional abuse, as are children living in households where there is family conflict, custody disputes, intimate partner violence, mental health problems or substance abuse.

Children and adolescents who have been emotionally abused are more likely to have anxiety, depression and eating disorders. They may manifest maladaptive behaviours, developmental delay, poor academic performance or social difficulties. They may also present with somatic symptoms such as headache, chest pain and abdominal pain.

Observations concerning for emotional abuse include:

- A child with behaviour that is attention-seeking, anti-social, aggressive or oppositional.
- A child who seems withdrawn, unconfident or insecure.
- A child who has poor emotional regulation.
- A child who is age-inappropriately responsible.
- A child who uses age-inappropriate language, including swear words.
- A child who deliberately annoys or hurts others or is cruel to animals.
- A child who seems wary or fearful of his or her parent.
- A young child who does not seem affectionate toward his or her parent.
- A young child who is overly affectionate to strangers.
- A parent who displays negativity or hostility towards the child.

Besides enquiring about the experiences of such a child, there is also a need to explore parental expectations and parenting difficulties faced. The boundary between suboptimal parenting and emotional abuse is difficult to define. Rather than applying a label of 'emotional abuse', it is more helpful for physicians and other professionals to:

- Describe specific harmful actions or words and explain how these are harmful.
- Encourage and enable modification of parenting behaviours, e.g. by referring for parenting skills training.

b4854_Sec-09.indd 258

12-Dec-22 11:00:42 AM

- Plan with the parents for the child's safety and well-being going forward.

In emotional abuse, the need for protection is usually less immediately pressing than in sexual abuse and physical abuse. Nevertheless, referral to the Child Protective Service may still be needed, especially if emotionally abusive behaviours persist. Clear evidence of harm is not a prerequisite for referral.

Paediatricians have a unique opportunity to prevent the emotional abuse of children, by proactively discussing approaches to parenting, helping parents to understand children's needs and normal behaviours at each stage of development and pointing parents to useful parenting resources.

## Neglect

Child neglect has occurred when a parent or caregiver has the means to, but does not, provide for the development of the child in any of these spheres: health, education, emotional development, nutrition, shelter and safe living conditions (definition adapted from the WHO). Examples of neglect include failure to provide a child with adequate food, clothing, supervision, medical care or opportunities to attend school.

The prevalence of child neglect worldwide has been estimated at 16–40%. Unlike victims of physical or sexual abuse, victims of neglect are not often brought to hospital for forensic evaluation. They may nevertheless require medical assessment and interventions, whether in primary care (e.g. to catch up with missed vaccinations) or by specialists (e.g. for associated failure to thrive or developmental delay).

Referral to the Child Protective Service may be necessary if a child has suffered serious accidental injury, is severely malnourished or significantly delayed in development due to suspected neglect, or if the parent consistently does not meet the child's basic needs.

Removal of a child from the home environment/admission to hospital as a place of safety may be necessary, such as when home conditions are eminently unsafe, or when caregivers are remanded for drug offences or incapacitated by intoxication or mental health issues.

Table 9.5 Some Alerting Features for Child Neglect

- Malnutrition/faltering growth due to inadequate or inappropriate diet
- Persistently poor hygiene/persistent infestation with head lice or scabies
- Poor school attendance without valid explanation
- Missed vaccinations/non-compliance to medications
- Recurrent accidental injuries due to lack of supervision or unsafe living environment
Adapted from WHO guidelines for the health sector response to child maltreatment, 2019

# CHAPTER 33

# Rashes

Ho Pui Yoong, Valerie; Koh Jean Aan, Mark

The key to establishing an accurate diagnosis and subsequent management of skin disorders in children is through a **focused clinical history and examination**.

## History

Pertinent key points to obtain on history include:

Age of onset (congenital or acquired)
Duration of the rash
Distribution of the rash
- Progression of the rash
- Recent new oral medications or topical contactants in the last 2 months

» Severe cutaneous adverse drug eruptions like drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS) are delayed hypersensitivity reactions and have a latency period of 2 weeks to 2 months
Topical applications of contactants can cause acute contact dermatitis

- Family history of skin conditions, similar acute eruptions or autoimmune conditions

e.g. atopic dermatitis runs in families with a strong atopy history
e.g. infestations like scabies and fungal infections can spread within close family contacts

- Contact history: Other affected family members, pets
- Travel history
Vaccination history
- Associated fever, systemic symptoms like arthralgia, mucous membrane involvement and constitutional symptoms like loss of weight and lethargy

## Clinical Examination

It is important to ascertain the following during clinical examination:

- General appearance of the child, vital signs and presence of fever
- Morphology and distribution of the rashes and presence of secondary changes (see Tables 10.1 and 10.2)

- Accurate morphological description of the rash will help to narrow down the differential diagnoses or clinch the diagnosis

# Rashes

- Mucous membrane involvement and presence of Nikolsky sign (epidermal sloughing from gentle shearing pressure)
- Examine the nails, hair and teeth
Systemic examination, e.g. hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy and arthritis

Table 10.1 Primary Morphology of Rashes

|  Primary Lesions | Definition  |
| --- | --- |
|  Macule | Flat, non-palpable lesion ≤1 cm in diameter  |
|  Patch | Flat, non-palpable lesion >1 cm in diameter  |
|  Papule | Elevated palpable solid lesion ≤1 cm in diameter  |
|  Plaque | Elevated palpable solid lesion >1 cm in diameter  |
|  Nodule | Elevated, rounded palpable solid lesion >1 cm in diameter and usually located primarily in the dermis and/or subcutis  |
|  Vesicle | Blister ≤1 cm (containing clear, serous or haemorrhagic fluid)  |
|  Bulla | Blister >1 cm (containing clear, serous or haemorrhagic fluid)  |
|  Pustule | A circumscribed lesion containing pus  |
|  Petechiae | Small (<3 mm) non-blanching haemorrhages  |
|  Purpura | Larger (>3 mm) non-blanching haemorrhages  |
|  Weal/Urticaria | A transient elevation of the skin due to dermal oedema, usually pale centrally with an erythematous rim  |

Table 10.2 Secondary Changes

|  Secondary Changes | Definition  |
| --- | --- |
|  Crust | Dried serum, blood or pus on the surface of the skin  |
|  Scale | A visible accumulation of keratin, forming a flat plate or flake  |
|  Erosion | A loss of either a portion of or the entire epidermis  |
|  Excoriation | A loss of the epidermis and a portion of the dermis, usually due to scratching or an injury (often linear)  |
|  Ulcer | Full-thickness loss of the epidermis and a portion of the dermis  |

## Clinical Pearls

In general, rash in the absence of fever or other systemic symptoms tend to be benign.

'Red flags' that require prompt attention include:

- Association with fever/prolonged fever, systemic symptoms like arthralgia \(\pm\) unwell child
» Serious systemic bacterial infections: Meningococcemia, staphylococcal scalded skin syndrome (SSSS)
» Drug eruptions: DRESS, Stevens-Johnson syndrome (SJS) and toxic epidermal necrolysis (TEN)

b4854_Sec-10.indd 263

12-Dec-22 11:01:03 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

» Vasculitic/rheumatologic conditions: Kawasaki disease, systemic onset juvenile idiopathic arthritis (SoJIA), systemic lupus erythematosus
» Malignancy: Acute lymphocytic leukaemia

- Mucocutaneous involvement (oral, conjunctival, anogenital) may signify a delayed hypersensitivity cutaneous drug eruption like SJS and TEN
- Wheals or urticarial rash with angioedema, respiratory distress, wheezing and hypotension may signify acute anaphylaxis and needs urgent management

Clinically, a rash may be broadly classified as macular/maculopapular, papular, vesiculobullous, pustular, urticaria or petechial/purpuric. These rashes may co-exist or evolve from one form to another. Diagnosis should be focused on the exclusion of emergent or life-threatening conditions that require prompt intervention.

Please refer to the attached algorithms.

## When to Refer Urgently to the Dermatologist

- Diagnostic dilemmas that may warrant a skin biopsy
- Index of suspicion for a severe cutaneous adverse reaction to drugs like DRESS, SJS and TEN
- Concerns for atypical presentations of serious systemic infections in the immunocompromised population and very young children
- Concerns for autoimmune conditions, in which concurrent rheumatological opinion should also be sought if needed
- Complex or complicated dermatological conditions (genodermatoses or vascular anomalies) that require specialised or multi-disciplinary care

# CHAPTER 34

# Atopic Dermatitis

Ho Pui Yoong, Valerie; Koh Jean Aan, Mark

## Introduction

Atopic dermatitis (AD) is a common, chronic, recurrent, pruritic skin condition. It is one of the most common skin conditions seen in infants and children, affecting more than 20% of children and adolescents. There is often a personal or family history of other atopic conditions, like allergic rhinitis and asthma. AD is also associated with Immunoglobulin E (IgE)-mediated food allergies, pityriasis alba, keratosis pilaris and ichthyosis.

The onset of AD is usually in the first few months of life, with 85% of affected individuals having onset of symptoms by 5 years of age. Many children with AD improve with age, although about 10% will persist to or recur in adulthood.

## Clinical Presentation

AD may present as acute, subacute, or chronic forms, although many patients present with a combination. Patients usually complain of severe itch along with dryness, scaling, oozing, and swelling of the skin. Acute eczema presents as papulovesicular and oozy lesions. Subacute forms present with erythematous papules and plaques. Lichenification (accentuation of skin lines) is characteristic of chronically rubbed sites.

There are also 3 phases that may be defined according to age. In the infantile stage, which occurs from a few weeks after birth to a few months of age, commonly affected sites include the cheeks, forehead, and scalp (cradle cap), and may extend to the trunk or flexor surfaces of the extremities. There are scattered, ill-defined, often symmetrical, papules, patches, and plaques, sometimes with oozing and crusting. Annular forms may be seen, mimicking tinea infection. Severe AD in infancy, if associated with failure to thrive and gastrointestinal symptoms may indicate a food allergy (e.g. cow’s milk, eggs or peanuts).

The childhood phase occurs from 1 year of age to puberty. There is a greater tendency towards chronicity and lichenification. Classically, the flexures, especially the neck, cubital fossae, popliteal fossae and ankles, are affected. The eyelids are also commonly affected.

The adult stage of the condition begins at puberty, continuing into adulthood. Common areas affected include the flexures, the face and neck, and the hands and feet. Prurigo nodules and lichen simplex chronicus are also common manifestations.

In all phases of AD, post-inflammatory hypo- and hyperpigmentation may occur. These changes are usually transient and reversible when the inflammation settles. Repigmentation, however, may take several months to years to reverse.

268

b4854_Sec-10.indd 268

12-Dec-22 11:01:07 AM

# Atopic Dermatitis

# Treatment

## Dry Skin Care

- Daily baths with cool or luke-warm water, for 5-10 minutes is ideal. A mild soap or soap substitute should be used during showers or baths.
- After patting dry, moisturisers should be applied immediately. Creams and ointments are more effective than lotions. The 'best moisturiser' is one that the child and parent will use frequently, which works for the patient and is affordable. Moisturisers should be applied at least 2 to 3 times daily, with applications more frequent during exacerbations.
- The frequent daily use of moisturisers, even during times of quiescence of disease, should be emphasised to patients and parents.

## Avoid Irritants/Allergens

- Common triggers of eczema include changes in climate, sweating, dusty environments, harsh soaps, cigarette smoke, and chemicals. Carpets, pets and soft toys should be avoided as they are common sources of house dust mites. Soft, loose cotton clothing is recommended. Food allergy as a cause of eczema is controversial. It may play a role in some infants with severe eczema. Allergen-specific IgE or skin prick tests should be ordered only after consultation with a paediatric allergist or dermatologist.

## Topical Corticosteroids

- Topical corticosteroids (TCS) are the mainstay of treatment of AD. Complications of TCS use can be minimised with appropriate and safe use. TCS should be applied just before the application of moisturisers. In general, ointments are more moisturising, afford better penetration, more efficacious and less irritating than creams and lotions. Creams are less potent than ointments but are more cosmetically elegant. Lotions are used for scalp application.
- The recommended guidelines for use of TCS are:
  - Low-potency TCS (e.g. \(1\%\) hydrocortisone cream, \(0.025\%\) betamethasone valerate cream, \(0.05\%\) desonide) are recommended for the face, flexures and genitalia for all ages.
Mid-potency TCS (e.g. \(0.1\%\) betamethasone valerate cream, mometasone furoate cream) are recommended for thicker plaques on the trunk and limbs of children.
High-potency TCS (e.g. betamethasone dipropionate) are reserved for stubborn, severely lichenified eczematous plaques, and should be ordered only by paediatric dermatologists.
- Patients should be advised to use TCS twice daily and to stop applications once the eruptions are not itchy, red or bumpy. After 2 weeks, if the rash does not improve, patients should be reviewed.
- The use of oral corticosteroids should only be reserved for severe exacerbations of AD (e.g. prednisolone \(0.5\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\), in tailing doses over 10-14 days).

b4854_Sec-10.indd 269

12-Dec-22 11:01:07 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Topical Calcineurin Inhibitors

- Topical calcineurin inhibitors (TCI), tacrolimus ointment (Protopic) and pimecrolimus cream (Elidel) are non-steroidal anti-inflammatory medications used for the treatment of eczema. Tacrolimus may be used in patients with moderate to severe AD and pimecrolimus in patients with mild AD. They are recommended to be used twice daily over affected areas. Common side effects include irritation and burning. Similar to TCS, TCI should be applied until the skin is not itchy, red or bumpy.

## Treatment of Infections

- Staphylococcus aureus is a common coloniser of the skin of patients with AD and can lead to infective flares. Anti-staphylococcal antibiotics should be prescribed during exacerbations of disease (e.g. \(50\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) TDS or cloxacillin \(50\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) QDS or erythromycin \(50\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) QDS for 1 to 2 weeks). Skin swabs should be sent for bacterial culture. Adjunctive therapies, including potassium permanganate soaks and a TCS/antibiotic combination (e.g. fucicort or \(0.1\%\) betamethasone/vioform), can be applied to the oozy lesions.
- If eczema herpeticum is suspected, systemic acyclovir, either oral or intravenous, can be started. Herpes simplex virus polymerase chain reaction swab should be sent from a blister or erosion to confirm the diagnosis.

## Oral Anti-histamines and other Anti-itch Measures

- Sedative anti-histamines (e.g. hydroxyzine or chlorpheniramine) before bedtime may help lessen scratching during sleep. Non-sedating anti-histamines have minimal effect on itch in AD. Other anti-itch topicals can also be recommended (e.g. Suu Balm, Suu Balm Kids, Kidsclin Mousse, Awemed Spray). Application of fridge-chilled moisturisers can also help to relief the itch. Avoid calamine lotion as it is very drying and can worsen AD.

## Wet-wrap Treatment

- The use of wet-wrap therapy has shown good effect in patients with acute flares of eczema. It can also be used for patients with thick, lichenified plaques of eczema (e.g. lichen simplex chronicus). It is best to initiate wet-wraps as inpatient treatment.

## When To Refer To A Paediatric Dematologist

Moderate to severe AD.
- Patients with frequent, recurrent flares of AD, especially if recurrent bacterial infections or eczema herpeticum.
- Patients with reduced quality of life secondary to AD or if there are other psychosocial problems that are associated with AD.
- Patients with suspected complications of AD treatment (e.g. from corticosteroid use) or patients with poor compliance or understanding of AD treatment.

# CHAPTER 35

# Adrenal Insufficiency

Aravind Venkatesh; Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Introduction

Glucocorticoids are important in carbohydrate, protein and fat metabolism and also play an integral part in the stress response. Mineralocorticoids deal with water retention as well as sodium and electrolyte balance. Glucocorticoid replacement is required in *all forms* of adrenal insufficiency, while mineralocorticoids are *only* required in primary adrenal insufficiency.

Acute adrenal insufficiency (adrenal crisis) in children is a medical emergency.

Suspect acute adrenal insufficiency in any:

- Unwell child known to have adrenal insufficiency who has an intercurrent illness,
- Child withdrawing from steroid therapy, and
- Child with fluid unresponsive shock.

## Causes

Primary adrenal insufficiency

Congenital adrenal hyperplasia
Adrenal hypoplasia congenita
Adrenoleukodystrophy
Adrenal damage or removal
Autoimmune adrenalitis

Secondary adrenal insufficiency

Hypothalamic/Pituitary damage or tumours
Cranial irradiation
- Withdrawal from chronic steroid therapy causing adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) suppression

## Clinical and Biochemical Features

- Acute manifestations include:
Hypotension and shock (from loss of vasomotor tone)
Hypoglycaemia (from low liver glucose output)
- Subacute/Chronic manifestations include:

274

b4854_Sec-11.indd 274

12-Dec-22 3:17:40 PM

# Adrenal Insufficiency

» Hyperpigmentation (from chronic ACTH exposure in primary adrenal insufficiency)
» Lethargy, weakness and tiredness (from electrolyte changes)
» Loss of appetite and weight loss (from anorexia and volume depletion)

Biochemical features

» Hyponatraemia ± hyperkalaemia (from aldosterone deficiency)
» Hypoglycaemia (from cortisol deficiency)
» Raised urea ± creatinine (from dehydration)
» Raised plasma renin activity (from volume depletion) and serum ACTH (in primary adrenal insufficiency)
» Inadequate cortisol response to Synacthen stimulation

## Investigations

In any severely ill child who may be presenting with features of suspected adrenal insufficiency for the first time, take samples for: serum electrolytes (Na/K), glucose, cortisol, 17-OH progesterone, ACTH, plasma renin and aldosterone before administering glucocorticoids.

## Management

### Adrenal Crisis

- **Fluid Management**

» Resuscitate with intravenous (IV) normal saline bolus \(20\mathrm{ml / kg}\) if hypotensive, in shock or poorly perfused.
» Repeat IV normal saline bolus \(10\mathrm{ml / kg}\), if needed.

- **Hyperkalaemia**

» Manage severe hyperkalaemia urgently, if needed.

- **Hydrocortisone Replacement**

» Administer IV or intramuscular hydrocortisone \((100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\) body surface area [BSA]) IMMEDIATELY. [BSA guide: 3-year-old \(0.5\mathrm{m}^2\) (50 mg), 8-year-old 1 \(\mathrm{m}^2\) (100 mg), adult \(1.7\mathrm{m}^2\) (150-200 mg)]
» Continue stress doses of IV hydrocortisone \(100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\) /day in 4-6 divided doses until the clinical condition stabilises.

- **Hypoglycaemia Management**

» Administer \(10\%\) dextrose \(2\mathrm{ml / kg}\) as a slow bolus if blood glucose is low.
» Subsequently, provide adequate dextrose (5%) in the maintenance drip (0.9% normal saline), withholding potassium until the serum potassium is normal.

- **Consult Paediatric Endocrinologist**

### Replacement Therapy

- **Hydrocortisone**

» Hydrocortisone is the preferred glucocorticoid option.

b4854_Sec-11.indd 275

12-Dec-22 3:17:40 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

The replacement hydrocortisone dose in primary adrenal insufficiency is \(12 - 15\mathrm{mg / m^2}\) /day. Requirements are higher (3-4 times) during stress to the body (febrile illness, injury, surgery, anaesthesia).
The replacement hydrocortisone dose in secondary adrenal insufficiency due to ACTH deficiency is usually less than the above doses at about \(8 - 10\mathrm{mg}/\) \(\mathrm{m}^2 /\) day.

# - Mineralocorticoid

- The replacement fludrocortisone dose in primary adrenal disease is 0.05–0.2 mg/day (NOTE: Absolute dose and not per body weight or surface area). Dose adjustments are based on serum electrolytes, blood pressure and plasma renin activity.

# Glucocorticoid Cover for Intercurrent Illness

- Children with, or who are at risk of, adrenal insufficiency **must be given increased** replacement hydrocortisone doses for stress according to the following guidelines:

Moderately unwell and/or temperature \(38 - 39^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\): give two to three times the usual dose of hydrocortisone.
More unwell and/or temperature \(>39^{\circ}\mathrm{C}\): give three to four times the usual dose of hydrocortisone.
If the dose is vomited within 30 minutes of ingestion, repeat the dose.
If the dose is vomited again, bring the child to Children's Emergency.
For gastroenteritis or diarrhoea: Give four times the usual dose of hydrocortisone.
When the stress is over, return to routine dosing regimen without tapering.

# Peri-operative Glucocorticoid Cover

# Major surgery

At induction of anaesthesia give IV hydrocortisone \(100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\)
Then continue IV hydrocortisone \(100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\) /day in 4-6 hourly doses intra and post-operatively for 24-48 hours.
Hydrocortisone doses are weaned down depending on the post-operative recovery.

# Minor surgery

At induction of anaesthesia give IV hydrocortisone \(100\mathrm{mg / m^2}\)
Then give three to four times the usual dose of oral hydrocortisone for 24 hours.

# Bibliography

1. Bornstein SR, Allolio B, Arlt W, et al. (2016) Diagnosis and treatment of primary adrenal insufficiency: An endocrine society clinical practice guideline. J Clin Endocrinol Metab 101(2):364-389.
2. Miller BS, Spencer SP, Geffner ME, et al. (2020) Emergency management of adrenal insufficiency in children: Advocating for treatment options in outpatient and field settings. J Investig Med 68(1):16-25.
