# CHAPTER 36

# Hypoglycaemia

Chin Xinyi; Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Aravind Venkatesh; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Definition

What constitutes low blood glucose (BG), i.e. hypoglycaemia, depends on the clinical context, epidemiological norms, long-term neurological outcome or threshold for neuroendocrine counter-regulatory response. Autonomic effects and hypoglycaemia awareness occur at BG of 3 mmol/L. As such, recurrent BG <3 mmol/L (<3.5 mmol/L if symptomatic) warrants further investigation. (The definition for hypoglycaemia for diabetic patients is BG <4.0 mmol/L.)

Normal responses to hypoglycaemia include:

- Hormonal: Suppressed insulin, and raised growth hormone (GH), glucagon and cortisol levels.
- Metabolic: Fat catabolism to produce ketones as an alternative metabolic fuel.

## Causes

Inadequate responses to a drop in BP lead to hypoglycaemia. See Table 11.1 for causes of hypoglycaemia.

## History

- Symptoms of hypoglycaemia

Palpitations, tremor, anxiety, sweating, hunger, paraesthesia
> Severe hypoglycaemia: Lethargy, irritability, uncharacteristic behaviour, hypothermia, confusion, coma, seizures
Neonate \(< 48\) hours: Apnoea, hypotonia, jitteriness, poor feeding, high pitched cry

Past history

Birth history (Birth weight, gestation, maternal gestational diabetes mellitus
Neonatal history of hypoglycaemia
Episodes suggestive of hypoglycaemia (undiagnosed seizure disorder)
Previous gastric surgery, fundoplication (postprandial hypoglycaemia)

- Dietary history

Milk products (galactosaemia)
Fructose, e.g. juices (hereditary fructose intolerance)

277

Hormonal deficiencies and hyperinsulinism

- Macrosomia (Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome)
Dysmorphic features

# Hypoglycaemia

- Features of endocrinopathy

Hypopituitarism (midline defects, micropenis, undescended testes, growth failure)
Skin hyperpigmentation (primary adrenal insufficiency)
Ambiguous genitalia (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)

- Hepato- or hepatosplenomegaly and/or hypotonia (IEM)

- Blood pressure

## Investigations

- Obtain blood and urine samples at the time of hypoglycaemia (critical sample) AND before any glucose load is given (if this is not possible, always treat the patient first and do not send any critical sample to the lab)

Hormonal — Insulin, C-peptide, cortisol and GH
Metabolic

1st line: Glucose, ketones, plasma acylcarnitine, free fatty acid, lactate
2nd line: Plasma ammonia, amino acid, urine organic acid

- Estimate the glucose utilisation needs from the intravenous (IV) glucose infusion rate:

Glucose infusion rate \(>10\mathrm{mg / kg / min}\) is usually due to hyperinsulinism.
Glucose infusion rate \(< 10\mathrm{mg / kg / min}\) is due to other causes of hypoglycaemia.

- A fasting study that might induce hypoglycaemia (to be done under close medical supervision).
Further tests may be needed to define:

Hormonal abnormality
Hepatic enzyme function

Table 11.2 Interpretation of Laboratory Results

|  High Ketones, High Free Fatty Acid, Acidaemia | Low Ketones, No Acidaemia  |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  • Ketotic hypoglycaemia • Disorder of gluconeogenesis (high lactate) • Glycogenoses • Cortisol/Growth hormone deficiencies | Low Free Fatty Acid • Hyperinsulinism | High Free Fatty Acid • Fatty acid oxidation defects • Carnitine deficiency  |

## Treatment

- Obtain critical samples before corrective measures (if possible).
- Aim to restore BG \(>3.5\) mmol/L through normal nutritional intake.
If asymptomatic, offer:

Dose of Rapilose (Glucose \(40\%\) oral gel): Buccal \(0.5\mathrm{mg / kg}\)
Oral glucose \(15\mathrm{g}\) or \(150\mathrm{ml}\) juice

# The Baby Bear Book

» Followed by a carbohydrate serving

- If symptomatic, administer IV glucose:

Slow bolus \(2\mathrm{ml / kg}\) of \(10\%\) dextrose over 2-5 min
» Maintenance IV dextrose at 4-8 mg/kg/min
The rate must be adjusted to achieve and maintain BG \(>3.5\) mmol/L

- Recheck BG 15–30 minutes after each corrective measure

- If hypoglycaemia recurs after Rapilose, give IV \(10\%\) dextrose bolus \(2\mathrm{ml / kg}\) and commence IV dextrose drip.
- If hypoglycaemia recurs after IV dextrose bolus, repeat IV \(10\%\) dextrose bolus and increase the IV glucose infusion rate.
- For recalcitrant cases, refer to endocrine to consider the use of:

» Intramuscular/IV/Subcutaneous glucagon \(0.5\mathrm{mg}\) (<3 years old); \(1\mathrm{mg}\) (>3 years old).
Glucagon can be used as a continuous IV infusion at \(5 - 20\mu \mathrm{g / kg / h}\) in severe ongoing hyperinsulinism
» IV hydrocortisone 3-5 mg/kg/day

- Definitive therapy is based on specific aetiology of the hypoglycaemic disorder:

Nutritional therapy
» Avoidance of prolonged fast
Hyperinsulinism:

Oral diazoxide \(3 - 15\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\) Q6-12H
Chlorothiazide in conjunction with diazoxide
Somatostatins — Safety issues in its use
Total or sub-total pancreatectomy

# CHAPTER 37

# Hyperglycaemia

Aravind Venkatesh; Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Introduction

Hyperglycaemia refers to high blood glucose (BG). In the fasted state, people without diabetes maintain BG level between 4 and 6 mmol/L. A person whose BG is consistently above 7mmol/L has hyperglycaemia. Symptoms of hyperglycaemia (increased passage of urine and thirst) may not be noticeable until BG levels remain consistently above 15 mmol/L. **Hyperglycaemia may be transient or persistent, asymptomatic or symptomatic. It may or may not indicate that the person has diabetes mellitus (DM).**

## Causes

DM
- Stress hyperglycaemia

» Febrile child
Critically ill child
» Dehydrated child

Medication induced, e.g. steroids
- Other endocrine cases

Thyrotoxicosis
Cushing syndrome/disease
» Phaeochromocytoma

## Diabetes Mellitus

DM can present as early as the neonatal period. Neonatal DM can be temporary or permanent. In pre-pubertal patients, the most likely diagnosis is Type 1 DM associated with autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells. In the pubertal and post-pubertal age group, both Type 1 and Type 2 DM, as well as Maturity Onset Diabetes in the Young (MODY) can occur. Both Type 1 and Type 2 patients can present with diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA). Antibody tests, presenting clinical features, insulin, C-peptide and family history may help differentiate the two.

Impaired Glucose Tolerance (IGT) and Type 2 DM may be asymptomatic. It should be looked for in a child who is overweight or obese, has a family history of DM or gestational diabetes, intrauterine growth restriction or is large for gestational age,

281

b4854_Sec-11.indd 281

12-Dec-22 3:17:40 PM

acanthosis nigricans, hypertension for age and obstructive sleep apnoea. An oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) may be useful.

## Workup of a Child with Hyperglycaemia

- Determine the clinical context to identify if immediate action is needed.
Take a complete history, focusing on the following and the timing of the events:

Polyuria and thirst
Weight loss and appetite
Energy levels
Nausea, vomiting and abdominal pain

- Examine for Kussmaul breathing (for clinical acidosis) and quantify the degree of dehydration.
- Determine the neurological state (which will be affected in more acidotic states).
- Check blood or urine for ketones.
- Check blood for urea, creatinine, glucose and electrolytes.
- Check the capillary blood gas.

## Further Workup of a Child with Suspected Diabetes Mellitus

- Assess and investigate to classify the aetiology of diabetes.

Take a full family history

Autoimmune conditions
Metabolic conditions

Examine for signs of insulin resistance

Acanthosis nigricans (hyperinsulinism)
Skin tags (hyperinsulinism)

Examine for autoimmune conditions

Goitre
Vitiligo
Mucocutaneous candidiasis

Examine for features of metabolic syndrome

High Body Mass Index
Hypertension
Hirsutism in females

Determine the pubertal stage
Obtain blood samples for:

Glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD) antibodies/islet cell antibodies (ICA)
Fasting glucose, fasting lipids, fasting insulin, C-peptide and HbA1c

# Hyperglycaemia

Table 11.3 Practical Issues and Responses

|  Issue | Question/Action | Practice Points  |   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Diagnosis | Does the hyperglycaemic child have diabetes mellitus (DM)? Test • Fasting blood glucose (BG) • Oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT) | Existing diagnostic criteria for DM are: • Symptoms of diabetes AND • Random BG ≥11.1 mmol/L OR • Fasting BG ≥7.0 mmol/L (no caloric intake for at least 8 hr) OR • 2-hr post glucose load BG ≥11.1 mmol/L during an OGTT (glucose 1.75 g/kg body weight, max 75g) Asymptomatic or mildly symptomatic children with equivocal hyperglycaemia may have ‘pre-diabetes’ and need retesting on another day.  |   |
|  Classification of diabetes | Does the hyperglycaemic child have insulin deficiency or resistance? Test • Fasting BG • Fasting lipids • Fasting insulin and C-peptide • Blood ketones • HbA1c • Glutamic acid decarboxylase antibodies • Islet cell antibodies | Type 1 diabetes mellitus (T1DM): • Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) at presentation is common • Family history (FH) of autoimmune conditions, including T1DM, thyroid disease, vitiligo and celiac disease • Immune-mediated (autoantibody positive) | Type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM): • DKA at presentation is uncommon • FH of obesity, T2DM, raised triglycerides and hypertension • Acanthosis nigricans • Autoantibody negative  |
|   |   |  Classification may not be obvious. Early diagnosis of T1DM is mandatory as it may be life-saving. Refer suspected diabetes early, where possible on the same day.  |   |
|  Therapy | How severe is the insulin deficiency? | See Table 11.4 for management of hyperglycaemia in children and adolescents.  |   |

# Management

The clinical presentation of a child with newly diagnosed or known to have diabetes depends on the degree of insulin deficiency and metabolic compensation.

Varying degrees of insulin deficiency are reflected by the insulin deficiency pyramid score (IDPS, 0 being the most severe) and corresponding symptoms and biochemical markers are mapped into the insulin deficiency pyramid (See Table 11.4). These indicators help define the clinical context and appropriate management.

b4854_Sec-11.indd 283

12-Dec-22 3:17:40 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

## Diabetic Ketoacidosis

DKA is the leading cause of morbidity and mortality in children and adolescents with Type 1 DM. DKA may also occur in patients with Type 2 DM.

### Suspect DKA if:

- Dehydration
- Tachycardia
- Tachypnoea
- Deep sighing respiration
- Breath smells of acetone
- Nausea and/or vomiting
Abdominal pain
- Blurry vision
Confusion
- Drowsiness
- Decrease in the level of consciousness and eventually loss of consciousness

### Criteria for DKA are:

- Hyperglycaemia (BG \(>11\) mmol/L)
- Metabolic acidosis \((\mathrm{pH} < 7.3, \mathrm{HCO}_3 < 15 \mathrm{mmol/L})\)
- Blood ketones \(>1.5 \mathrm{mmol} / \mathrm{L}\) or urine ketones \(2+\) and higher

Upon diagnosis, note the **goals, principles and complications of DKA treatment**.

Then refer to Figure 11.1 for the workflow for the management of DKA.

### Goals of treatment are:

- Correction of acidosis and reversal of ketosis
Gradual, smooth correction of dehydration
- Restoration of BG to near normal
- Avoid complications of therapy (hypokalaemia)
- Identify and treat precipitating illness

### Key principles of management are:

- To recognise differences in the severity of DKA
- Provide sufficient fluid correction based on severity of DKA
- To correct dehydration over 48-72 hours
- To maintain sufficient insulin dose (adding dextrose if needed) until acidosis \((\mathrm{HCO}_3 > 15\mathrm{mmol / L})\) is reversed
- To assess neurological and biochemical status frequently during insulin infusion

### Complications of DKA are:

Cerebral oedema

- Risk factors: Age <5 years, elevated serum urea, severe acidosis, severe hypocapnia

# The Baby Bear Book

- Hypokalaemia
Hypoglycaemia
- Inadequate rehydration
- Venous thromboembolic disease

## Practice Points

- **Inform the paediatric endocrinologist of all patients with DKA.**

- **Resuscitation**

» Bolus fluid as needed.
» All moderate to severe DKA patients should be kept 'nil by mouth'.

- **Fluid therapy**

Hydrate intravenously over 48 hours.
» Correct for \(5 - 10\%\) dehydration (dehydration is rarely above \(10\%\)).
For children and young people who are clinically dehydrated but not in shock:

Give an initial intravenous (IV) bolus of \(10\mathrm{ml / kg}\) \(0.9\%\) normal saline over 30 minutes.
Only consider giving a second bolus after discussion with a senior if needed to improve tissue perfusion and only after reassessing their clinical status.
When calculating the total fluid replacements, subtract these initial bolus volumes from the total fluid deficit.

- For children and young people who have signs of shock (weak thread pulse, hypotension):

Give an initial IV bolus of \(20\mathrm{ml / kg}\) \(0.9\%\) normal saline as soon as possible.
When calculating the total fluid requirement, do not subtract this fluid bolus from the total fluids.

» Use \(0.9\%\) NaCl as the initial fluid for the first 12 hours.
» Replace ongoing fluid losses such as vomiting or excess diuresis only if significant.

- **Ongoing fluid prescription**

There is usually some deficit in body sodium (Na) and potassium at DKA presentation.
Always determine corrected Na levels, as high glucose causes pseudohyponatraemia.
Falling corrected serum Na during DKA therapy may indicate excessive rehydration.
» Rehydrate over 72 hours if corrected Na >150 mmol/L.
After 12 hours, consider switching to \(0.45\%\) sodium chloride if plasma Na level is stable or increasing.
» Add \(5\%\) dextrose once glucose level falls below \(14\mathrm{mmol / L}\).
If necessary, use \(10\%\) dextrose to maintain glucose between 5 and \(11\mathrm{mmol / L}\), until ketoacidosis resolves.

# Hyperglycaemia

# Insulin therapy

» Start insulin therapy 1 hour after initiating fluid therapy.
» Prepare insulin infusion by adding 50 units (0.5 ml) of soluble insulin (Actrapid) to 49.5 ml of 0.9% normal saline in a 50 ml syringe pump to produce an insulin concentration of 1 units/ml.
» Commence insulin infusion at 0.1 units/kg/h; consider using 0.05 units/kg/h for children <5 years.
» Consider reducing to 0.05 units/kg/h if the rate of fall of BG exceeds 5 mmol/L per hour or when the acidosis is corrected (pH >7.3, HCO₃ >15 mmol/L).
» Mild DKA can be managed with subcutaneous (SC) insulin — please consult the paediatric endocrinologist.

# - Potassium replacement

» If the patient is hypokalaemic at presentation, start potassium replacement at the time of initial volume expansion, before starting insulin therapy. Otherwise, start concurrent with insulin. If the patient is hyperkalaemic, defer potassium therapy until urine output is documented.
» Starting potassium concentration should be \(40\mathrm{mmol / L}\). Subsequently, base it on potassium measurements.
» Potassium phosphate may be used together with potassium chloride (half and half) as administration of potassium chloride alone contributes to the risk of hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis. Do not use potassium phosphate alone as it may precipitate hypocalcaemia.

# - Acidosis and bicarbonate therapy

» Adequate hydration and insulin therapy will reverse even severe acidosis.
» Bicarbonate therapy is rarely required and may increase risk of cerebral oedema.

Table 11.5 Important Formulae

|   | Formula/Fluid | Additional Instructions  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Maintenance fluid | First 10 kg: 100 ml/kg; Next 10–20 kg: add 50 ml/kg; Above 20 kg: add 20 ml/kg | Males: max 2500 ml/day Females: max 2000 ml/day  |
|  Fluid requirement | 48 hr' maintenance fluids + volume deficit | Give over 48 hr Give over 72 hr if corrected Na >150 mmol/L  |
|  Fluid deficit (ml) | % Dehydration x body weight (kg) x 1,000 | 5–7% if mild or moderate, 8–10% if severe  |
|  Initial fluid choice | 0.9% NaCl with 20 mmol KCl to each bag of 500 ml | Given for at least 12 hr  |
|  Subsequent fluid | 0.45% NaCl with 20 mmol KCl to each bag of 500 ml |   |
|  Corrected Na (mmol/L) | Measured Na + 0.4 (blood glucose · 5.5 mmol/L) | Estimate: add 1 mmol/L Na for every 3 mmol/L glucose >5.5 mmol/L  |

# CHAPTER 38

# Normal Puberty and Disorders of Puberty

Aravind Venkatesh; Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Introduction

Puberty is a process of physical transformation of the child into a sexually functional adult.

- Puberty is a process, meaning a series of related events and not a single event.
- The process is sequential, meaning there is an order to the process of normal puberty.
- There is substantial variation in the onset, tempo and hence duration of normal puberty.
The outcomes of normal puberty are:

- Attainment of adult height and reproductive competence.

- Disorders of puberty can therefore be categorised into:

Disorders of timing — Precocious or delayed onset of puberty.
Disorders of tempo and duration — Rapid or poor progress/arrest of puberty.
Disorders of sequence — Also known as pseudo-puberty.

## Normal Female Puberty

The physical changes of normal female puberty are primarily the result of rising levels of ovarian oestrogen.

- Breast development is typically the first sign, which may begin as early as 7–8 years old or as late as 13 years old.
- Breast development is followed by height acceleration, change in body fat distribution and vaginal discharge.
Vaginal discharge is initially random, then becomes cyclical and precedes menstruation.
- Menarche typically occurs 2-3 years after the onset of breast development.
- Menarche is the penultimate event of normal puberty.
- The final event of normal female puberty is epiphyseal fusion and commonly occurs 1–2 years after menarche.

The appearance of pubic and/or axillary hair is mediated by adrenal and ovarian androgens and is dependent on genetic factors, race, ethnicity as well as body fat mass. Sexual hair development typically occurs 1–1.5 years following onset of breast development.

290

b4854 Sec-11.indd 290

12-Dec-22 3:17:42 PM

# Normal Puberty and Disorders of Puberty

## Normal Male Puberty

The physical changes of normal male puberty are primarily the result of rising levels of testicular androgens.

- Testicular enlargement \((\geq 4\mathrm{ml})\) represents the onset of normal male puberty which may occur as early as 9 years old or as late as 14 years old.
- As the testicular volume increases, the scrotal skin becomes progressively thinner, the penile length and girth increase, the glans penis becomes obvious and pubic hair growth begins.
- Muscle mass acquisition is followed by height acceleration.
- Voice changes occur late as a result of chronic androgen stimulated changes to the voice box structure.

Like females, the appearance of pubic and/or axillary hair is variably influenced by genetic, race and ethnicity factors.

## Adrenarche

Adrenarche is the onset of adrenal gland production of sex hormones, mainly adrenal androgens, responsible for the following changes in both sexes:

Pubic hair
Oily skin
Oily hair
Body odour

Premature adrenarche is when these changes begin early, i.e. before the age of 8 years for girls and 9 years for boys. While this may be physiological in some children, an evaluation should be done.

## Hirsutism and Hypertrichosis

Hirsutism is the excessive growth of thick or dark hair in sex-hormone-dependent areas while hypertrichosis is the increase in hair growth anywhere on the body in males and females.

Table 11.6 Guidelines for Referral to a Paediatric Endocrinologist

|  Refer disorders of TIMING: - Girls with breast development before the age of 7 yr. - Girls with both breast development and pubic/axillary hair development before the age of 8 yr. - Girls with no evidence of breast development by the age of 13 yr. - Boys with testicular volumes of ≥4 ml before the age of 9 yr. - Boys with testicular volumes of ≤4 ml by the age of 14 yr. Note: Early normal puberty is not precocious puberty and care should be taken not to 'medicalise' an otherwise normal condition.  |
| --- |
|  Refer disorders of SEQUENCE: - Girls with pubic/axillary hair development and no evidence of breast development. - Girls with vaginal discharge or menstruation and no evidence of breast development. - Boys with pubic/axillary hair development and testicular volumes of ≤4 ml. Note: Pseudo-precocious puberty may be pathological and needs to be evaluated.  |
|  Refer disorders of TEMPO: - Girls with breast development who fail to menstruate by the age of 15 yr. - Girls who start breast development when they are ≤120 cm tall. - Any child whose pubertal development fails to progress along the expected trajectory (pubertal arrest). Note: The duration from onset of breast development to menstruation is typically 2–3 yr.  |

# Determining the Pubertal Stage

Pubertal stage is determined by a combination of visual inspection and palpation.

Genital Stage Testes Size

![img-86.jpeg](img-86.jpeg)

Stage 1: prepubertal; testicular size less than 4ml in volume and 2.5cm in longest dimension

Stage 2: enlargement of scrotum and testes; scrotal skin reddens and changes in texture; growth of testes to 4ml or greater in volume

Stage 3: enlargement of penis (length at first); further growth of testes

Stage 4: increased size of penis with growth in breadth and development of glans; testes and scrotum larger, scrotal skin darker

Stage 5: adult genitalia

Pubic hair

Tanner 1: hair is absent

Tanner 2: strands of dark, curly pubic hair over the penis

Tanner 3: pubic hair over the base of the penis, too many to count

Tanner 4: pubic hair densely fills the pubic triangle

Tanner 5: spread of pubic hair to thighs and towards abdomen

Breast Development

Stage 1: prepubertal

Stage 2: breast bud with elevation of breast and papilla, enlargement of areola

Stage 3: further enlargement of breast and areola, no separate contour

Stage 4: areola and papilla form secondary mound above level of the breast

Stage 5: mature stage, projection of papilla only, related to recession of areola

![img-88.jpeg](img-88.jpeg)

Pubic hair

Tanner 1: hair is absent

Tanner 2: strands of dark, curly pubic hair over the labia

# CHAPTER 39

# Obesity

Chin Xinyi; Chew Chu Shan, Elaine; Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Aravind Venkatesh;
Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

# Introduction

Obesity has become a worldwide epidemic resulting in significant health problems and costs, for both the individual and the society. Childhood obesity tends to track into adulthood and is an early risk factor for adult morbidity and mortality. As such, the prevention and treatment of obesity should be a paediatric goal.

# Definition

Obesity refers to having excess body fat and is commonly defined in terms of Body Mass Index (BMI) >97th percentile for age and gender. Increased morbidity and mortality associated with obesity can vary for different racial, ethnic and socioeconomic groups, as well as between individuals. Children with BMIs in the 90–97th percentile can also be at risk of developing obesity-related morbidities.

# Pathogenesis and Aetiology

The pathogenesis of obesity is often multi-factorial — genetic, biological, psychological, sociocultural, and environmental (intra- and extra-uterine) — and affects both ends of the energy balance equation. See Table 11.7 for some causes of obesity.

# Workup

# History

- Birth and antenatal history, including birth weight, presence of maternal gestational diabetes
- Previous growth/weights (in particular the onset of weight gain)
- Detailed dietary intake
- Physical activity, screen time
- Symptoms suggestive of secondary causes of obesity (see Table 11.7)
- Symptoms suggestive of co-morbidities (see Table 11.8)

- Drug history, e.g. steroids, herbal supplements, traditional cures
Family history of obesity \(\pm\) co-morbidities
- Social history including assessment of caregivers' involvement in daily routine, i.e. meals, physical activity

# The Baby Bear Book

- Psychosocial history, including issues of self-esteem, depression, risk for disordered eating

## Clinical Examination

- Accurate height and weight, calculate BMI
Growth charts:

Children with exogenous obesity from over-nutrition are usually tall for age with good growth velocity.
Children with underlying pathology tend to be short for age with poor growth velocity.
» Reducing height velocity associated with increasing weight is suggestive of an endocrine cause.

- Pattern of obesity: Central or generalised
Dysmorphic features
Signs of endocrinopathy: Cushingoid features, i.e. moon face, acne, hirsutism, truncal obesity, violaceous striae and hypertension.
Signs of hypothyroidism
Pubertal assessment

- Hypogonadism may suggest a syndromic cause, e.g. Prader–Willi syndrome (PWS), Bardet–Biedl syndrome, Alstrom syndrome

- Acanthosis nigricans, skin tags
Blood pressure
- Assessment of other complications of obesity as guided by clinical history

## Investigations

To identify:

Causes of obesity
Consequences arising from obesity

- Based on clinical assessment, investigations may include:

Liver function test
Thyroid function test
Lipid profile
Fasting glucose, insulin and oral glucose tolerance test
LH, FSH, oestradiol, androgens, sex hormone binding globulin (if suspicious for polycystic ovary syndrome)
Serum cortisol levels (if suspicious for Cushing syndrome)
Genetic testing

## Management

Children with lower degree of obesity and without any obesity-related complications can be managed in a primary care setting. Emphasis is placed on age-appropriate gradual weight loss and/or weight maintenance.

Management of children on exogenous obesity include:

- Dietary intervention

# CHAPTER 40

# Thyroid Disorders

Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Aravind Venkatesh; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Hyperthyroidism in Children

Hyperthyroidism refers to thyroid gland overactivity leading to excess thyroid hormone synthesis. The most common cause of thyrotoxicosis in childhood is Graves' Disease.

## Clinical Features

- **Suspect hyperthyroidism** when the patient reports clusters of the following:

Palpitations
Difficulty in sleeping
Heat intolerance
Muscle fatigue
Unintentional weight loss or difficulty gaining weight, despite increased food intake
Restlessness/emotional lability

- ☐ There may be feedback about a child having poor concentration and a deterioration in school performance.

- Increased bowel motility/frequency — diarrhoea

## Clinical Examination

Examine for clinical signs of:

**Goitre**

Usually smooth, symmetrical and diffuse
A bruit may be found on auscultation of the thyroid gland.

- **Signs of excessive sympathetic activity**

Sweaty palms
Fine hand tremors
Tachycardia with increased pulse volume indicative of a hyperdynamic circulation. An irregularly irregular pulse may be present if complicated by atrial fibrillation (rare in children).
Hyperreflexia
Proximal muscle weakness

298

b4854_Sec-11.indd 298

12-Dec-22 3:17:43 PM

# Thyroid Disorders

# » Eye signs and other autoimmune features

☐ Proptosis, lid lag and exophthalmos may be present, which is associated with Graves' disease. This may be complicated by exposure keratopathy.

Vitiligo

# Investigations

- Send blood investigations for:

Free thyroxine (fT4)
Thyroid stimulating hormone (TSH)
Thyroid antibodies (usually thyroid receptor antibodies [TrAb] and thyroid peroxidase antibodies [TPOAb])

Further evaluation may include:

Thyroid ultrasound (US)
Random blood glucose level

# Management

- Refer early to a paediatric endocrinologist for further evaluation and management, especially in severe thyrotoxicosis, which can cause life-threatening thyrotoxic crisis or thyroid storm.
- Consult a paediatric ophthalmologist if eye signs are severe on clinical examination.
Therapy:

- **Reduce thyroid hormone synthesis** — Anti-thyroid drug (ATD) treatment with carbimazole is a common first-line treatment. The recommended starting dose for carbimazole is 0.5–1 mg/kg/day in two divided doses. Inform patients of uncommon but potentially serious side effects of ATDs, including agranulocytosis, skin rashes, arthralgia and hepatotoxicity.

» Control the symptoms of sympathetic overdrive — Beta-blockers (propranolol) are often used as an adjunct to treatment in the first few weeks for symptom control. The recommended starting dose for propranolol is 0.5–1 mg/kg/day in two divided doses.
» Consider alternative therapy for patients who relapse or are refractory to first-line therapy with ATDs. Alternative modalities such as radioactive iodine or thyroidectomy may be offered.

# Acquired Hypothyroidism in Children

Hypothyroidism can result in significant detrimental effects on the child's health, growth and development. Hence, prompt diagnosis and treatment are important for improved outcomes.

# Clinical Features

- **Suspect hypothyroidism** when the patient reports clusters of the following:

» Lethargy, low energy levels

b4854_5ec-11.indd 299

12-Dec-22 3:17:44 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

☐ This can adversely affect a child's school work and academic performance.

Cold intolerance
Constipation
» Muscle ache and pains
» Faltering height growth — may present with short stature due to delay in skeletal maturation.
Dry skin
Neurological symptoms or manifestations of other pituitary hormone deficiencies may be present in a patient with central hypothyroidism.

## Clinical Examination

- Examine for clinical signs of:

» Bradycardia
» Goitre
Myxoedema
» Slow relaxing deep tendon reflexes
» Hair loss and thinning may be noted
Patients may appear apparently obese/overweight due to reduced metabolic rate and faltering height.
» Pericardial/pleural effusions may be present in severe presentations.

## Investigations

- Avoid ordering thyroid function test (TFT) for patients who have been acutely unwell/ill, as an abnormal thyroid function test may reflect non-thyroidal illness syndrome (NTIS) (previously also known as 'sick euthyroid syndrome'). If in doubt, consult a paediatric endocrinologist before ordering.
- Always interpret TFT in the clinical context to determine if hypothyroidism is central (rarely)/primary in origin (much more common):

» Low fT4, raised TSH: Suggests primary hypothyroidism.
» Low fT4, low TSH: Suggests central hypothyroidism.
» Low fT4, normal TSH: May be central hypothyroidism. Consult endocrine.
» Normal fT4, raised TSH: Suggests subclinical hypothyroidism.

- **Further evaluation** for primary hypothyroidism includes:

» TPOAb is associated with Hashimoto's thyroiditis, a common cause of autoimmune hypothyroidism.
» Thyroid US looking for signs of thyroiditis

- **Further evaluation** for central hypothyroidism includes:

» Testing for deficiencies in other pituitary hormones
» Performing Magnetic Resonance Imaging of the pituitary gland, if indicated

## Management

- **Start levothyroxine (LT4) replacement** for patients diagnosed with hypothyroidism. Treatment may be lifelong, and regular follow-up of growth and dose adjustment is required.

# Thyroid Disorders

- Patients with Hashimoto's thyroiditis may have a transient hyperthyroid phase or Hashitoxicosis. They may require treatment as per hyperthyroidism during this period. However, with time, the child is likely to develop hypothyroidism and require LT4 replacement accordingly.

## Hypothyroidism in Newborn

Ensure that all newborn babies are screened for congenital hypothyroidism.

A newborn baby can still have congenital hypothyroidism even if the screening result is normal. **Early recognition** is critical and **timely treatment** must be instituted to reduce the risk of developing neurodevelopmental delay.

## Clinical Features

- Ask for history of:

Poor feeding
Lethargy
Detailed birth history, including cord TSH, neonatal jaundice requiring phototherapy
Family history of thyroid disorders, including maternal history of hyperthyroidism requiring ATDs

## Clinical Examination

- Examine for signs of:

» Coarse facies
» Protruding tongue
» Jaundice
» Abdominal distension
» Umbilical hernia
» Goitre
» Lingual thyroid

## Investigations

- Suspect congenital hypothyroidism if screening cord TSH \(>20\) mIU/L.
- Confirm congenital hypothyroidism by obtaining venous blood samples for TFT (fT4/TSH) after 72 hours of life (Day 4) if the baby has an abnormal cord TSH.
- Perform imaging, with either a thyroid US or scintigraphy using Technetium (theTc). However, imaging should never be allowed to delay the initiation of treatment.

## Management

- **Commence LT4 replacement** as soon as the diagnosis is confirmed, ideally within 2 weeks after birth. Aim to normalise TSH levels within 2 weeks after initiation of treatment.

b4854,Sec-11.indd 301

12-Dec-22 3:17:44 PM

# The Baby Bear Book

- Follow-up and monitor closely, with regular TFTs and assessment of the child's growth and developmental milestones. The physician who follows up the baby through childhood and adolescence must be adequately trained and experienced in the management of patients with congenital hypothyroidism.

# CHAPTER 41

# Disorders of Calcium Metabolism Chan

Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Aravind Venkatesh; Lek Ngee;

Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Introduction

Normal reference ranges for serum calcium levels are adjusted according to age and corrected according to serum albumin levels.

$$
\text{Adjusted Calcium} = \text{Total Calcium} + 0.02 (40 - [\text{Alb}])
$$

[Alb]: Plasma albumin in g/L

The total adjusted serum calcium levels normally range between 2.3 and 2.6 mmol/L. When the pH is 7.35–7.45, ionised calcium constitutes approximately 50% of the total calcium and is normally maintained between 1.1 and 1.3 mmol/L. The threshold for parathyroid hormone (PTH) secretion is when ionic calcium starts falling below 1.0 mmol/L.

## Hypocalcaemia

The patient with hypocalcaemia usually remains asymptomatic unless plasma concentrations of ionised calcium fall below 1.0 mmol/L and drop rapidly.

## Causes

### Neonatal Hypocalcaemia

- Maternal factors (vitamin D deficiency, hyperparathyroidism, pre-eclampsia, diabetes — pre-existing or gestational)
- Placental factors (placental insufficiency, calcium transporter defects)
- Neonatal factors (low birth weight, prematurity, perinatal hypoxia, intrauterine growth restriction/small for gestational age, neonatal sepsis or stress)
- High phosphate load — from total parental nutrition or high phosphate milk formula
- Inadequate calcium supply — oral or parenteral
- Hypoparathyroidism — transient versus permanent

### Hypocalcaemia with Low PTH

- Abnormal parathyroid gland development or defects in PTH synthesis (e.g. DiGeorge syndrome, autosomal recessive hypoparathyroidism, HDR [hypoparathyroidism, sensorineural deafness and renal disease] syndrome)

303

b4854_Sec-11.indd 303

12-Dec-22 3:17:44 PM

304

# The Baby Bear Book

- Postsurgical, radiation, infiltrative process of the parathyroid gland
Hypomagnesaemia

## Hypocalcaemia with Inappropriately Normal PTH

- Abnormal calcium sensing receptor, e.g. Familial hypercalciuric hypocalcaemia
Familial hypocalcaemia (Autosomal-dominant)
- Hypoparathyroidism is also a consideration (causes as per 'Hypocalcaemia with low PTH')

## Hypocalcaemia with High PTH

Vitamin D related

Vitamin D deficiency—dietary insufficiency, poor intestinal absorption, inadequate sun exposure
Vitamin D metabolism defects—vitamin D dependent rickets, liver/renal dysfunction, vitamin D resistance

- Increased calcium consumption — tumour lysis syndrome, pancreatitis, hungry bone syndrome, intravenous (IV) infusions containing citrate (some forms of continuous renal replacement therapies, massive blood transfusions)
End-organ resistance to PTH — pseudohypoparathyroidism

## Clinical Features

- Muscle cramps and twitching
- Carpopedal spasms
Seizures - which can be focal or generalised
- In neonates/infants, they can present with non-specific symptoms like apnoea and/or stridor
- Chronic untreated hypocalcaemia can result in cardiomyopathy (especially from vitamin D deficiency)

## Clinical Examination

- Chvostek sign (twitching of the ipsilateral facial muscles on tapping on the face, just below the zygomatic bone)
- Trousseau sign (trigger of a carpopedal spasm from inflation of the blood pressure cuff)
- Dysmorphic features if hypocalcaemia is related to underlying genetic condition, such as DiGeorge syndrome, pseudohypoparathyroidism

## First-Line Investigations

- Point of care tests — Electrocardiogram (ECG) to look for prolonged QT interval, blood gas to look for ionic calcium levels and alkalosis.
Critical samples should include:

Renal panel

b4854_Sec-11.indd 304

12-Dec-22 3:17:44 PM

# Disorders of Calcium Metabolism

» Total adjusted serum calcium
» Serum magnesium
» Serum phosphate
» Serum Alkaline phosphatase
» Serum intact PTH
» Serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D
» Urine calcium, magnesium, phosphate and creatinine levels
» Urinary calcium/creatinine ratio

- Blood and urine samples should always be **paired**, and whenever possible, taken before administering any treatment.

## Management

### Acutely Symptomatic Patients

- To obtain critical samples and correct with IV 10% calcium gluconate 0.5 ml/kg over 10 minutes diluted with saline. Treatment of the patient must **never** be delayed in order to collect the samples.

*Note: Ensure good venous access*

This must be followed by calcium replacement either enterally or parenterally, providing 50–70 mg/kg/day of elemental calcium (monitor IV site closely for tissue necrosis).

*Note: Calcium Gluconate 1 ml = 9 mg elemental calcium.*

- In persistent symptomatic hypocalcaemia, calcitriol can be used to normalise calcium levels faster in addition to oral or parenteral calcium replacement.

*Note: Calcitriol is available as oral capsules 0.25 mcg or 0.5 mcg.*

- It is important to correct low serum magnesium as well, if present.

### Asymptomatic Patients and Post-Correction of Symptomatic Hypocalcaemia

- Treatment depends on the underlying aetiology:

» Vitamin D supplementation: Cholecalciferol (D3), Alfacalcidol (1α Vitamin D3), or Calcitriol (1,25 Vitamin D3), depending on the aetiology of hypocalcaemia
» Calcium supplementation: Oral (PO) Calcium Lactogluconate, PO Calcium Carbonate. Starting elemental dose of calcium ranges from 50 to \(70\mathrm{mg / kg / day}\).
» Note: Different calcium preparations have varied content of elemental calcium, please check with pharmacist before prescribing.

After achieving normocalcaemia, it is important for patients on long-term calcium and vitamin D replacement to be monitored for iatrogenic hypercalcaemia, hypercalciuria, nephrocalcinosis and renal insufficiency.

## Hypercalcaemia

Patients with mild hypercalcaemia are usually asymptomatic. However, as adjusted calcium concentrations rise above 3.0 mmol/L, they may start experiencing symptoms of hypercalcemia.

## Mechanisms of Hypercalcaemia

- Increased PTH secretion (inappropriate for the serum calcium levels)
- Increased \(1,25(\mathrm{OH})_{2}\) Vitamin \(\mathrm{D}_3\) synthesis
- Increased bone resorption
- Abnormal set point of Calcium Sensing Receptor
- Increased PTH-related peptide secretion

## Causes of Childhood Hypercalcaemia

Primary and tertiary hyperparathyroidism
Familial benign hypercalcaemia
Williams Syndrome
Idiopathic infantile hypercalcaemia
Vitamin D toxicity
Hypercalcaemia of malignancy
- Immobilisation
Granulomatous disorders
- Subcutaneous fat necrosis

# The Baby Bear Book

## Clinical Features

*Painful bones, renal stones, abdominal groans, psychic moans*

- Vomiting, constipation
- Failure to thrive
- Muscle hypotonia, lethargy
- Loss of appetite
Abdominal pain
Polyuria, polydipsia, dehydration
- If complicated by nephrolithiasis/nephrocalcinosis, patient may experience dysuria, haematuria and other lower urinary tract symptoms.
- Neuropsychiatric disturbances — altered mental state, confusion.

## Clinical Examination

- Hypotension, arrhythmia, shortened QT interval
Confused state, abnormal Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)
Hypotonia, hyporeflexia
- Dry mucous membranes, reduced skin turgor, cool peripheries
- Abdominal tenderness, if complicated by pancreatitis, peptic ulcer disease
- Abdominal masses, organomegaly or lymphadenopathy may be present if there is underlying malignancy
- A neck nodule may be present if hypercalcemia is due to a parathyroid adenoma
- Dysmorphic features if related to underlying genetic syndrome, such as Williams syndrome.

## First-Line Investigations

- ECG to look for shortened QT interval
- Adjusted serum calcium
- Serum magnesium
- Serum phosphate
- Serum alkaline phosphatase
- Serum intact PTH
- Serum 25-hydroxyvitamin D
- Paired urine calcium, magnesium, phosphate and creatinine levels
Urinary calcium/creatinine ratio
- Renal ultrasound — to look for nephrocalcinosis, nephrolithiasis, especially in long-standing hypercalcaemia

## Follow-Up Investigations

- Hand X-ray for phalangeal erosions
- Serum calcium of parents
- Sestamibi scan/neck ultrasound
- Search for malignancy, e.g. bone marrow aspirate
- Genetic analysis for MEN 1

# Disorders of Calcium Metabolism

## Management

- Management involves treating the underlying aetiology.
- A symptomatic patient with total serum calcium level more than \(3\mathrm{mmol / L}\) with an increasing trend should be initiated with hyperhydration, at 1.5 to 2 times the daily maintenance fluid requirement. Fluid balance must be strictly monitored, and forced diuresis with a loop diuretic (frusemide) must be judiciously instituted as necessary.
- Renal function must be monitored as there is potential risk of acute renal failure.
- Most patients should respond to hyperhydration and forced diuresis; consult a paediatric endocrinologist for other therapeutic options such as:
  - Bisphosphonates
  - Calcitonin
  - Steroids

# CHAPTER 42

# Diabetes Insipidus

Chan Wei Keong, Daniel; Chin Xinyi; Aravind Venkatesh; Lek Ngee; Rashida Farhad Vasanwala; Yap Kok Peng, Fabian

## Introduction

Diabetes insipidus (DI) is characterised by primary polyuria due to the lack of anti-diuretic hormone (ADH) or ADH action.

- Polyuria must first be objectively assessed by means of strict intake/output charting.
- Polyuria refers to urine output in excess of \(4 - 5\mathrm{ml / kg / h}\) (up to \(6\mathrm{ml / kg / h}\) for neonates at birth).

## Note that urinary frequency, incontinence and enuresis must be distinguished from polyuria:

- Urinary frequency refers to the increased occurrence of usually voluntary episodes of micturition compared to normal; however, it may be either large or small volumes of urine each time. Hence, frequency does not equate a polyuric state.
- Incontinence refers to the inability to retain urine voluntarily, leading to leakage of urine. It may be mistaken for increased urinary frequency.
- Enuresis refers to involuntary episodes of micturition in children older than 6 years, leading to bedwetting.

Table 11.10 Causes of Diabetes Insipidus

|  Type | Cranial Diabetes Insipidus | Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Familial | • Familial Neurohypophyseal Diabetes Insipidus (FNDI) — Autosomal Dominant • DIDMOAD or Wolfram syndrome | • X-linked Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus • Autosomal recessive Nephrogenic Diabetes Insipidus  |
|  Acquired | • Trauma (head injury, neurosurgery) • Tumours (craniopharyngioma, germinoma, glioma) • Infections (meningoencephalitis, congenital CMV/toxoplasmosis) • Granulomatous disease (tuberculosis, sarcoidosis, histiocytosis) • Hypoxic brain injury • Vascular malformations | • Chronic renal disease • Post-obstructive uropathy • Osmotic diuresis » Glucose (Diabetes mellitus) » Calcium (Hypercalcaemia) » Mannitol (Drug induced)  |

CMV: cytomegalovirus; DIDMOAD: diabetes insipidus, diabetes mellitus, optic atrophy and deafness.

# Diabetes Insipidus

## Clinical Approach

An accurate history is crucial to differentiate polyuria (increase in frequency and volume), from frequent urination and to exclude other reasons, such as:

Primary polydipsia/excessive water intake causing secondary polyuria
Primary monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis
- Urological issues like overactive bladder, detrusor instability
- Renal pathologies like urinary tract infections, tubulopathies
Spinal pathologies
Diabetes mellitus

## Basic Investigations for Frequent Urination

- Basic investigations for all children with frequent micturition includes:

Renal panel
Urinalysis (especially specific gravity, glucose)
Urine culture
Urine calcium/creatinine ratio

- If DI is suspected, include **paired** samples for:

Urine osmolality
Serum osmolality

- Based on the clinical presentation, a water-deprivation test (with Desmopressin [DDAVP] trial) may be required in equivocal cases.

## Diagnosis of Diabetes Insipidus

- Polyuria (exclude urinary frequency, incontinence and enuresis)
- The consequences of polyuria secondary to DI include:

Excessive thirst
Weight loss
Headache due to dehydration and hypernatremia

- In an appropriate clinical setting, we can usually diagnose DI if there is polyuria for 2 or more consecutive hours, with:

» Urine-specific gravity \(< 1.005\) and/or urine osmolality \(< 300\mathrm{mOsm/kg}\) \(\mathrm{H}_2\mathrm{O}\) (before hydration), AND
Elevated serum sodium \(>145\mathrm{mmol / L}\) AND
Serum osmolality nears or exceeds \(300\mathrm{mOsm / kg}\)

- Note that serum sodium and osmolality may also be within normal ranges if child has an intact thirst mechanism and is allowed free access to fluids.
- In established clinical DI in the absence of renal pathology, Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) of the pituitary gland will be needed to evaluate the hypothalamic-pituitary region for any pathologies, including a space-occupying lesion or congenital anomaly. Evaluation of the other pituitary hormone axes is also required. Absence of a normally placed bright spot in the posterior pituitary on T1-weighted MRI scan is highly suggestive of central DI.

# The Baby Bear Book

- If further assessment with a water deprivation test is required, consult with a paediatric endocrinologist.

## Overnight Water Restriction under Monitoring

*(Note: This must never be performed in children with severe polyuria)*

- No fluids after bedtime and at breakfast.
- Collect sample at 8:00 am to 8:30 am for paired serum and urine osmolality, serum sodium.
- Patients with complete DI may not be able to tolerate overnight fluid restriction. Where desired, patients must be allowed access to water and the water restriction process aborted.
- Those unable to tolerate overnight water restriction may require a formal water deprivation test to be performed by a paediatric endocrinologist.

## Water Deprivation Test

- Patient must be closely observed and supervised throughout the test.
- Before the test, the patient is allowed usual fluid intake.
- The biochemistry laboratory must be informed ahead of the test in order to coordinate rapid processing of samples and timely reporting of the results.
- Start the test at 8:00 am in the morning after collecting baseline paired serum and urine osmolality, serum sodium.
- Patient is not allowed to drink water or any fluid after that.
- Timed collection of paired serum and urine osmolality, serum sodium is done at every 1-2 hourly intervals. Patient must be under close surveillance, and this test is discontinued when a set endpoint is met: i) DI is diagnosed or excluded ii) patient is unable to tolerate the test safely.
- Administration of DDAVP could help differentiate central DI from nephrogenic DI.

## Treatment of Diabetes Insipidus

- The treatment of central DI hinges on replacing fluids and ADH. The former is dependent on functional thirst mechanisms as well as the child's ability or inability (e.g. mental impairment, post-operative states, neonates and infants) to source for and consume fluids independently.
- Anti-diuresis can be achieved orally (PO Desmopressin), sublingually (MELT - orally disintegrating lyophilisate tablet), intranasally (IN Desmopressin) and where indicated, through injections either subcutaneously or intravenously (SC/IV Vasopressin).

## Bibliography

1. Refardt J. (2020) Diagnosis and differential diagnosis of diabetes insipidus: Update. *Best Pract Res Clin Endocrinol Metab* **34**(5):101398.

# CHAPTER 43

# Management of Fluids & Electrolytes

Chan Meng Fai, Joel; Leow Hui Min, Esther; Mok Yee Hui; Ng Yong Hong

# Management of Fluids

## 1. Maintenance fluid requirement

Full-term neonates:

Day 1 - 60 ml/kg/day
Day 2 - 90 ml/kg/day
Day 3 - 120 ml/kg/day
Day 4 - 150 ml/kg/day

*In a sick neonate (>Day 3 of life), consider restricting intravenous (IV) fluids to 100 to 120 ml/kg/day.

**IV fluid requirements should be capped at 100 ml/kg/day.

Child:

Table 12.1 Calculating Maintenance Fluids in Children

|  <10 kg | 100 ml/kg/d Or 4 ml/kg/hr  |
| --- | --- |
|  10–20 kg | 1000 ml + 50 ml/kg/d for each kg over 10 kg Or 40 ml/hr plus 2 ml/kg/hr for each kg over 10 kg  |
|  >20 kg | 1500 ml plus 20 ml/kg/d for each kg over 20 kg Or 60 ml/hr plus 1 ml/kg/hr for each kg over 20 kg  |

For example: the maintenance fluid requirement of a 35 kg child would be \((100 \times 10) + (50 \times 10) + (20 \times 15) = 1800 \mathrm{ml}\)

Special considerations

Higher fluid requirements: Children who are febrile, under phototherapy, or a radiant warmer, children with ongoing fluid losses (vomiting, diarrhoea, high stoma output, burns).
Lower fluid requirements: Stable post-operative children, children with renal or cardiac failure, children with Syndrome of Inappropriate Anti-Diuretic Hormone (SIADH).

Choice of IV fluids:

- The recommended maintenance fluids for the various age groups are shown in Table 12.2. The choice of fluids should also be based on the ongoing physiological disorder and prevalent electrolyte or glucose abnormality.

## 2. Fluid deficit in dehydrated children

Children with reduced intravascular fluid status should be assessed early and preferably with a senior doctor.
- Table 12.3 provides a guide to the assessment of the intravascular fluid status of a child. When in doubt, the more severe dehydration status should be used.
- The rate of replacement of fluid deficit depends on the primary pathology and its severity. For example, a slower replacement rate should be used for children with diabetic ketoacidosis and intracranial pathology, due to the risk of cerebral oedema.
- Oral rehydration therapy is discussed in Chapter 51: 'Acute Gastroenteritis'.

# The Baby Bear Book

Table 12.4 Calculating Fluid Deficit

Body weight x % dehydration = Deficit in Litres

E.g. 10 kg child, \(3\%\) dehydration: \(10 \times 0.03 = 0.3 \mathrm{~L} = 300 \mathrm{ml}\) deficit
Fluid used for rehydration is the same as maintenance fluids. Rehydration should be carried out evenly over at least 24-48 hr.
E.g. 20 kg child, 5% rehydration
Maintenance: 1500 ml/d, Deficit: 1000 ml
Therefore prescribe 2500 ml/d for first 24 hr, 1500 ml/d thereafter

## Management of Electrolytes

### 1. Introduction

Electrolyte abnormalities are frequently encountered in paediatric practice. When encountering problems with fluid and electrolyte imbalance, it is useful to think about what the expected renal response is to a given situation and evaluate the actual response. This is done by measuring serum and urine parameters:

- Serum: urea, creatinine, sodium (Na), potassium (K), chloride (Cl), calcium (Ca), magnesium (Mg), phosphate \((\mathrm{PO}_4)\), osmolality, pH, \(\mathsf{pCO}_2\)
- Urine: Na, K, Cl, osmolality, creatinine, pH

When considering the appropriate level of care that the patient requires (general ward, high-dependency, intensive care unit), particular attention should also be given to the requirement for:

Cardiac monitoring
Haemodynamic monitoring/intervention
- Neurological monitoring: Level of consciousness, seizures
Frequency of blood sampling and availability of venous access

### 2. Daily electrolyte requirement

Table 12.5 Daily Electrolyte Requirement

|  Sodium | 2–4 mmol/kg/d  |
| --- | --- |
|  Potassium | 1–2 mmol/kg/d  |
|  Chloride | 2–4 mmol/kg/d  |
|  Calcium | 0.5–1 mmol/kg/d  |
|  Magnesium | 0.2–0.4 mmol/kg/d  |
|  Phosphate | 2 mmol/kg/d  |

# CHAPTER 44

# Sodium Disorders

Chan Meng Fai, Joel; Leow Hui Min, Esther; Mok Yee Hui; Ng Yong Hong

## Introduction

Sodium (Na) abnormalities are more commonly the result of disruption in fluid homeostasis rather than sodium homeostasis. Assessment of the intravascular volume status and anti-diuretic hormone (ADH) activity (via comparison of serum and urine sodium and osmolality) provides a guide to the underlying pathology.

## Hypernatraemia

Definition: Serum sodium [Na] \(>150\mathrm{mmol / L}\)
Clinical presentation:

- Irritability, lethargy, hyper-reflexia, ataxia, seizures, coma

Assessment:

Refer to Figure 12.1 for a diagnostic approach.
History: Weight changes, intake and output (urinary, gastric, stool), medication history.
Examination: Assess the fluid status

Weight, skin changes (oedema, skin turgor), fontanelle changes, blood pressure, mucous membranes, cardiac gallop.
In hypernatraemic dehydration, the extracellular fluid volume may be preserved, the classic signs of dehydration (sunken eyes and fontanelle, reduced skin turgor, hypotension) may be absent.

Determine the urine volume.
Paired serum and urine osmolality and sodium, serum urea, creatinine and chloride.

Management

Water deficit \((\mathrm{L}) = 0.6\times\) Weight \((\mathrm{kg})\times\) ([Current Na]/140-1)
Aim: Decrease of [Na] by no more than \(0.5\mathrm{mmol / L / hour}\) (max. of 8-10 mmol/L/day) due to risk of cerebral oedema.
Hypervolaemia: Restriction and removal of excess Na with loop diuretics and hypotonic fluids.
Hypovolaemia: Give relatively hypo-osmolar fluid.
Treat underlying cause

- Vasopressin (DDAVP) for central diabetes insipidus (DI)

- Removal of exogenous Na

# Sodium Disorders

# Hyponatraemia

Definition: Serum Na <135 mmol/L

» Corrected [Na] levels = measured [Na] + 0.3 (glucose – 5.5) mmol/L

Clinical presentation

» Lethargy, weakness, altered sensorium, decreased level of consciousness, seizures, coma

Assessment

» History: changes in weight, intake and output (urinary, gastric, stool), medication history.

» Assess the hydration status.

Weight, skin changes (oedema, turgor), fontanelle changes, blood pressure, mucous membranes, apex beat, cardiac gallop.

» Determine the urine volume.

Investigations:

Paired serum and urine: Osmolality (osm), sodium and creatinine

Serum urea and chloride

» Calculated serum osmolality = (2 × [Na]) + [Glucose] + [Urea]

» Calculate the Fractional Excretion of Sodium, FeNa (%) = [(UNa/PNa) / (UCr/PCr)] × 100

(UNa - urine sodium, PNa - plasma sodium, UCr - urine creatinine, PCr - plasma creatinine)

Management

» Depends on

Chronicity of hyponatraemia

Severity of symptoms

Underlying cause (see Figure 12.2)

Hypovolaemia: Sodium and water replacement

Euvolaemia: Water restriction

Hypervolaemia: Sodium and water restriction, diuretics

» Na deficit (mmol) = 0.6 × Weight (kg) × [135 – (current Na)]

» Aim: Increase of [Na] by no more than 0.5 mmol/L/h (max. of 12 mmol/L/day) due to risk of central pontine myelinolysis (acute paralysis, dysphagia, dysarthria)

» If [Na] ≥120 mmol/L, correct by restriction of free water

» If [Na] <120 mmol/L or severely symptomatic (e.g. hyponatraemic seizures)

Correct to 125 mmol/L (can be 1–2 mmol/L/hour in the first few hours).

Monitor for the resolution of symptoms and rate of rise of [Na] and scale back the speed of correction accordingly.

» Drugs used

3% NaCl: 0.5 mmol of Na per ml

2 ml/kg of 3% NaCl increases Na by approximately 1 mmol/L

Dextrose 5%/0.45% NaCl or Dextrose 5%/0.9% NaCl: As fluid replacement or maintenance

# CHAPTER 45

# Potassium Disorders

Chan Meng Fai, Joel; Leow Hui Min, Esther; Mok Yee Hui; Ng Yong Hong

## Hyperkalaemia

Definition:

Serum potassium levels:

Neonate: \(\mathrm{K} > 6.0\mathrm{mmol / L}\)
Older child: \(\mathrm{K} > 5.5\mathrm{mmol / L}\)

- Exclude spurious results (e.g. blood taken from a vein proximal to a drip containing potassium) or haemolysed sample

Clinical presentation:

Arrhythmia (with palpitations, syncope)
Electrocardiogram (ECG) abnormalities (tall T-waves, depressed ST-segment, decreased R-wave amplitude, prolonged PR, small or absent P-waves, prolonged QRS, sine-wave, ventricular fibrillation). Hyperkalaemia with ECG abnormalities is a medical emergency. The ECG changes may not progress in a particular sequence and may not be reflective of the degree of hyperkalaemia.
Neuromuscular weakness (ascending from the legs to the arms and trunk) or paraesthesia, hyporeflexia

Causes:

Increased intake: Over-replacement via oral route, IV fluids (containing excessive potassium), massive transfusion
Transcellular movement of potassium: Increased cellular injury (rhabdomyolysis, tumour lysis, haemolysis), metabolic acidosis, insulin-deficiency states
Decreased renal excretion: decreased glomerular filtration rate (in kidney injury), hypoaldosteronism or impaired sensitivity to aldosterone, impaired renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (congenital adrenal hyperplasia, potassium-sparing diuretics, angiotensin-converting enzyme [ACE] inhibitors)

Management

Management guidelines are discussed in Table 12.7.
It is crucial to correct the underlying cause of the hyperkalaemia.

1. Salbutamol is a preferred first-line treatment for hyperkalaemia because of the ease of administration, while other treatments are being prepared.

2. Concentrated insulin is a high-alert medication and care should be taken in its dilution and preparation.

# The Baby Bear Book

# Hypokalaemia

Definition:Serum \(\mathrm{K} <   3.5\) mmol/L
- Clinical presentation: Arrhythmia, ECG abnormalities (prolonged PR, ST depression, T-inversion), neuromuscular weakness
Management:

» Correction of hypokalaemia is more crucial in children with cardiac disease or who are on digoxin
» Correct deficit on top of providing maintenance potassium. The required maintenance may be higher depending on the underlying pathology (e.g. severe dehydration in DKA, with the expected requirement for IV Insulin)
» Correcting hypokalaemia

K-Deficit (mmol): (Desired [K] - current [K]) \(\times 0.3 \times\) Weight (kg)
Non-urgent replacement:

- Oral K replacement is the preferred route (due to risk of cardiac arrhythmia with rapid IV administration)
- Increase maintenance K in the IV drip

Urgent replacement: Consider IV replacement in the following cases

Severe hypokalaemia \(\mathrm{K} <   2.5\mathrm{mmol / L}\)
- Symptomatic: Severe weakness, arrhythmia, post-cardiac surgery
- Unpredictable enteral absorption: Significant enterocolitis, postmajor surgery

Concentrated potassium chloride (KCl)

- Replacement of K deficit over 2 to 3 hours

o Peripheral line: Dilute the \(7.45\%\) KCl into \(0.9\%\) NaCl (maximal concentration of \(40~\mathrm{mmol / L}\))
Central line: Dilute the \(7.45\%\) KCl into \(0.9\%\) NaCl (maximal concentration of \(150~\mathrm{mmol / L}\))
Maximum rate of \(0.4\mathrm{mmol / kg / hour}\)

- Replacement should be done under cardiac monitoring and in high dependency, oncology wards or intensive care unit
- Recheck K level at the end of replacement
- IV \(7.45\%\) KCl should NEVER be given undiluted or as bolus

Potassium-containing preparations

Oral KCl: 1 ml contains 1.34 mmol of K
Oral Span K (600 mg): 8 mmol of K/tablet
IV \(7.45\%\) KCl: 1 ml contains 1 mmol of K and 1 mmol of Cl
\(\mathrm{KH}_2\mathrm{PO}_4\) : 1 ml contains 1 mmol of K and 1 mmol of \(\mathrm{PO_4}\)

# CHAPTER 46

# Acid–Base Disorders

Chan Meng Fai, Joel; Leow Hui Min, Esther; Mok Yee Hui; Ng Yong Hong

## Introduction

- A good *clinical evaluation* is essential to guide the workup of acid–base disorders.

History of underlying medical problems (e.g. diabetes, cardiac failure)
Symptomatology: e.g. vomiting, diarrhoea
Vital signs (e.g. shock, hypoxia)
Respiratory status: Rate and depth of breathing (e.g. Kussmaul's respiration)
Neurological status: Level of consciousness
Medication or drug use

- Blood gas: Determine the primary acid–base disorder

Ascertain if there is acidaemia \((\mathrm{pH} < 7.38)\) or alkalaemia \((\mathrm{pH} > 7.42)\).
Is this a primary respiratory or metabolic disorder?
Determine the appropriate physiological response to the primary disorder. Hence, evaluate if there is a secondary component to the acid-base disorder. Please refer to Figures 12.5 and 12.6.

- For high-anion gap metabolic acidosis, evaluate:

Osmolal gap = measured serum osmolality - calculated serum osmolality
Calculated serum osmolality \(= 2[\mathrm{Na}] + [\mathrm{glucose}] + [\mathrm{Urea}]\)
Osmolal gap of greater than \(10\mathrm{mmol / L}\) suggests the presence of toxic alcohols (ethylene glycol, methanol) or the presence of ketoacidosis or lactic acidosis.

## High Anion-gap Metabolic Acidosis (HAGMA)

Overproduction of acid

Ketoacidosis (diabetic ketoacidosis, starvation)
Lactic acidosis

Type A (hypoxic): Shock, carbon monoxide and cyanide poisoning
\(\odot\) Type B (non-hypoxic): Medications (propofol, metformin, isoniazid, salicylates), toxic alcohols (methanol, ethylene glycol)

- Impaired clearance of acid: Severe renal injury, liver failure (lactate clearance)
- Cell lysis: Severe rhabdomyolysis

## Normal Anion-gap Metabolic acidosis (NAGMA)

- Loss of bicarbonate

- Gastrointestinal (GI) losses: Diarrhoea, stoma losses, fistulae

# The Baby Bear Book

» Renal losses: Proximal RTA, medications (ifosfamide, topiramate, acetazolamide)

- Decreased renal acid excretion
  » Early uremic acidosis
  » Type 1 renal tubular acidosis (e.g. due to amphotericin, lithium, Sjögren's syndrome)
  » Type 4 renal tubular acidosis (hypoaldosteronism or pseudohypoaldosteronism)
- Other causes: Fluid resuscitation with saline

# Respiratory Acidosis

- Acute

» Normal alveolar-arterial (A-a) gradient: Depression of the respiratory centre by CNS disease (meningoencephalitis) or drugs
» High A-a gradient: Acute airway obstruction, acute lung disease (acute respiratory distress syndrome, pneumonia)

- Chronic

» Normal A-a gradient: Neuromuscular disease
» High A-a gradient: Chronic lung disease (from broncho-pulmonary dysplasia or bronchiectasis)

# Metabolic Alkalosis

- The kidney is highly efficient in excreting excess bicarbonate. Hence, metabolic alkalosis usually requires the presence of excess intake of alkali and the impairment of renal excretion of bicarbonate.
- Evaluating the cause

» Assess for excessive loss of nasogastric fluid, diuretic use or exogenous alkali intake.
» Should the history not provide a clue to the cause, assess the urinary chloride (Urine Cl).

  ◦ Chloride-responsive metabolic alkalosis: Replenishment of fluid volume, sodium chloride and potassium corrects the metabolic alkalosis.

  ◦ Chloride-resistant metabolic alkalosis: Due to excessive renal loss of sodium chloride.

# Respiratory Alkalosis

- Acute

» Normal A-a gradient: Pain, fever, hyperventilation (in anxiety), salicylate toxicity
» High A-a gradient: Pneumonia, pulmonary oedema, sepsis, aspiration, pulmonary embolism

- Chronic

» Normal A-a gradient: Hyperthyroidism
» High A-a gradient: Pulmonary embolism

- Treatment of the acid–base disorders will depend on the underlying cause

# CHAPTER 47

# Failure to Thrive

Charanya Rajan; Veena Logarajah

## Introduction

Failure to thrive (FTT) in childhood is a state of impaired growth due to inadequate caloric intake, inadequate caloric absorption/retention, or excessive caloric expenditure. In the developed world, it is seen in 5–10% of children. A careful history and physical examination can identify most causes of FTT, thereby avoiding protracted or costly evaluations.

## Definition

A child is said to have FTT in the presence of one of the following:

- Weight for age that falls below the 5th percentile on multiple occasions
- Weight deceleration that crosses two major percentile lines on a growth chart

Use of any single indicator has a low positive predictive value, hence the below anthropometric criteria are also helpful to diagnose FTT:

Length \(< 5\)th percentile
Weight \(< 75\%\) of median weight for length
Weight velocity \(< 5\)th percentile
- Body mass index \(< 5\)th percentile

NOTE: Criteria should be met on multiple occasions.

## Causes

Most cases of FTT involve inadequate caloric intake caused by behavioural or psychosocial issues. Routine laboratory testing identifies an organic cause of FTT in <1% of children and is not generally recommended. Child neglect or abuse must be considered in all age groups, because children with FTT are four times more likely to be abused than children without FTT.

A practical way to categorise FTT is according to calories (see Table 13.1):

- Inadequate caloric intake
- Inadequate caloric absorption/retention
- Excessive caloric expenditure
Unclassified (unrelated to calories)

# Failure to Thrive

Table 13.1 Differential Diagnosis of Failure to Thrive

|  Inadequate Caloric Intake | Inadequate Caloric Absorption/ Retention | Excessive Caloric Expenditure | Unclassified  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Infant or toddler  |   |   |   |
|  Feeding difficulties | Food allergy | Thyroid disease | SGA  |
|  Improper food/formula preparation | GER | Chronic infections | Neglect/abuse  |
|  Caregiver depression | Pyloric stenosis | Immunodeficiency | Skeletal dysplasia  |
|  Behavioural/ neurological issues | Malabsorption, e.g. Coeliac, CF | Chronic pulmonary disease | Hypopituituarism  |
|  Poverty/lack of food availability/cleft lip or palate/airway issues | Inborn error of metabolism | Congenital heart disease | Genetic Russell-Silver, Turner Syndrome  |
|   |  | Chronic liver disease |   |
|   |  | Malignancy |   |
|  Child or adolescent  |   |   |   |
|  Mood disorder | Food allergy | Thyroid disease | Constitutional  |
|  Eating disorder | Inflammatory bowel disease | Chronic infection or immunodeficiency | Delayed puberty  |
|  Drug abuse | Malabsorption, e.g. Coeliac, CF | Chronic pulmonary disease |   |
|   | Inborn error of metabolism | Heart disease or failure |   |
|   |  | Malignancy |   |
|   |  | Renal failure, renal tubular acidosis |   |

CF and coeliac disease is extremely rare in the Chinese race.

## Approach

### History

The most important part of the outpatient evaluation of FTT is obtaining an accurate account of:

Age of onset and growth pattern
- Feeding history
- Dietary intake
- Parent-child interactions
- Review of systems to elicit symptoms of organic causes

b4854_Sec-13.indd 339

12-Dec-22 11:02:49 AM

# The Baby Bear Book

In exclusively breast-fed infants, help of a lactation specialist nurse should be sought to ensure proper technique, latch-on, and swallow. In rare cases where intake is uncertain, consider asking breastfeeding mothers to express breastmilk and measure the amount of consumed breast milk. Alternatively, obtaining the weight of an undressed breastfed infant before and after feeding may be considered. For formula-fed infants, apart from the volume of feeds consumed, the correct feed preparation technique should also be checked.

Observing a toddler's eating habits can be helpful in evaluating for picky eating or food refusal. Asking older children and adolescents, together with their parents, to maintain a food journal for 3 days can give the physician a way to measure caloric intake. Particular attention should also be given to chart "loss of calories" via regurgitation and vomiting.

Taking a psychosocial history is essential for detecting parental or patient depression or identifying concerns about the caregiver's intellectual abilities or social circumstances.

Some children who fail to meet normal growth parameters actually demonstrate a normal variant of growth, such as children of small parents who are growing to their full genetic potential but along a lower percentile curve, large-for-gestational-age infants who regress toward the mean ("catch-down growth"), children with constitutional delay in growth or premature infants whose growth parameters are normal when corrected for gestational age.

A review of systems that elicits recurrent infections, respiratory symptoms, developmental delay, vomiting, or diarrhoea may point to an organic cause. In a study on children without obvious organic symptoms elicited on history, 92% were ultimately diagnosed with a behavioural cause of FTT.

## Clinical Examination

The first consideration in examining a child with FTT is ensuring accurate measurements. Height (or length), weight, and head circumference should be plotted on an appropriate growth chart over time.

Although most children with FTT will have a normal examination, physicians should be alert for signs of physical abuse or neglect. Physicians should also seek red flag signs or symptoms of medical conditions that might be causing FTT (See Table 13.2).

Table 13.2 Red Flags Signs and Symptoms Suggesting Medical Causes of Failure to Thrive

|  • Cardiac findings suggesting congenital heart disease (e.g. clubbing, cyanosis, murmur)  |
| --- |
|  • Developmental delay  |
|  • Dysmorphic features  |
|  • Recurrent or severe respiratory, mucocutaneous, or urinary infection  |
|  • Failure to gain weight despite adequate caloric intake  |
|  • Organomegaly or lymphadenopathy  |
|  • Recurrent vomiting, diarrhoea, or dehydration  |

## Investigations

Routine laboratory testing rarely identifies a cause of FTT and is not generally recommended. However, history or physical examination findings sometimes suggest the need for further testing.

b4854_Sec-13.indd 340

12-Dec-22 11:02:49 AM

# Failure to Thrive

Baseline tests: Consider full blood count, renal panel, liver function test, thyroid function, C-reactive protein, erythrocyte sedimentation rate, urinalysis, urine culture.

If indicated by history, physical examination or initial laboratory testing, consider the following:

- Respiratory — Chest X-ray, sweat test
- Cardiovascular — Echocardiography
Gastrointestinal Tract — Stool for culture, ova, parasites, analysis for fat globules, elastase and reducing substances, celiac screen
Endocrine Bone age, Synacthen test, and growth hormone testing (under specialist supervision)
- Infection and immunology — Tests for human immunodeficiency virus, TORCHES, tuberculosis, complements, immunoglobulins, and allergy
- Genetic and chromosomal — Turner syndrome, etc.

In rare cases, hospitalisation for observed feeding and further investigation may be helpful. Hospitalisation should be considered for the following:

- If the child does not improve with outpatient management
- Suspicion of abuse or neglect exists
Signs of traumatic injury are present
- Severe psychosocial impairment/stress of the caregiver is evident
There are signs of serious malnutrition

## Management

If a disease or medical condition is identified on history, physical examination, or additional testing, the correct treatment will vary depending on the condition.

If a diagnosis of FTT is made and no medical conditions are suggested on evaluation, appropriate guidance for catch-up growth should be made and age-appropriate nutritional counselling should be provided to parents. Consider early referral to dietitian for suggestion of suitable forms, amount, and frequency of nutrition to allow for meeting daily nutritional requirements and allowing for catch-up growth.

Breastfed infants: Recommend feeding more often. Ensure lactation support. Consider expressed breastmilk to quantify feed volume accurately. **Discuss concentrating or supplementing feeds with milk formula until catch-up growth is achieved.**

Formula-fed infants: Clear instructions on how to make energy-dense formula by concentrating the ratio of formula to water may be helpful.

Toddlers should avoid excessive juice or milk consumption as this interferes with nutrition. Nutritional supplements may be given until catch-up growth is achieved.

Parents may also be instructed to provide calorie-dense foods by adding rice cereal to foods for toddlers, cream sauces, or butter to foods for older children or adolescents.

For children with swallowing difficulties or underlying neurological problems, consider speech therapist assessment of swallowing function. Alternative methods of feeding, e.g. orogastric tube feeding, may be required temporarily in severely malnourished children who are unable to or refuse to take adequate oral feeds.

In those who are severely malnourished, it is important to watch for refeeding syndrome while feeds are being established. Consider monitoring electrolytes (especially

b4854_Sec-13.indd 341

12-Dec-22 11:02:49 AM

serum potassium, magnesium, phosphate) closely for at least 3–5 days and address any electrolyte abnormality promptly.

Close and serial follow-up including evaluation of height (or length) and weight should be performed in the physician's office after intervention to ensure appropriate catch-up growth. Multidisciplinary interventions, including home medical social worker visits, dietetic advice, and psychology inputs should be considered to optimise weight gain, parent–child relationships, and cognitive development. Finally, although medications such as cyproheptadine have been shown to help promote weight gain in children with cancer-related cachexia, they have not been studied in other causes of FTT.

## Prognosis and Outcomes

There is consensus that severe, prolonged malnutrition, which is common in developing countries, can negatively affect a child's future growth and cognitive development. Low-birth-weight infants who develop FTT have also demonstrated long-term developmental effects. However, it is unclear from current studies if normal-birth-weight infants who develop FTT and then recover have similar long-term consequences. A systematic review showed that FTT during the first 2 years of life was not associated with a significant reduction in IQ, although some long-term reductions in weight and height were present. Nevertheless, it important to always review the nutritional status of our patients and attempt to optimise them at every encounter.

## Bibliography

1. Levy Y, Levy A, Zangen T, et al. (2009) Diagnostic clues for identification of nonorganic vs organic causes of food refusal and poor feeding. J Pediatr Gastroenterol Nutr 48(3):355–362.
2. Cole SZ, Lanham JS. (2011) Failure to thrive: An update. Am Fam Physician 83(7):829–834.
3. Kliegman RM, Stanton BF, St. Geme JW, et al. Nelson Textbook of Pediatrics, 19th ed., Chap. 38, pp. 147–149.
4. Panetta F, Magazzu D, Sferlazzas C, et al. (2008) Diagnosis on a positive fashion of nonorganic failure to thrive. Acta Paediatr 97(9):1281–1284.
5. Black MM, Dubowitz H, Krishnakumar A, Starr RH Jr. (2007) Early intervention and recovery among children with failure to thrive: Follow-up at age 8. Pediatrics 120(1):59–69.

# CHAPTER 48

# Recurrent Abdominal Pain

Charanya Rajan; Veena Logarajah

## Introduction

Approximately 10–45% of school-aged children experience recurrent abdominal pain (RAP). In most cases, the cause of the symptoms is not attributed to a particular medical condition. Children with RAP are diagnosed with functional abdominal pain disorders (FAPD) when there are no organic causes to explain their symptoms. Only 3–8% of children have an underlying pathology, presenting with RAP associated with alarm features as listed below. A complete history, including a detailed pain history, effect on the daily activities of the child and family, possible psychosocial factors and examination, including growth and pubertal status, are the most important components in evaluating the cause of the abdominal pain.

## Definition

Abdominal pain occurring at least four times per month over a period of at least 2 months and severe enough to affect the child's ability to perform daily activities.

## Alarm Features Suggestive of Organic Cause

- Family history of inflammatory bowel disease, coeliac disease, peptic ulcer disease
- Persistent right upper or lower quadrant pain
Dysphagia
Odynophagia
Persistent vomiting
Gastrointestinal blood loss
- Nocturnal diarrhoea or abdominal pain
- Constitutional symptoms, e.g. arthritis, oral ulcers, unexplained fever
- Perirectal disease
- Involuntary weight loss
- Deceleration of linear growth
- Delayed puberty

b4854_Sec-13.indd 343
